\nu
in
\eta
-normal form,
a(\kappa\overset{\beta^{n}}{\longrightarrow}\phi)\leq a(\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\phi)
and
n<n+1
.
We obtain an
\eta
-normal proof
\mu
with
\nu\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\mu\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\eta^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\phi
, solving the second case.

We can now prove the main result of this section,
8
.

Proof 3.4

(Proof of
8
)
.

We reason by induction on the length of the sequence
\pi\mathrel{=_{\beta\eta}}\tau
.
If it is of null length, then
\pi=\tau
hence
\eta(\pi)=\eta(\tau)
.
Otherwise, there is a proof
\phi
such that
\pi\mathrel{\text{\textemdash}}\phi\mathrel{=_{\beta\eta}}\tau
with
\mathrel{\text{\textemdash}}~\in\{\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}};\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longleftarrow}};\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\eta$}}}{\longrightarrow}};\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\eta$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\}
.
By induction hypothesis,
\eta(\phi)\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\eta(\tau)
, with only axiom-expanded proofs in this sequence.
We distinguish cases according to
\pi\mathrel{\text{\textemdash}}\phi
.

If
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\phi
, then as
\eta(\pi)\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\eta^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi
we can apply
11
to obtain an
\eta
-normal proof
\mu
such that
\eta(\pi)\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\mu\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\eta^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\phi
.
Thus,
\mu=\eta(\phi)
, so
\eta(\pi)\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\eta(\phi)\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\eta(\tau)
and the result holds.

Similarly, if
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\phi
then, as
\phi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\eta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\eta(\phi)
, there exists an
\eta
-normal proof
\mu
such that
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\eta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\mu\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\eta(\phi)
(
11
).
Thus
\mu=\eta(\pi)
, and
\eta(\pi)\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\eta(\phi)\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\eta(\tau)
.

Finally, if
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\eta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\phi
or
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\eta$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\phi
, then
\eta(\pi)=\eta(\phi)\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\eta(\tau)
and the conclusion follows.

4.
Cut-elimination and rule commutations

It is not possible to apply the reasoning from the previous section (about axiom-expansion) to cut-elimination, because cut-elimination is confluent only up to rule commutations.
This is what we want to prove in this section, reducing the problem from looking at cut-elimination to considering rule commutations.

A first, easy but important, result is that rule commutation is included in equality up to cut-elimination.

Proposition 12

(
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}~\subseteq~\mathrel{=_{\beta}}
)
.

Given proofs
\pi
and
\tau
, if
\pi\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\tau
then
\pi\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\tau
.

Proof 4.1
.

It suffices, for each commutation, to give a proof that can be reduced by cut elimination to both sides.
We give here only a few representative cases.

•

C_{\with}^{\top}
and
C_{\top}^{\with}
commutations.
The proof

\top

\vdash

0,\top

\top

\vdash

A_{1},\top,\Gamma

\top

\vdash

A_{2},\top,\Gamma

\with

\vdash

A_{1}\with A_{2},\top,\Gamma

cut

\vdash

A_{1}\with A_{2},\top,\Gamma

reduces to both

\top

\vdash

A_{1}\with A_{2},\top,\Gamma

and

\top

\vdash

A_{1},\top,\Gamma

\top

\vdash

A_{2},\top,\Gamma

\with

\vdash

A_{1}\with A_{2},\top,\Gamma

according respectively to whether the
cut
-rule is first commuted with the
\top
-rule on its left or with the
\with
-rule on its right and then with the
\top
-rule on its left in both occurrences.

•

C_{\oplus_{i}}^{\bot}
and
C_{\bot}^{\oplus_{i}}
commutations.
The proof

\pi

\vdash

A_{i},\Gamma

\bot

\vdash

A_{i},\bot,\Gamma

ax

\vdash

A_{i}
{}^{\perp},
A_{i}

\oplus_{i}

\vdash

A_{i}^{\perp},A_{1}\oplus A_{2}

cut

\vdash

A_{1}\oplus A_{2},\bot,\Gamma

reduces to both

\pi

\vdash

A_{i},\Gamma

\oplus_{i}

\vdash

A_{1}\oplus A_{2},\Gamma

\bot

\vdash

A_{1}\oplus A_{2},\bot,\Gamma

and

\pi

\vdash

A_{i},\Gamma

\bot

\vdash

A_{i},\bot,\Gamma

\oplus_{i}

\vdash

A_{1}\oplus A_{2},\bot,\Gamma

according respectively to whether the
cut
-rule is first commuted with the
\bot
-rule on its left or with the
\oplus_{i}
-rule on its right.

•

C_{\otimes}^{\otimes}
commutations.
The proof

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},\Gamma

ax

\vdash

A_{2}
{}^{\perp},
A_{2}

\otimes

\vdash

A_{2}^{\perp},A_{1}\otimes A_{2},\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{2},B_{1},\Delta

\pi_{3}

\vdash

B_{2},\Sigma

\otimes

\vdash

B_{1}\otimes B_{2},\Delta,\Sigma,A_{2}

cut

\vdash

A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1}\otimes B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma

reduces to both

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{2},B_{1},\Delta

\pi_{3}

\vdash

B_{2},\Sigma

\otimes

\vdash

A_{2},B_{1}\otimes B_{2},\Delta,\Sigma

\otimes

\vdash

A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1}\otimes B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma

and

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{2},B_{1},\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1},\Gamma,\Delta

\pi_{3}

\vdash

B_{2},\Sigma

\otimes

\vdash

A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1}\otimes B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma

according respectively to whether the
cut
-rule is first commuted with the
\otimes
-rule on its left or on its right.

Other
C_{\otimes}^{\otimes}
commutations are similar.

•

C_{\invamp}^{\with}
and
C_{\with}^{\invamp}
commutations.
The proof

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},A_{2},B_{1},\Gamma

\invamp

\vdash

A_{1}\invamp A_{2},B_{1},\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{1},A_{2},B_{2},\Gamma

\invamp

\vdash

A_{1}\invamp A_{2},B_{2},\Gamma

\with

\vdash

A_{1}\invamp A_{2},B_{1}\with B_{2},\Gamma

ax

\vdash

A_{2}
{}^{\perp},
A_{2}

ax

\vdash

A_{1}
{}^{\perp},
A_{1}

\otimes

\vdash

A_{1},A_{2},A_{2}^{\perp}\otimes A_{1}^{\perp}

\invamp

\vdash

A_{1}\invamp A_{2},A_{2}^{\perp}\otimes A_{1}^{\perp}

cut

\vdash

A_{1}\invamp A_{2},B_{1}\with B_{2},\Gamma

reduces to both

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},A_{2},B_{1},\Gamma

\invamp

\vdash

A_{1}\invamp A_{2},B_{1},\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{1},A_{2},B_{2},\Gamma

\invamp

\vdash

A_{1}\invamp A_{2},B_{2},\Gamma

\with

\vdash

A_{1}\invamp A_{2},B_{1}\with B_{2},\Gamma

and

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},A_{2},B_{1},\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{1},A_{2},B_{2},\Gamma

\with

\vdash

A_{1},A_{2},B_{1}\with B_{2},\Gamma

\invamp

\vdash

A_{1}\invamp A_{2},B_{1}\with B_{2},\Gamma

according respectively to whether the
cut
-rule is first commuted with the
\with
-rule on its left or with the
\invamp
-rule on its right.

Cut-elimination
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
is weakly normalizing (see
89
).
Furthermore, rule commutation is its “core”, in the following meaning.

Theorem 13

(Rule commutation is the core of cut-elimination)
.

If two axiom-expanded proofs
\pi
and
\tau
are
\beta
-equal, then any of their normal forms by
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
are related by
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
.

This result is not surprising but has not already been proved as far as we know for it is rather tedious to establish.
It is an important general result about sequent calculus, which we are convinced should hold for full linear logic.
We can extend it to
\beta\eta
-equality.

Theorem 14
.

Let
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
be
\beta\eta
-equal proofs.
Then, letting
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
(resp.
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
) be a result of expanding all axioms and then eliminating all cuts in
\pi_{1}
(resp.
\pi_{2}
),
\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}^{\prime}
.

Proof 4.2
.

We have
\pi_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta\eta}}\pi_{2}
, so
\eta(\pi_{1})\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\eta(\pi_{2})
by
8
.
By
13
, it follows
\eta(\pi_{1})\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\eta(\pi_{2})
.

The proof of
13
involves a result from rewriting theory modulo an equivalence relation, as well as plenty of case studies and intermediate results.
Whence, for the sake of clarity, it is written in its own part in
Appendix A
.

5.
Reduction to the unit-free distributed fragment

As written in the introduction, our plan is to use proof-nets, which currently exist only for axiom-expanded proofs in unit-free MALL.
For we already reduced the problem to axiom-expanded proofs in
Section 3
, it stays to take care of the units.
The main idea is that the multiplicative and additive units can be replaced by fresh atoms for the study of isomorphisms.
However, this is not true in general, for instance we have
(1\oplus A)\otimes B\mathrel{\simeq}B\oplus(A\otimes B)
(using the soundness theorem,
5
), and this isomorphism uses that
1
is unital for
\otimes
.
Hence, we begin by reducing the problem to so-called
distributed
formulas, using the distributivity, unitality and cancellation equations of
Table 1
on
\autopageref
tab:eqisos (
Section 5.1
).
Then, for theses special formulas, we identify patterns containing units in proofs equal to an identity up to rule commutations, patterns which can be lifted to proofs of isomorphisms (
Section 5.2
).
Finally, we prove that, in an isomorphism between distributed formulas, replacing units by fresh atoms preserves being an isomorphism, reducing the study of isomorphisms to distributed formulas in the unit-free fragment (
Section 5.3
).

5.1.
Reduction to distributed formulas

{defi}

[Distributed formula]

A formula is
distributed
if it does not have any sub-formula of the form
A\otimes(B\oplus C)
,
(A\oplus B)\otimes C
,
A\otimes 1
,
1\otimes A
,
A\oplus 0
,
0\oplus A
,
A\otimes 0
,
0\otimes A
or their duals
(C\with B)\invamp A
,
C\invamp(B\with A)
,
\bot\invamp A
,
A\invamp\bot
,
\top\with A
,
A\with\top
,
\top\invamp A
,
A\invamp\top
(where
A
,
B
and
C
are any formulas).

Remark 15
.

This notion is stable under duality: if
A
is distributed, so is
A^{\perp}
.

Proposition 16
.

If the equational theory denoted
\mathcal{L}
(see
Table 1
on
\autopageref
tab:eqisos) is complete for isomorphisms between distributed formulas, then it is complete for isomorphisms between arbitrary formulas.

Proof 5.1
.

The following rewriting system is strongly normalizing (but not confluent), with as normal forms distributed formulas, and each rule corresponds to a valid equality in the theory
\mathcal{L}
:

A\otimes(B\oplus C)

\rightarrow

(A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes C)

(C\with B)\invamp A

\rightarrow

(C\invamp A)\with(B\invamp A)

(A\oplus B)\otimes C

\rightarrow

(A\otimes C)\oplus(B\otimes C)

C\invamp(B\with A)

\rightarrow

(C\invamp B)\with(C\invamp A)

A\otimes 1

\rightarrow

A

1\otimes A

\rightarrow

A

A\invamp\bot

\rightarrow

A

\bot\invamp A

\rightarrow

A

A\oplus 0

\rightarrow

A

0\oplus A

\rightarrow

A

A\with\top

\rightarrow

A

\top\with A

\rightarrow

A

A\otimes 0

\rightarrow

0

0\otimes A

\rightarrow

0

A\invamp\top

\rightarrow

\top

\top\invamp A

\rightarrow

\top

Consider an isomorphism
A\mathrel{\simeq}B
between two arbitrary formulas
A
and
B
.
Let
A_{d}
and
B_{d}
be associated distributed formulas, obtained as normal forms of the above rewriting system.
As this rewriting system is included in
\mathcal{L}
, we have
A=_{\mathcal{L}}A_{d}
and
B=_{\mathcal{L}}B_{d}
.

By soundness of
\mathcal{L}
(
5
) and as linear isomorphism is a congruence, we deduce
A_{d}\mathrel{\simeq}A\mathrel{\simeq}B\mathrel{\simeq}B_{d}
.
The completeness hypothesis on
\mathcal{L}
for distributed formulas yields
A_{d}=_{\mathcal{L}}B_{d}
from
A_{d}\mathrel{\simeq}B_{d}
.
Thus
A=_{\mathcal{L}}A_{d}=_{\mathcal{L}}B_{d}=_{\mathcal{L}}B
.

Therefore, we can now consider only distributed formulas.
We will use this to study units, as well as when solving the unit-free case to prove there are only commutativity and associativity isomorphisms left.

5.2.
Patterns in distributed isomorphisms

We analyze the behaviour of units in proofs equal to
\textnormal{id}_{A}
up to rule commutations (recall
\textnormal{id}_{A}
is the axiom-expansion of
\textnormal{ax}_{A}
, the proof composed of an
ax
-rule on
A
).
We only do so for a
distributed
formula
A
as we have already seen it is enough in
Section 5.1
, and as the patterns we find hold only for these formulas.

Proposition 17
.

Let
\pi
be a proof equal, up to rule commutations, to
\textnormal{id}_{A}
with
A

distributed
.
Then:

•

the
\top
-rules of
\pi
are of the shape

\top

\vdash

\top,0

(with
\top
in
A
being the dual of
0
in
A^{\perp}
, or vice-versa);

•

\bot
-rules and
1
-rules come by pairs separated with
\oplus_{i}
-rules only, called a
1/\oplus/\bot
-pattern
:

1

\vdash 1

\rho

\vdash F

\bot

\vdash

\bot,F

where
\rho
is a sequence of
\oplus_{i}
-rules (with
\bot
in
A
being the dual of
1
in
A^{\perp}
, or vice-versa);

•

there is no sequent in
\pi
of the shape
\vdash B\with C
.

Proof 5.2
.

Given a formula
B
, we define the grammar
\resizebox{}{458752}{$\bigoplus$}[B]\coloneqq B\mid\resizebox{}{458752}{$\bigoplus$}[B]\oplus D\mid D\oplus\resizebox{}{458752}{$\bigoplus$}[B]
where
D
ranges over all formulas.
The key idea is to find properties of
\textnormal{id}_{A}
preserved by all rule commutations and ensuring the properties described in the statement.
Hence, we prove a stronger property: any sequent
S
of a proof
\pi
obtained through a sequence of rule commutations from
\textnormal{id}_{A}
for a distributed formula
A
respects:

(1)

the formulas of
S
are distributed;

(2)

if
\top
is a formula of
S
, then
S=~\vdash\top,0
, with
0
in
A^{\perp}
the dual of
\top
if
\top
is a sub-formula of
A
(or vice-versa);

(3)

if
\bot
is a formula of
S
, then
S=~\vdash\bot,\resizebox{}{458752}{$\bigoplus$}[1]
with
\resizebox{}{458752}{$\bigoplus$}[1]
the grammar defined above, where the distinguished
1
is the dual of
\bot
in
A^{\perp}
if
\bot
is a sub-formula of
A
(or vice-versa), and the sub-proof of
\pi
above
S
is a sequence of
\oplus_{i}
rules leading to the distinguished
1
, with in addition a
\bot
-rule inside this sequence;

(4)

if
B\with C
is a formula of
S
, then
S=~\vdash B\with C,\resizebox{}{458752}{$\bigoplus$}[C^{\perp}\oplus B^{\perp}]
with
\resizebox{}{458752}{$\bigoplus$}[C^{\perp}\oplus B^{\perp}]
the grammar defined above, where the distinguished
C^{\perp}\oplus B^{\perp}
is the dual of
B\with C
in
A^{\perp}
if
B\with C
is a sub-formula of
A
(or vice-versa), and in the sub-proof of
\pi
above
S
the
\oplus
-rules of the distinguished
C^{\perp}\oplus B^{\perp}
are a
\oplus_{2}
-rule in the left branch of the
\with
-rule of
B\with C
, and a
\oplus_{1}
-rule in its right branch;

(5)

if
S
contains several negative formulas or several positive formulas, then its negative formulas are all
\invamp
-formulas or negated atoms.

Remark that
5
is a corollary of properties
2
,
3
and
4
.
As in
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
there is no commutation with a
cut
-rule (in particular no
cut-\top
commutation) and no
\otimes-\top
commutation creating a sub-proof with a
cut
-rule, it follows that
\pi
is cut-free and has the sub-formula property, making
1
trivially true.
We prove that the fully expanded axiom respects properties
2
,
3
and
4
, and that these properties are preserved by any rule commutation of
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
.

The fully expanded axiom respects the properties.
We prove it by induction on the distributed formula
A
.
Notice that sub-formulas of
A
are also distributed.
By symmetry, assume
A
is positive.

If
A\in\{X,1,0\}
where
X
is an atom, then:

\textnormal{id}_{A}\in\left\{\text{
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces
\ignorespaces\lower 9.34909pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 18.17775pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to44.3555pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$ax$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash$ $X$${}^{\perp},$$X$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}
\ignorespaces};\text{
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces
\ignorespaces
\ignorespaces
\ignorespaces\lower 19.3093pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 6.51112pt\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 6.94441pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-0.72035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to21.88884pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.72035pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$1$}}\vskip-0.72035pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash 1$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\vskip-0.97035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to34.91109pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.97035pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$\bot$}}\vskip-0.97035pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash$ $\bot,1$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}
\ignorespaces};\text{
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces
\ignorespaces\lower 8.02965pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 13.45554pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-0.97035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to34.91109pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.97035pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$\top$}}\vskip-0.97035pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash$ $\top,0$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}
\ignorespaces}\right\}

Each of these proofs respects
2
,
3
and
4
.

Assume the result holds for
B
and
C
, and that
A=B\otimes C
.
The proof
\textnormal{id}_{A}
is:

\textnormal{id}_{B}

\vdash

B^{\perp},B

\textnormal{id}_{C}

\vdash

C^{\perp},C

\otimes

\vdash

C^{\perp},B^{\perp},B\otimes C

\invamp

\vdash

C^{\perp}\invamp B^{\perp},B\otimes C

We have to prove the sequents
\vdash C^{\perp},B^{\perp},B\otimes C
and
\vdash C^{\perp}\invamp B^{\perp},B\otimes C
respect the properties.
The latter respects
2
,
3
and
4
trivially for it has neither a
\top
,
\bot
nor
\with
formula.
As
C^{\perp}\invamp B^{\perp}
is distributed, it follows that neither
C^{\perp}
nor
B^{\perp}
can be a
\top
,
\bot
or
\with
formula, and as such the former sequent also respects the properties.

Suppose
A=B\oplus C
with sequents of
B
and
C
respecting the properties.
Now,
\textnormal{id}_{A}
is:

\textnormal{id}_{C}

\vdash

C^{\perp},C

\oplus_{2}

\vdash

C^{\perp},B\oplus C

\textnormal{id}_{B}

\vdash

B^{\perp},B

\oplus_{1}

\vdash

B^{\perp},B\oplus C

\with

\vdash

C^{\perp}\with B^{\perp},B\oplus C

The sequent
\vdash C^{\perp}\with B^{\perp},B\oplus C
respects
2
,
3
and
4
, as the
\oplus
is the dual of the
\with
.
By symmetry, we show the properties are also fulfilled by
\vdash B^{\perp},B\oplus C
, and they will be respected by
\vdash C^{\perp},B\oplus C
with a similar proof.
As the formulas are distributed,
B^{\perp}
cannot be a
\top
formula, hence the sequent respects
2
.
If
B^{\perp}
is not a
\bot
nor
\with
formula, then
3
and
4
hold for
\vdash B^{\perp},B\oplus C
.
If it is, then using that
\vdash B^{\perp},B
respects
3
and
4
, it follows that
B\oplus C
is also of the required shape, for
B
was.

Every possible rule commutation preserves the properties.
We show it for each rule commutation, using every time the notations from Tables
5
and
6
in
Section 2.2
, on Pages
5
and
6
.
By symmetry, we treat only one case for
\otimes-\otimes
,
\invamp-\otimes
,
\with-\otimes
and
\oplus_{i}-\otimes
commutations.

\top
-commutations:

Using property
2
, we cannot do any commutation between a
\top
-rule and a
\invamp
,
\otimes
,
\with
,
\oplus_{i}
,
\bot
or
\top
-rule, so no commutations at all involving a
\top
-rule.

\bot
-commutations:

Using property
3
, we cannot do any commutation between a
\bot
-rule and a
\invamp
,
\otimes
,
\with
or
\bot
-rule.
A commutation between a
\bot
and a
\oplus_{i}
-rule preserves property
3
: we have by hypothesis
\Gamma
empty and
A_{1}\oplus A_{2}
of the right shape.
It also respects
2
and
4
trivially.

C_{\invamp}^{\invamp}
commutation:

We have to show the properties for
\vdash A_{1},A_{2},B_{1}\invamp B_{2},\Gamma
.
Because
\vdash A_{1}\invamp A_{2},B_{1}\invamp B_{2},\Gamma
respects them, negative formulas of
\Gamma
are
\invamp
-formulas or negated atoms by
5
.
By distributivity, if
A_{1}
(or
A_{2}
) is a negative formula, then it must be a
\invamp
one or a negated atom.
Thus,
\vdash A_{1},A_{2},B_{1}\invamp B_{2},\Gamma
fulfills
2
,
3
and
4
.

C_{\oplus_{j}}^{\oplus_{i}}
commutation:

We show the properties for
\vdash A_{i},B_{1}\oplus B_{2},\Gamma
.
As
\vdash A_{1}\oplus A_{2},B_{1}\oplus B_{2},\Gamma
respects them, negative formulas of
\Gamma
are
\invamp
-formulas or negated atoms by
5
.
If
A_{i}
is positive, a
\invamp
or a negated atom, then we are done.
Otherwise, as
\vdash A_{i},B_{j},\Gamma
fulfills the properties, it follows
\Gamma
is empty and
B_{j}
of the desired shape.
By
1
,
B_{j}
is not
0
, thus
A_{i}
is not
\top
.
Whether
A_{i}
is
\bot
or
\with
, the sequent
\vdash A_{i},B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
respects the properties.

C_{\otimes}^{\otimes}
commutation:

We have to show the properties for
\vdash A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1},\Gamma,\Delta
.
Because
{\vdash A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1}\otimes B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma}
respects them, negative formulas of
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are
\invamp
-formulas or negated atoms by
5
.
If
B_{1}
is positive, a
\invamp
or a negated atom, then we are done.
Otherwise, as
\vdash A_{2},B_{1},\Delta
fulfills the properties, it follows
\Delta
is empty and
B_{1}
of the desired shape, so
B_{1}
is a
0
,
1
or
\oplus
-formula.
This is impossible as
B_{1}\otimes B_{2}
is distributed by
1
.

C_{\with}^{\with}
,
C_{\invamp}^{\with}
,
C_{\with}^{\invamp}
,
C_{\otimes}^{\with}
and
C_{\with}^{\otimes}
commutations:

These cases are impossible by property
4
.

C_{\oplus_{i}}^{\with}
and
C_{\with}^{\oplus_{i}}
commutations:

In these cases,
4
for
\vdash A_{1}\with A_{2},B_{1}\oplus B_{2},\Gamma
implies
\Gamma
empty and
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
of the desired shape.
Thus
B_{i}
of the desired shape (
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
is not the distinguished formula as it has the same rule
\oplus_{i}
in both branches of the
\with
-rule), proving the result for
\vdash A_{1}\with A_{2},B_{i}
.
For
\vdash A_{1},B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
(and similarly
\vdash A_{2},B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
),
A_{1}
cannot be a
\top
by
1
, and if it is a
\bot
or a
\with
, then the hypothesis on
\vdash A_{1},B_{i}
implies that the properties are also respected in
\vdash A_{1},B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
.

C_{\oplus_{i}}^{\invamp}
and
C_{\invamp}^{\oplus_{i}}
commutations:

Let us show the properties for
\vdash A_{1},A_{2},B_{1}\oplus B_{2},\Gamma
in the first commutation and
\vdash A_{1}\invamp A_{2},B_{i},\Gamma
in the second.
As they hold for
\vdash A_{1},A_{2},B_{i},\Gamma
, negative formulas in
A_{1},A_{2},B_{i},\Gamma
are
\invamp
-formulas or negated atoms by
5
and the result follows.

C_{\oplus_{i}}^{\otimes}
commutation:

We have to prove
\vdash A_{1},B_{1}\oplus B_{2},\Gamma
respects the properties.
Because
\vdash A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1}\oplus B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta
fulfills them, negative formulas of
\Gamma
are
\invamp
or negated atoms by
5
.
If
A_{1}
is a negative formula other than a
\invamp
or an atom, then for
\vdash A_{1},B_{i},\Gamma
respects the properties we have that
\Gamma
is empty and
B_{i}
of the desired shape.
By
1
,
B_{i}
is not a
0
, so
A_{1}
is not a
\top
.
But then
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
also has the wished shape for
A_{1}
, and
\vdash A_{1},B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
fulfills the properties.

C_{\otimes}^{\oplus_{i}}
commutation:

We prove
\vdash A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{i},\Gamma,\Delta
respects the properties.
As they are fulfilled by
\vdash A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1}\oplus B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta
, negative formulas of
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are
\invamp
or atoms by
5
.
As
A_{1}\otimes A_{2}
is distributed by
1
,
A_{1}
cannot be a
0
,
1
nor
\oplus
formula, so by
\vdash A_{1},B_{i},\Gamma
fulfilling the properties it follows that
B_{i}
cannot be a negative formula other than a
\invamp
or an atom.
The conclusion follows.

C_{\otimes}^{\invamp}
commutation:

We prove the properties for
\vdash A_{1},A_{2},B_{1}\otimes B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta
.
As they are respected by
\vdash A_{1}\invamp A_{2},B_{1}\otimes B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta
, according to
5
negative formulas of
\Gamma
and
\Delta
can only be
\invamp
-formulas or atoms.
As
A_{1}\invamp A_{2}
is distributed by
1
,
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are positive or
\invamp
-formulas or atoms.
The conclusion follows.

C_{\invamp}^{\otimes}
commutation:

We prove the properties for
\vdash A_{1}\invamp A_{2},B_{1},\Gamma
.
As
\vdash A_{1},A_{2},B_{1},\Gamma
respects them, by
5
negative formulas of
\Delta
and
B_{1}
can only be
\invamp
-formulas or atoms, proving the result.

Therefore, we proved the expanded axiom respects these properties, and they are preserved by all rule commutations.
The conclusion follows.

The main goal of this part is proving
\top
and
\bot
-rules in proofs of distributed isomorphisms also belong to these patterns.
Indeed, this property is preserved by cut anti-reduction, but it is not so easy to prove it.
We will consider slices to do so (recall
Section 2.1
on
\autopageref
def:slice_pt).

Cut-elimination can be extended from proofs to slices except that some reduction steps produce failures for slices: when a
\with_{i}
faces a
\oplus_{i}
and conversely.
The reduction of the slice

\vdash A,\Gamma

\oplus_{1}

\vdash

A\oplus B,\Gamma

\vdash B^{\perp},\Delta

\with_{1}

\vdash

B^{\perp}\with A^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta

is a failure since the selected sub-formulas of
A\oplus B
and its dual do not match.
Given two slices
s\in\mathcal{S}(\pi)
and
r\in\mathcal{S}(\rho)
with respective conclusions
\vdash A,\Gamma
and
\vdash A^{\perp},\Delta
, their composition by cut
s\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}r
reduces either to a slice of a normal form of
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\rho
or to a failure.

Lemma 18
.

Let
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
be proofs such that
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{2}
.
For each
s_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi_{2})
, there exists
s_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi_{1})
such that
s_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}s_{2}
or
s_{1}=s_{2}
.
Reciprocally, for each
s_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi_{1})
,
s_{1}
reduces to a failure or there exists
s_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi_{2})
such that
s_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}s_{2}
or
s_{1}=s_{2}
.

Proof 5.3
.

We can check that each cut-elimination step respects this property, with the equality case coming from a reduction in
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{2}
on rules not in the considered slice
s_{2}
(resp.
s_{1}
).

Lemma 19
.

Let
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
be proofs whose composition over
A
reduces to a proof
\tau
.
For each
s\in\mathcal{S}(\tau)
, there exist
s_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi_{1})
and
s_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi_{2})
such that
s_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}s_{2}
reduces to
s
.

Proof 5.4
.

By induction on
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\tau
, using
18
.

Lemma 20
.

Let
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
be cut-free proofs of
\vdash\Gamma
with
\top
-rules only of the shape

\top

\vdash

\top,0

.
Assume that
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}
, where in this sequence there is no rule commutation involving a
\top
-rule.
Then for each slice
s_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi_{1})
, there exists a unique
s_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi_{2})
such that
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
make the same choices for additive connectives in
\Gamma
.

Proof 5.5
.

This can be easily checked for each equation in
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
, save for those involving
\top
.

Given a choice
\mathcal{C}
of premises for some additive connectives of a sequent, we say a slice is
on
\mathcal{C}
if each
\with_{i}
and
\oplus_{i}
-rule in this slice takes premise
i
for a connective in
\mathcal{C}
whose chosen premise is
i
.
This concept will be essential when speaking about proof-nets (in
Section 6.1
for instance), as it is related to additive resolutions.

Lemma 21
.

Given a choice
\mathcal{C}
of premises for additive connectives of
A
(but not
A^{\perp}
), there exists a unique slice of
\mathcal{S}(\textnormal{id}_{A})
on it, which furthermore makes on
A^{\perp}
the dual choices of
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof 5.6
.

Direct induction on
A
, following the definition of
\textnormal{id}_{A}
on
Table 2
.

We now prove a partial reciprocal to
19
.

Lemma 22
.

Let
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
be cut-free proofs respectively of
\vdash A^{\perp},B
and
\vdash B^{\perp},A
, whose composition over
A
reduces to
\textnormal{id}_{B}
up to rule commutation.
Set
\rho
a normal form of
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}\pi^{\prime}
,
i.e.
any result of eliminating all
cut
-rules in this proof.
Then, for any slice
s
of
\pi
, there exists a slice
s^{\prime}
of
\pi^{\prime}
such that
s\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}s^{\prime}
reduces to a slice of
\rho
.

Proof 5.7
.

Take
s\in\mathcal{S}(\pi)
, and denote by
\mathcal{C}
the choices made in
s
on
\with
and
\oplus
connectives of the formula
B
.
We will use that the composition over the formula
A
reduces to
\textnormal{id}_{B}
up to
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
to find
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi^{\prime})
that makes the dual choices of
\mathcal{C}
on additive connectives of
B^{\perp}
.
This ensures no failure happens during the reduction of
s\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}s^{\prime}
, which thus reduces to a slice of
\rho
(
18
).

By hypothesis, call
\tau
a cut-free proof resulting from cut-elimination of
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\pi^{\prime}
, with
\tau\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\textnormal{id}_{B}
.
By
21
, there is a (unique) slice of
\textnormal{id}_{B}
with choices
\mathcal{C}
on
B
and dual choices
\mathcal{C}^{\perp}
on
B^{\perp}
.
Applying
17
, all proofs in the sequence
\tau\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\textnormal{id}_{B}
have
\top
-rules with only
0
in their context; in particular, there cannot be any commutation involving a
\top
-rule in this sequence.
Using
20
, there is a slice
t
of
\tau
with choices
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\perp}
.
According to
19
, we have slices
r\in\mathcal{S}(\pi)
and
r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi^{\prime})
whose composition on
A
reduces to
t
.
In particular,
r
makes choices
\mathcal{C}
on
B
and
r^{\prime}
choices
\mathcal{C}^{\perp}
on
B^{\perp}
, as these choices are those in the resulting slice
t
, and no reversed cut-elimination step can modify them (a
\top-cut
commutative case can erase such choices, but taking it in the other direction can only create choices).
Therefore,
r^{\prime}
makes on
B^{\perp}
the dual choices of
s
on
B
, and we can take such a slice as
s^{\prime}
.

22
will be used to prove that any non-
ax
rule
r
in
\pi
is not erased in all slices by
\with-\oplus_{i}
key cases during normalization.
More precisely, taking a slice
s
containing
r
, of principal connective in, say
A^{\perp}
, the lemma gives a slice
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi^{\prime})
that has no failure for a composition over
B
.
As
r
does not introduce a cut formula, the only way for it to be erased during the reduction is by a
\top-cut
commutative case, in which case its principal formula becomes a sub-formula of the sequent on which a
\top
-rule is applied.
This will be enough to conclude in our cases, as we know that the resulting normal form only has
\top
-rules of the shape

\top

\vdash

\top,0

(using
14
and
17
).

Remark 23
.

Cut-free MALL proofs have the
sub-sequent property
: every sequent in such a proof is a sub-sequent of the conclusion sequent, as every rule respects this property, except the
cut
-rule.

Lemma 24
.

Let
r
be a non-
ax
rule in a cut-free slice
s
of conclusion
\vdash\Gamma,A
, with the conclusion sequent of
r
of the shape
\Sigma_{\Gamma},\Sigma_{A}
where
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
is a sub-sequent of
\Gamma
and
\Sigma_{A}
of
A
.
Also assume the principal formula of
r
belongs to
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
,
i.e.
is not a sub-formula of
A
.
Take
s^{\prime}
a cut-free slice of conclusion
\vdash A^{\perp},\Delta
such that
s\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}s^{\prime}
reduces to a cut-free slice
s^{\prime\prime}
.
Then either there is in
s^{\prime\prime}
a rule of the same kind as
r
, applied on a sequent
\vdash\Sigma_{\Gamma},\Theta
with the same principal formula as
r
, or
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
is a sub-sequent of a
\top
-rule in
s^{\prime\prime}
, whose main
\top
-formula is not in
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
.

Proof 5.8
.

By hypothesis,
r
does not introduce a
cut
-formula, for these formulas are sub-formulas of
A
or
A^{\perp}
.
Therefore, the only reductions in
s\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}s^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}s^{\prime\prime}
that can erase a rule are
\top-cut
commutative cases, for the
\with_{i}-\oplus_{j}
key cases in the reduction do not lead to a failure.
If no such erasure happens, then we are done: other cut commutative cases involving
r
may modify its conclusion sequent, but only in the part coming from
A
, thus
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
is preserved.

If a
\top-cut
reduction erases
r
, then
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
is in the context of the resulting
\top
-rule
t
, possibly as a sub-sequent.
Furthermore, the principal
\top
-formula of
t
is not in
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
.
This is enough to conclude, using an induction on the number of steps in
s\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}s^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}s^{\prime\prime}
.
Notice that
t
may be erased too during the reduction but, like
r
, this would lead to
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
being a sub-sequent of the context of another
\top
-rule.

Lemma 25
.

If
\overset{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
, then all
\top
-rules in
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are of the form

\top

\vdash

\top,0

.

Proof 5.9
.

Consider
t
a
\top
-rule

\top

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta

in
\pi
, with
\Gamma
occurrences of sub-formulas of
A^{\perp}
and
\Delta
of
B
(as the cut-free
\pi
has for conclusion sequent is
\vdash A^{\perp},B
, see
23
).
By symmetry, say the main
\top
-formula of
t
belongs to
\Gamma
,
i.e.
is a sub-formula of
A^{\perp}
.
Call
s
a slice the
\top
-rule
t
belongs to.

Set
\rho
a normal form of
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}\pi^{\prime}
.
By
22
, there exists a slice
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi^{\prime})
such that
s\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}s^{\prime}
reduces to a slice
s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(\rho)
.
By
24
applied to
t
,
t
is either preserved and
\Gamma
as well, or
t
is absorbed by another
\top
-rule (during a
\top-cut
commutative case) and
\Gamma
stays in the context of a
\top
-rule, possibly as a sub-sequent.
But by
14
,
\rho\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\textnormal{id}_{A}
and, by
17
, the only
\top
-rules of
\rho
, and so of
s^{\prime\prime}
, are

\top

\vdash

\top,0

rules, with
\top
being the dual occurrence of
0
.
Thus,
\top
and
0
are not both sub-formula of
A^{\perp}
, and it follows
\Gamma
is either a sub-sequent of
\top
or one of
0
.
For
\Gamma
contains
\top
, we conclude that
\Gamma
is
\top
.
Moreover, it follows that
t
had not been erased during a
\top-cut
commutative case, using
24
(otherwise there would be at least two
\top
-formulas in the resulting
\top
-rule).
This implies that
\Delta
cannot be empty: if it were,
t
could not commute with any
cut
-rule, as it could not do a
\top-cut
commutative case for it does not have a cut formula, which is a sub-formula of
B
, in its context, and no other cut-elimination step can change this.
Thus, if
\Delta
were empty then
t
would be a rule of
s^{\prime\prime}
, impossible as it is a

\top

\vdash

\top

rule.

Similarly, set
\tau
a normal form of
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\pi^{\prime}
.
By
22
and
24
,
t
is either preserved during the reduction (in a slice) and
\Delta
as well, or
t
is absorbed by another
\top
-rule and
\Delta
stays in the context of a
\top
-rule, possibly as a sub-sequent.
But
t
cannot be preserved: its main formula is a sub-formula of
A^{\perp}
, and in
\tau
there are only occurrences of sub-formulas of
B
and
B^{\perp}
.
Therefore,
\Delta
in
\tau
is in the context of a
\top
-rule, and does not contains its principal
\top
-formula.
But by
14
and
17
, the only
\top
-rules of
\tau
are

\top

\vdash

\top,0

rules, with
\top
being the dual occurrence of
0
.
Hence,
\Delta
must be a sub-sequent of
0
.
As
\Delta
cannot be empty,
\Delta
is
0
.

Thus, any
\top
-rule
t
in
\pi
(and
\pi^{\prime}
by symmetry) is of the shape

\top

\vdash

\top,0

.

Lemma 26
.

Let
\pi
be an axiom-expanded proof whose
\top
-rules are all of the shape

\top

\vdash

\top,0

.
There is a cut elimination strategy, of
\pi
, such that all
\top-cut
commutative cases are of the form:

\top

\vdash

\top,0

\top

\vdash

\top,0

cut

\vdash

\top,0

\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}

\top

\vdash

\top,0

Proof 5.10
.

If a
\top-cut
commutative case can be applied, then the considered
cut
-rule is below a

\top

\vdash

\top,0

, with
\top
not the formula we cut on.
But then
0
is the formula we cut on, so there is a
\top
-formula on the other premise; we are in the following situation:

\top

\vdash

\top,0

\phi

\vdash

\top,\Gamma

cut

\vdash

\top,\Gamma

We commute non-
\top
-rules of
\phi
with the
cut
-rule, until reaching the rule introducing the found
\top
(in each slice).
This rule cannot be an
ax
-rule by hypothesis, so it is a
\top
-rule, of the given form.
At this point, our sub-proof is:

\top

\vdash

\top,0

\top

\vdash

\top,0

cut

\vdash

\top,0

We apply the
\top-cut
commutative case, obtaining

\top

\vdash

\top,0

as wished.
For other reduction steps, one can do whatever, except doing an infinity of
cut-cut
commutative cases.
This strategy terminates by
87
.

Lemma 27
.

If
\overset{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
, then there is no sequent of the shape
\vdash D\with E
in
\pi
(and
\pi^{\prime}
).

Proof 5.11
.

Assume
w.l.o.g.

D\with E
is a sub-formula of
A^{\perp}
, and let
s
be a slice containing the sequent
\vdash D\with E
.
Pose
\rho
a normal form of
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}\pi^{\prime}
obtained by following the strategy given by
26
.
By
22
, there exists a slice
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi^{\prime})
such that
s\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}s^{\prime}
reduces to a slice
s^{\prime\prime}
of
\rho
.
Since
D\with E
is a sub-formula of
A^{\perp}
, it is not a cut formula during the reduction, and the sequent
\vdash D\with E
remains in
s^{\prime\prime}
, so in
\rho
.
Indeed, reducing cuts using these steps preserves having the sequent
\vdash D\with E
(as there is no failure in the reduction).
This is trivial for all steps except
\top-cut
.
In the case a
\top-cut
step, by hypothesis on the reduction strategy, it cannot erase the sequent
\vdash D\with E
from the proof.
Thus, the sequent
\vdash D\with E
belongs to
\rho
, which is equal to the identity up to rule commutations (
14
).
This is impossible by
17
.

Lemma 28
.

If
\overset{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
, then all
\bot
-rules and
1
-rules belong to
1/\oplus/\bot
-patterns.

Proof 5.12
.

In
\pi
, we look at a possible rule
r
below a sequent
\vdash\resizebox{}{458752}{$\bigoplus$}[1]
(see proof of
17
for the definition of
\resizebox{}{458752}{$\bigoplus$}[\_]
).
It cannot be a
\otimes
-rule by distributivity, nor a
\invamp
-rule for the sequent has a unique formula, nor a
\with
-rule due to
27
.
If
r
is a
\oplus_{i}
-rule, then we keep a sequent
\vdash\resizebox{}{458752}{$\bigoplus$}[1]
, and if it is a
\bot
-rule then it is one of the required shape.

As a consequence, each
1
-rule is followed by some
\oplus_{i}
-rules and possibly a
\bot
-rule; let us call a
1/\oplus
-pattern
a
1
-rule followed by a maximal such sequence of
\oplus_{i}
-rules.
If a
1/\oplus
-pattern stops without a
\bot
-rule below it, we have only one formula in the conclusion sequent of the proof: impossible as
\pi
is a proof of
\vdash A^{\perp},B
.
Thus, the
\bot
-rule exists and to each
1
-rule we can associate a
\bot
-rule leading to a
1/\oplus/\bot
-pattern.
Henceforth, there are at least as many
\bot
-rules as
1
-rules, and as the patterns they belong to have no
\with
-rule, this also holds in any slice.

Consider a slice
s
of
\pi
.
By
22
, there exists a slice
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(\pi^{\prime})
such that
s\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}s^{\prime}
reduces to a slice
s^{\prime\prime}
of
\rho
, the latter being a normal form of
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}\pi^{\prime}
obtained by following the strategy given by
26
.
Moreover,
s^{\prime\prime}
contains as many
\bot
-rules as
1
-rules, as in
\rho
they belong to a
1/\oplus/\bot
-pattern (
14
and
17
), so are in the same slices.
Furthermore, in
s^{\prime\prime}
, each
1
from
A
(resp.
A^{\perp}
) corresponds to a
\bot
from
A^{\perp}
(resp.
A
).

Remark that, in the reduction
s\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}s^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}s^{\prime\prime}
, the only steps that may erase a
\bot
or
1
-rule are
\bot-1
and
\top-cut
cases.
But a
\top-cut
commutative case cannot erase non-
\top
-rules by definition of our cut-elimination strategy.
Furthermore, a
\bot-1
key case erases one
1
-rule and one
\bot
-rule.
Therefore, with
r_{s}
the number of
r
-rules of a slice
s
, we have
\bot_{s}+\bot_{s^{\prime}}=1_{s}+1_{s^{\prime}}
as
\bot_{s^{\prime\prime}}=1_{s^{\prime\prime}}
and any reduction step in
s\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}s^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}s^{\prime\prime}
preserves this equality.
But, by our analysis at the beginning of this proof,
1_{s}\leq\bot_{s}
and
1_{s^{\prime}}\leq\bot_{s^{\prime}}
.
We conclude
1_{s}=\bot_{s}
,
i.e.
that
s
has as many
\bot
-rules than
1
-rules.

However, each
1
-rule, belonging to a
1/\oplus/\bot
-pattern, belongs to exactly the same slices as the corresponding
\bot
-rule of the pattern.
Hence, a
\bot
-rule not in a
1/\oplus/\bot
-pattern would yield a slice
s
with strictly more
\bot
-rules than
1
-rules (taking
s
any slice containing this
\bot
-rule).
Thus, every
\bot
-rule belongs to a
1/\oplus/\bot
-pattern.

5.3.
Completeness with units from unit-free completeness

In the
1/\oplus/\bot
-patterns, moving each
\bot
-rule up to the associated
1
-rule (which can be done up to
\beta
-equality by
12
) allows us to consider units as fresh atoms introduced by
ax
-rules, whence reducing the problem to the unit-free fragment.

Theorem 29

(Isomorphisms completeness from unit-free completeness)
.

If
{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}
is complete for isomorphisms in unit-free MALL, then
\mathcal{L}
is complete for isomorphisms in MALL (
i.e.

A\mathrel{\simeq}B\implies A=_{\mathcal{L}}B
).

Proof 5.13
.

Take
A
and
B
formulas in MALL (possibly with units) such that
A\mathrel{\simeq}B
.
We assume
A
and
B
to be distributed thanks to
16
.
It suffices to prove that
A=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}B
.
Suppose
\overset{\pi,\tau}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
by
4
.
By Lemmas
25
and
28
,
\pi
and
\tau
have
\top
-rules only of the shape

\top

\vdash

\top,0

and
\bot
and
1
-rules in
1/\oplus/\bot
-patterns.
Using
\bot
-commutations to move each
\bot
-rule just below the
1
-rule above it, we build
\pi^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}
such that
\pi^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}
have
\top
-rules only of the shape

\top

\vdash

\top,0

,
\bot
and
1
-rules of the form

1

\vdash 1

\bot

\vdash

\bot,1

,
\pi\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi^{\prime}
and
\tau\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\tau^{\prime}
.
Whence,
\pi^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}\tau
and
\pi^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\tau
.
By
12
and
14
, for any normal form
\rho
of
\pi^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}\tau^{\prime}
(resp.
\pi^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\tau^{\prime}
),
\rho\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\textnormal{id}_{A}
(resp.
\rho\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\textnormal{id}_{B}
).

We reduce cuts in
\pi^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}\tau^{\prime}
(and similarly in
\pi^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\tau^{\prime}
) following a particular strategy, ensuring that the proofs obtained during the reduction have
\top
-rules only of the shape

\top

\vdash

\top,0

, and
\bot
and
1
-rules of the form

1

\vdash 1

\bot

\vdash

\bot,1

.

First, while we can apply a step of cut-elimination which is not a
\top-cut
,
\bot-cut
,
\bot-1
or
cut-cut
case, we do such a reduction step.
These operations preserve that all
\top
-rules are applied on sequents
\vdash\top,0
(up to exchange) and
\bot
and
1
-rules are in the wished pattern.

If no such reduction is possible, consider a highest
cut
-rule,
i.e.
one with no
cut
-rule above it.
The only possible cases that can be applied are
\top-cut
,
\bot-cut
or
\bot-1
.

•

If a
\top-cut
commutative case can be applied, then the
cut
-rule is below a
\top
-rule, so necessarily one of its premises is

\top

\vdash

\top,0

, with
\top
not the formula we cut on.
But then
0
is the formula we cut on, so there is a
\top
-formula on the other premise; we are in the following situation:

\top

\vdash

\top,0

\phi

r

\top,\Gamma

cut

\vdash

\top,\Gamma

We prove the rule
r
above the premise
\vdash\top,\Gamma
of the
cut
-rule is the
\top
-rule corresponding to the cut formula
\top
.
If it were not the case, then
r
commutes with the
cut
-rule.
But
r
cannot be a
\bot
-rule (which cannot have a
\top
-formula in its context), nor a
\top
-rule corresponding to another
\top
-formula (because such a rule would have two
\top
-formulas in its conclusion).
Thence,
r
is the
\top
-rule introducing the formula we cut on.
Thus, our sub-proof is:

\top

\vdash

\top,0

\top

\vdash

\top,0

cut

\vdash

\top,0

A
\top-cut
commutative case yields

\top

\vdash

\top,0

as if we had done an
ax
key case.

•

If a
\bot-cut
commutative case can be applied, then the
cut
-rule is below a
\bot
-rule, so necessarily one of its premises is

1

\vdash 1

\bot

\vdash

\bot,1

, with
\bot
not the formula we cut on.
But then
1
is the formula we cut on, so there is a
\bot
-formula on the other premise; we are in the following situation:

1

\vdash 1

\bot

\vdash

\bot,1

\phi

r

\bot,\Gamma

cut

\vdash

\bot,\Gamma

We prove the rule
r
above the premise
\vdash\bot,\Gamma
of the
cut
-rule is the
\bot
-rule corresponding to the cut formula
\bot
.
If it were not the case, then
r
commutes with the
cut
-rule.
But
r
cannot be a
\top
-rule (which cannot have a
\bot
-formula in its context), nor a
\bot
-rule corresponding to another
\bot
-formula (because such a rule would have two
\bot
-formulas in its conclusion).
Thence,
r
is the
\bot
-rule introducing the formula we cut on.
Thus, our sub-proof is:

1

\vdash 1

\bot

\vdash

\bot,1

1

\vdash 1

\bot

\vdash

\bot,1

cut

\vdash

\bot,1

We apply a
\bot-cut
commutative case, followed by a
\bot-1
key case, obtaining

1

\vdash 1

\bot

\vdash

\bot,1

as if we had done an
ax
key case.

•

No
\bot-1
key case can be applied, for
1
-rules have below them a
\bot
-rule, so not a
cut
-rule.

This strategy of reduction allows reaching a normal form
\rho
, with
\top
-rules only of the shape

\top

\vdash

\top,0

, and
\bot
and
1
-rules of the form

1

\vdash 1

\bot

\vdash

\bot,1

(
87
).

Furthermore, call
\sigma
the substitution replacing
\top
,
0
,
\bot
and
1
-formulas respectively by
X^{\perp}
,
X
,
Y^{\perp}
and
Y
, for
X
and
Y
fresh atoms.
We can reach
\sigma(\rho)
by cut-elimination from
\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\sigma(B)$}}}{\bowtie}}\sigma(\tau^{\prime})
, for the reductions we did on units could as well have been done by
ax
-key cases: no
\bot-cut
, nor
\bot-1
, nor
\top-cut
case were used, except for cases that could be simulated using
ax
-key cases.
Moreover,
\sigma(\textnormal{id}_{A})=\textnormal{id}_{\sigma(A)}
, and in
\rho\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\textnormal{id}_{A}
we can assume not to commute any
\bot
-rule because we start and end with
1
-rules and
\bot
-rules in

1

\vdash 1

\bot

\vdash

\bot,1

shapes only, and such commutations could only move the
\bot
-rule below or above some
\oplus_{i}
-rules according to
17
.
Thus,
\sigma(\rho)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\textnormal{id}_{\sigma(A)}
.
Using
12
, it follows
\sigma(\rho)\mathrel{=_{\beta\eta}}\textnormal{id}_{\sigma(A)}
, and therefore
\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\sigma(B)$}}}{\bowtie}}\sigma(\tau^{\prime})\mathrel{=_{\beta\eta}}\textnormal{ax}_{\sigma(A)}
with
\textnormal{ax}_{\sigma(A)}
the
ax
-rule on
\sigma(A)
.
A similar result holding for a cut over
A
, we have
\sigma(A)\mathrel{\simeq}\sigma(B)
, these formulas being unit-free.
For we assume
{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}
to be complete for unit-free isomorphisms, this yields
\sigma(A)=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}\sigma(B)
.
We conclude

by substituting
X
by
0
and
Y
by
