, as
X
and
Y
were fresh.

6.
Proof-nets for unit-free MALL

We will at present change the syntax, no longer considering sequent calculus proofs but
proof-nets
, choosing the syntax from Hughes & Van Glabbeek for unit-free MALL
[
HvG05
]
.
A key property of proof-nets is to be a more canonical representation of proofs, as they define a quotient of sequent calculus proofs up to rule commutations
[
HvG16
]
(we recall Tables
5
and
6
on Pages
5
and
6
give rule commutations).
2
2

2

While the definition of rule commutations in
[
HvG16
]
differs a little from ours, for they also consider commutations involving a
cut
-rule, both definitions coincide on cut-free proof-nets, which are the objects we want canonical.

This yields better properties concerning the study of isomorphisms.
As a key example, cut-elimination in proof-nets is confluent and leads to a unique normal form.
This spares us tedious case studies on rule commutations – like the one in the proof of
17
, which was due to the need to relate the different possible cut-free proofs obtained by cut-elimination.
Thus, studying isomorphisms using proof-nets will be less complex.
Nonetheless, we leave an inductive definition to a graphical syntax.
It would have been ideal to use this syntax from the very beginning.
Unfortunately, this was not possible as no notion of proof-nets, with exact quotient on normal forms, exists with units at the time this article is written.

Other definitions of proof-nets exist, see the original one from Girard
[
Gir96
]
, or others such as
[
DG11
,
HH16
]
. Still, the definition we take is one of the most satisfactory, from the point of view of canonicity and cut-elimination for instance (see
[
HvG05
,
HvG16
]
, or the introduction of
[
HH16
]
for a comparison of alternative definitions).
We recall here this definition of proof-nets, and define composition by cut and cut-elimination for this syntax.
Please refer to
[
HvG05
]
for more details, as well as the intuitions behind the definition.

In all that follows, as we use proof-nets, we consider only the unit-free fragment of MALL, unless stated otherwise.

6.1.
Proof-net

A sequent is seen as its syntactic forest, with as internal vertices its connectives and as leaves the atoms of its formulas.
We always identify a formula
A
with its syntactic tree
T(A)
.
A
cut pair
is a formula
A\ast A^{\perp}
, given a formula
A
; the connective
\ast
is unordered.
A
cut sequent

[\Sigma]~\Gamma
is composed of a list
\Sigma
of cut pairs and a sequent
\Gamma
. When
\Sigma=\emptyset
is empty, we denote it simply by
\Gamma
.
For instance,
[X_{5}\ast X_{6}^{\perp}]~X_{1}\with X_{2}^{\perp},X_{3}\oplus X_{4}^{\perp}
(where each
X_{i}
is an occurrence of the same atom
X
) is a cut sequent, on which we will instantiate the concepts defined in this part.

An
additive resolution
of a cut sequent
[\Sigma]~\Gamma
is any result of deleting zero or more cut pairs from
\Sigma
and one argument sub-tree of each additive connective (
\with
or
\oplus
) of
\Sigma\cup\Gamma
.
A
\with
-resolution
of a cut sequent
[\Sigma]~\Gamma
is any result of deleting one argument sub-tree of each
\with
-connective of
\Sigma\cup\Gamma
.
For example,
[~]~X_{1}\with~,~\oplus X_{4}^{\perp}
is one of the eight additive resolutions of
[X_{5}\ast X_{6}^{\perp}]~X_{1}\with X_{2}^{\perp},X_{3}\oplus X_{4}^{\perp}
, while
[X_{5}\ast X_{6}^{\perp}]~X_{1}\with~,X_{3}\oplus X_{4}^{\perp}
is one of its two
\with
-resolutions.
Notice the difference on cut pairs: they may be deleted in an additive resolution, but never in a
\with
-resolution.

An
(axiom) link
on
[\Sigma]~\Gamma
is an unordered pair of complementary leaves in
\Sigma\cup\Gamma
(labeled with
X
and
X^{\perp}
for some atom
X
).
A
linking

\lambda
on
[\Sigma]~\Gamma
is a set of links on
[\Sigma]~\Gamma
such that the set of the leaves of its links forms a partition of the set of leaves of an additive resolution of
[\Sigma]~\Gamma
, additive resolution which is denoted
[\Sigma]~\Gamma\upharpoonright\lambda
.
For instance, on the left-most graph of
Figure 2
, the red axiom links form a linking
\lambda_{1}=\{(X_{1},X_{6}^{\perp});(X_{4}^{\perp},X_{5})\}
, whose additive resolution is
[X_{5}\ast X_{6}^{\perp}]~X_{1}\with X_{2}^{\perp},X_{3}\oplus X_{4}^{\perp}\upharpoonright\lambda_{1}=[X_{5}\ast X_{6}^{\perp}]~X_{1}\with~,~\oplus X_{4}^{\perp}
.

Note the relation between slices in the sequent calculus and linkings in proof-nets: a slice “belongs” to an additive resolution, and a
\with
-resolution “selects” a slice from a proof.

A set of linkings
\Lambda
on
[\Sigma]~\Gamma

toggles
a
\with
-vertex
W
if both arguments (called
premises
) of
W
are in
{[\Sigma]~\Gamma\upharpoonright\Lambda\coloneqq\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}[\Sigma]~\Gamma\upharpoonright\lambda}
.
We say a link
a

depends
on a
\with
-vertex
W
in
\Lambda
if there exist
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda
such that
a\in\lambda\backslash\lambda^{\prime}
and
W
is the only
\with
-vertex toggled by
\{\lambda;\lambda^{\prime}\}
.
Looking at our running example, and taking
\lambda_{1}=\{(X_{1},X_{6}^{\perp}),(X_{4}^{\perp},X_{5})\}
and
\lambda_{2}=\{(X_{2}^{\perp},X_{3})\}
, the
\with
-vertex is toggled by
\{\lambda_{1};\lambda_{2}\}
.
Furthermore, all links depend on this
\with
-vertex for
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
contain only different pairs.

The graph
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
is defined as
[\Sigma]~\Gamma\upharpoonright\Lambda
with the edges from
\bigcup\Lambda
and enriched with jump edges
l\rightarrow W
for each leaf
l
and each
\with
-vertex
W
such that there exists
a\in\lambda\in\Lambda
, between
l
and some
l^{\prime}
, with
a
depending on
W
in
\Lambda
.
When
\Lambda=\{\lambda\}
is composed of a single linking, we shall simply denote
\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{G}_{\{\lambda\}}
(which is the graph
[\Sigma]~\Gamma\upharpoonright\lambda
with the edges from
\lambda
and no jump edge).
For our example, the graphs
\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{1}}
,
\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{2}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\{\lambda_{1};\lambda_{2}\}}
are illustrated on
Figure 2
.

In the text of this paper (but not on the graphs), we write
l\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$j$}}}{\longrightarrow}}W
for a jump edge from a leaf
l
to a
\with
-vertex
W
.
When drawing proof-nets, we will denote membership of a linking by means of colors.

When we write a
\invamp\backslash\with
-vertex, we mean a
\invamp
- or
\with
-vertex (a
negative
vertex); similarly a
\otimes\backslash\oplus
-vertex is a
\otimes
- or
\oplus
-vertex (a
positive
vertex).
A
switch edge
of a
\invamp\backslash\with
-vertex
N
is an in-edge of
N
,
i.e.
an edge between
N
and one of its premises or a jump to
N
.
A
switching cycle
is a cycle with at most one switch edge of each
\invamp\backslash\with
-vertex.
A
\invamp
-switching
of a linking
\lambda
is any subgraph of
\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}
obtained by deleting a switch edge of each
\invamp
-vertex; denoting by
\phi
this choice of edges, the subgraph it yields is
\mathcal{G}_{\phi}
.
For example, a cycle in a
\invamp
-switching is a switching cycle, as in a graph
\mathcal{G}_{\phi}
all
\invamp\backslash\with
-vertices have one premise.

{defi}

[Proof-net]

A
unit-free MALL proof-net

\theta
on a cut sequent
[\Sigma]~\Gamma
is a set of linkings satisfying:

(P0)

Cut:
Every cut pair of
\Sigma
has a leaf in
\theta
.

(P1)

Resolution:
Exactly one linking of
\theta
is on any given
\with
-resolution of
[\Sigma]~\Gamma
.

(P2)

MLL:
For every
\invamp
-switching
\phi
of every linking
\lambda\in\theta
,
\mathcal{G}_{\phi}
is a tree.

(P3)

Toggling:
Every set
\Lambda\subseteq\theta
of two or more linkings toggles a
\with
-vertex that is in no switching cycle of
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
.

These conditions are called the
correctness criterion
.
Condition (P0) is here to prevent unused
\ast
-vertices.
A
cut-free
proof-net is one without
\ast
-vertices (it respects (P0) trivially).
Condition (P1) is a correctness criterion for additive proof-nets
[
HvG05
]
and (P2) is the Danos-Regnier criterion for multiplicative proof-nets
[
DR89
]
.
However, (P1) and (P2) together are insufficient for cut-free MALL proof-nets, hence the last condition (P3) taking into account interactions between the slices (see also
[
DG11
]
for a similar condition for example).
Sets composed of a single linking
\lambda
are not considered in (P3), for by (P2) the graph
\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}
has no switching cycle.
One can check that our example on
Figure 2
,
\{\lambda_{1};\lambda_{2}\}
, is a proof-net.

X_{1}
X_{4}^{\perp}
\with
\oplus
\ast
X_{5}
X_{6}^{\perp}

X_{2}^{\perp}
X_{3}
\with
\oplus

X_{1}
X_{2}^{\perp}
X_{3}
X_{4}^{\perp}
\with
\oplus
\ast
X_{5}
X_{6}^{\perp}

Figure 2.
Graphs from an example of a proof-net: from left to right
\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{1}}
,
\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{2}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\{\lambda_{1};\lambda_{2}\}}

In the particular setting of isomorphisms, we mainly consider proof-nets with two conclusions.
This allows to define a notion of duality on leaves and connectives.
Consider a cut sequent containing both
A
and
A^{\perp}
.
For
V
a vertex in (the syntactic tree
T(A)
of)
A
, we denote by
V^{\perp}
the corresponding vertex in
A^{\perp}
.
As expected,
V^{\perp\perp}=V
.
This also respects orthogonality for formulas on leaves: given a leaf
l
of
A
, labeled by a formula
X
, the label of
l^{\perp}
is
X^{\perp}
.
We can also define a notion of duality on premises:
given a premise of a vertex
V\in T(A)
, the dual premise of
V^{\perp}
is the corresponding premise in
T(A^{\perp})
.
In other words, if in
L-V-R
we consider the premise
L
then in
R^{\perp}-V^{\perp}-L^{\perp}
its dual premise is
L^{\perp}
.

6.2.
Cut-elimination in proof-nets

{defi}

[Composition]

For proof-nets
\theta
and
\psi
of respective conclusions
[\Sigma]~\Gamma,A
and
[\Xi]~\Delta,A^{\perp}
, the
composition
over
A
of
\theta
and
\psi
is the proof-net
{\theta\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\psi=\{\lambda\cup\mu\ |\ \lambda\in\theta,\mu\in\psi\}}
, with conclusions
[\Sigma,\Xi,A\ast A^{\perp}]~\Gamma,\Delta
.

For example, see
Figure 8
with a composition of the proof-nets on
Figure 6
.

{defi}

[Cut-elimination]

Let
\theta
be a set of linkings on a cut sequent
[\Sigma]~\Gamma
, and
A\ast A^{\perp}
a cut pair in
\Sigma
.
Define the
elimination
of
A\ast A^{\perp}
(or of the cut
\ast
between
A
and
A^{\perp}
) as:

(1)

If
A
is an atom, delete
A\ast A^{\perp}
from
\Sigma
and replace any pair of links
(l,A)
,
(A^{\perp},m)
(
l
and
m
being other occurrences of
A^{\perp}
and
A
respectively) with the link
(l,m)
.

(2)

If
A=A_{1}\otimes A_{2}
and
A^{\perp}=A_{2}^{\perp}\invamp A_{1}^{\perp}
(or vice-versa), replace
A\ast A^{\perp}
with two cut pairs
A_{1}\ast A_{1}^{\perp}
and
A_{2}\ast A_{2}^{\perp}
. Retain all original linkings.

(3)

If
A=A_{1}\with A_{2}
and
A^{\perp}=A_{2}^{\perp}\oplus A_{1}^{\perp}
(or vice-versa), replace
A\ast A^{\perp}
with two cut pairs
A_{1}\ast A_{1}^{\perp}
and
A_{2}\ast A_{2}^{\perp}
. Delete all
inconsistent
linkings, namely those
\lambda\in\theta
such that in
[\Sigma]~\Gamma\upharpoonright\lambda
the children
\with
and
\oplus
of the cut do not take dual premises.
Finally, “garbage collect” by deleting any cut pair
B\ast B^{\perp}
for which no leaf of
B\ast B^{\perp}
is in any of the remaining linkings.

See
Figure 9
for a result on applying steps (b) and (c) to the proof-net of
Figure 8
.
We use for proof-nets the same notations
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
and
\mathrel{=_{\beta}}
as for the sequent calculus.

Proposition 30

(Proposition 5.4 in
[
HvG05
]
)
.

Eliminating a cut in a proof-net yields a proof-net.

Theorem 31

(Theorem 5.5 in
[
HvG05
]
)
.

Cut-elimination of proof-nets is strongly normalizing and confluent.

A linking
\lambda
on a cut sequent
[\Sigma]~\Gamma

matches
if, for every cut pair
A\ast A^{\perp}
in
\Sigma
, any given leaf
l
of
A
is in
[\Sigma]~\Gamma\upharpoonright\lambda
if and only if
l^{\perp}
of
A^{\perp}
is in
[\Sigma]~\Gamma\upharpoonright\lambda
.
A linking matches if and only if, when cut-elimination is carried out, the linking never becomes inconsistent, and thus is never deleted.
This allows defining
Turbo Cut-elimination

[
HvG05
]
, eliminating a cut in a single step by removing inconsistent linkings.

7.
Reduction to proof-nets

The goal of this section is to shift the study of isomorphisms to the syntax of proof-nets, never to speak of sequent calculus again.
We do so by first defining
desequentialization
, a function from sequent calculus proofs to proof-nets (
Section 7.1
).
We then show that cut-elimination in proof-nets simulates the one from sequent calculus (
Section 7.2
).
Finally, we define isomorphisms directly in the syntax of proof-nets (
Section 7.3
).
Recall that all sequent calculus proofs we consider have expanded axioms, thanks to
8
.

7.1.
Desequentialization

We desequentialize a unit-free MALL proof
\pi
(with expanded axioms) into a set of linkings
\mathcal{R}(\pi)
by induction on
\pi
:

ax

\{\{(X,X^{\perp})\}\}\triangleright[\emptyset]~X,X^{\perp}

\theta\triangleright[\Sigma]~\Gamma

ex

\theta\triangleright[\Sigma]~\sigma(\Gamma)

\theta\triangleright[\Sigma]~A,\Gamma

\psi\triangleright[\Xi]~A^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\{\lambda\cup\mu~|~\lambda\in\theta,\mu\in\psi\}\triangleright[\Sigma,\Xi,A\ast A^{\perp}]~\Gamma,\Delta

\theta\triangleright[\Sigma]~A,\Gamma

\psi\triangleright[\Xi]~B,\Delta

\otimes

\{\lambda\cup\mu~|~\lambda\in\theta,\mu\in\psi\}\triangleright[\Sigma,\Xi]~A\otimes B,\Gamma,\Delta

\theta\triangleright[\Sigma]~A,B,\Gamma

\invamp

\theta\triangleright[\Sigma]~A\invamp B,\Gamma

\theta\triangleright[\Xi]~A,\Gamma

\psi\triangleright[\Phi]~B,\Gamma

\with

\theta\cup\psi\triangleright[\Xi,\Phi]~A\with B,\Gamma

\theta\triangleright[\Sigma]~A,\Gamma

\oplus_{1}

\theta\triangleright[\Sigma]~A\oplus B,\Gamma

\theta\triangleright[\Sigma]~B,\Gamma

\oplus_{2}

\theta\triangleright[\Sigma]~A\oplus B,\Gamma

This definition uses the implicit tracking of formula occurrences downwards through the rules, and follows
[
HvG05
]
with the notation
\theta\triangleright[\Sigma]~\Gamma
for “
\theta
is a set of linkings on the cut sequent
[\Sigma]~\Gamma
”.
As identified in Section 5.3.4 of
[
HvG05
]
, desequentializing with both
cut
and
\with
-rules is complex, for cuts can be shared (or not) when translating a
\with
-rule:

\theta\triangleright[\Sigma,\Xi]~A,\Gamma

\psi\triangleright[\Sigma,\Phi]~B,\Gamma

\with

\theta\cup\psi\triangleright[\Sigma,\Xi,\Phi]~A\with B,\Gamma

.
We choose to never share cuts (
{\Sigma=\emptyset}
), thus desequentialization is a function.
The cost being that the following
\with-cut
commutation yields different proof-nets (contrary to the other commutations, see
[
HvG16
]
).

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A,B,\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A,C,\Gamma

\with

\vdash

A,B\with C,\Gamma

\pi_{3}

\vdash

A^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\vdash

B\with C,\Gamma,\Delta

\equiv

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A,B,\Gamma

\pi_{3}

\vdash

A^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\vdash

B,\Gamma,\Delta

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A,C,\Gamma

\pi_{3}

\vdash

A^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\vdash

C,\Gamma,\Delta

\with

\vdash

B\with C,\Gamma,\Delta

Remark 32
.

An alternative definition of desequentialization in
[
HvG05
]
consists in building a linking by slice.
In this spirit, if a proof-net
\theta
is obtained by desequentializing a proof
\pi
, there is a bijection between linkings in
\theta
and slices of
\pi
.

Theorem 33

(Sequentialization, Theorem 5.9 in
[
HvG05
]
)
.

A set of linkings on a cut sequent is a translation of a unit-free MALL proof if and only if it is a proof-net.

{defi}

[Identity proof-net]

We call
identity proof-net
of a unit-free formula
A
, the proof-net corresponding to the proof
\textnormal{id}_{A}
(the axiom-expansion of
\textnormal{ax}_{A}=

ax

\vdash

A
{}^{\perp},
A

).

7.2.
Simulation of cut elimination

We show here that cut elimination in proof-nets mimic the one in sequent calculus, which will allow us in the next section to consider isomorphisms on proof-nets only.
As written in
Section 7.1
, proof-nets have difficulties with the
\with-cut
commutation, which corresponds to superimposing
\ast
-vertices.

{defi}

[
\mathrel{\triangleleft}
]

Let
\theta
and
\psi
be proof-nets.
We denote
\theta\mathrel{\triangleleft}\psi
if there exists a
\ast
-vertex
C
in
\theta
such that the syntactic forest of
\psi
is the syntactic forest of
\theta
where the syntactic tree of
C
is duplicated into the syntactic trees of
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
(which are different occurrences of
C
),
\theta=\theta_{0}\sqcup\theta_{1}
3
3

3

The symbol
\sqcup
means a union
\cup
which happens to be between disjoint sets.
and
\psi=\psi_{0}\sqcup\psi_{1}
with, for
i\in\{0;1\}
,
\psi_{i}=\theta_{i}
up to assimilating
C_{i}
with
C
.

See
Figure 3
for a graphical representation of this concept, as well as the link with the
\with-cut
commutative case of cut-elimination.

\with
C
\mathrel{\triangleleft}
\with
C_{0}
C_{1}

\vdash A,B,\Gamma

\vdash A,D,\Gamma

\with

\vdash

A,B\with D,\Gamma

\vdash A^{\perp},\Delta

C

\vdash B\with D,\Gamma,\Delta

\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}

\vdash A,B,\Gamma

\vdash A^{\perp},\Delta

C_{0}

\vdash B,\Gamma,\Delta

\vdash A,D,\Gamma

\vdash A^{\perp},\Delta

C_{1}

\vdash D,\Gamma,\Delta

\with

\vdash

B\with D,\Gamma,\Delta

Figure 3.
Illustration of
\mathrel{\triangleleft}
(
Section 7.2
) and parallel with the
\with-cut
cut-elimination

Lemma 34

(Simulation -
\beta
)
.

Let
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
be unit-free MALL proof trees such that
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi^{\prime}
.
Then either
\mathcal{R}(\pi)=\mathcal{R}(\pi^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{R}(\pi)\mathrel{\triangleleft}\mathcal{R}(\pi^{\prime})
or
\mathcal{R}(\pi)\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{R}(\pi^{\prime})
.

Proof 7.1
.

We reason by cases according to the step
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi^{\prime}
.
Recall we desequentialize by separating all cuts, and use the notations for steps from
Section 6.2
.
If
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi^{\prime}
is an
ax
(resp.
\invamp-\otimes
,
\with-\oplus
) key case, then using a step (a) (resp. (b), (c)), we get
\mathcal{R}(\pi)\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{R}(\pi^{\prime})
.
If it is a
\invamp-cut
,
\otimes-cut-1
,
\otimes-cut-2
,
\oplus_{1}-cut
or
\oplus_{2}-cut
commutative case, then
\mathcal{R}(\pi)=\mathcal{R}(\pi^{\prime})
.
Finally, in a
\with-cut
commutative case, we duplicate the
cut
-rule:
\mathcal{R}(\pi)\mathrel{\triangleleft}\mathcal{R}(\pi^{\prime})
(see
Figure 3
).

Nonetheless, the
\mathrel{\triangleleft}
relation is not a hard problem since two proofs differing by a
\with-cut
commutation yield proof-nets equal up to cut-elimination.

Lemma 35

(
\mathrel{\triangleleft}~\subseteq~\mathrel{=_{\beta}}
)
.

Let
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
be proof-nets such that
\theta\mathrel{\triangleleft}\theta^{\prime}
.
Then
\theta\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\theta^{\prime}
.

Proof 7.2
.

By
Section 7.2
of
\mathrel{\triangleleft}
, there exists a
\ast
-vertex
C
in
\theta
, with
\theta=\theta_{0}\sqcup\theta_{1}
, such that
\theta^{\prime}
is
\theta
where the syntactic tree of
C
is duplicated into
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
, and linkings in
\theta_{0}
(respectively
\theta_{1}
) use
C_{0}
(respectively
C_{1}
) as
C
.

We reason by induction on the size of the formula
A
of
C
(and also
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
);
w.l.o.g.

A
is positive.
Applying a step of cut-elimination on
C
in
\theta
yields a proof-net
\Theta
.
On the other hand, a corresponding step of cut-elimination on
C_{0}
and one on
C_{1}
in
\theta^{\prime}
yields
\Theta^{\prime}
.

If
A
is an atom, then we applied step (a), and we find
\Theta=\Theta^{\prime}
.

If
A
is a
\otimes
-formula,
i.e.

A=A_{0}\otimes A_{1}
, then we applied step (b) and produced cuts
A_{0}\ast A_{0}^{\perp}
and
A_{1}\ast A_{1}^{\perp}
in
\Theta
, and two occurrences of these cuts in
\Theta^{\prime}
.
Thus,
\Theta\mathrel{\triangleleft}\Xi\mathrel{\triangleleft}\Theta^{\prime}
with
\Xi
the proof-net
\Theta
where the cut on
A_{0}
is duplicated.
By induction hypothesis,
\Theta\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\Xi\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\Theta^{\prime}
.
It follows
\theta\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\theta^{\prime}
as
\theta\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\Theta\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\Theta^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\theta^{\prime}
.

Finally, if
A
is a
\oplus
-formula with
A=A_{0}\oplus A_{1}
, then we used step (c), producing cuts
A_{0}\ast A_{0}^{\perp}
and
A_{1}\ast A_{1}^{\perp}
in
\Theta
, and two occurrences of these cuts in
\Theta^{\prime}
.
Remark that inconsistent linkings in
\theta^{\prime}
for these steps are exactly those of
\theta
, and therefore the same cuts are garbage collected.
Whence,
\Theta\mathrel{\triangleleft}\cdot\mathrel{\triangleleft}\Theta^{\prime}
,
\Theta\mathrel{\triangleleft}\Theta^{\prime}
or
\Theta=\Theta^{\prime}
(according to the number of cuts garbage collected).
In all cases, using the induction hypothesis we conclude
\theta\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\theta^{\prime}
.

Remark 36
.

Another proof of
35
, using the Turbo Cut-elimination procedure and no induction, is possible.
We use the Turbo Cut-elimination procedure on
C
in
\theta
, yielding a proof-net
\Theta
; we also use it in
\theta^{\prime}
on
C_{0}
then
C_{1}
, yielding
\Theta^{\prime}
.
Whence,
\theta\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\Theta
and
\Theta^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\theta^{\prime}
.
It stays to prove that
\Theta=\Theta^{\prime}
.
Remark that
\Theta
and
\Theta^{\prime}
can only differ by their linkings, for they have the same syntactic forest.
Notice that a linking in
\theta_{i}
,
i\in\{0;1\}
, matches for
C
in
\theta
if and only if it matches for
C_{i}
in
\theta^{\prime}
, because this linking uses
C_{i}
as
C
.
Thence, the same linkings stay in
\Theta
and
\Theta^{\prime}
, and
\Theta=\Theta^{\prime}
follows.

Theorem 37

(Simulation Theorem)
.

Let
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
be unit-free MALL proof trees with expanded axioms.
If
\pi\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\pi^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{R}(\pi)\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\mathcal{R}(\pi^{\prime})
.

Proof 7.3
.

This is a corollary of Lemmas
34
and
35
.

We call
\mathcal{B}(\theta)
the
\beta
-normal form of the proof-net
\theta
, with all cuts eliminated (thanks to
30
and
31
).

Lemma 38
.

Let
\pi
,
\tau
and
\rho
be unit-free MALL proof trees of respective sequents
\vdash A,\Gamma
,
\vdash A^{\perp},\Delta
and
\vdash\Gamma,\Delta
, with expanded axioms.
Assume
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\tau\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\rho
.
Then
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{R}(\pi))\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{R}(\tau))
reduces, after fully eliminating the cut on
A
, to
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{R}(\rho))
.

Proof 7.4
.

By the Simulation Theorem (
37
),
\mathcal{R}(\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\tau)\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\mathcal{R}(\rho)
.
By definition of
\mathcal{R}
(
Section 7
),
\mathcal{R}(\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\tau)=\mathcal{R}(\pi)\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\mathcal{R}(\tau)
, so
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{R}(\pi)\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\mathcal{R}(\tau))=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{R}(\rho))
.
Moreover, by confluence of cut-elimination (
31
),
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{R}(\pi)\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\mathcal{R}(\tau))
can be obtained by taking the
\beta
-normal forms of
\mathcal{R}(\pi)
and
\mathcal{R}(\tau)
, composing them over
A
and reducing this cut.
In other words,
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{R}(\pi))\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{R}(\tau)))=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{R}(\pi)\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\mathcal{R}(\tau))=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{R}(\rho))
, and the result follows.

7.3.
Isomorphisms in proof-nets

A notion of isomorphism
\overset{\theta,\psi}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
can be defined directly on proof-nets:
\theta
and
\psi
are two cut-free proof-nets of respective conclusions
A^{\perp},B
and
B^{\perp},A
such that
\theta\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}\psi
and
\psi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\theta
reduce by cut-elimination to identity proof-nets.
Using previous results, we obtain:

Theorem 39

(Type isomorphisms in proof-nets)
.

Let
A
and
B
be two unit-free MALL formulas.
If
A\mathrel{\simeq}B
then there exist two proof-nets
\theta
and
\psi
such that
\overset{\theta,\psi}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
.

Proof 7.5
.

Using
4
followed by
8
, there exist unit-free MALL cut-free proofs with expanded axioms
\pi
and
\tau
, respectively of
\vdash A^{\perp},B
and
\vdash B^{\perp},A
, such that
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}\tau\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\textnormal{id}_{A}
and
\tau\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\pi\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\textnormal{id}_{B}
.
By
38
and as
\mathcal{R}(\textnormal{id}_{A})
,
\mathcal{R}(\pi)
and
\mathcal{R}(\tau)
are cut-free,
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{R}(\pi)\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}\mathcal{R}(\tau))=\mathcal{R}(\textnormal{id}_{A})
.
Similarly,
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{R}(\tau)\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\mathcal{R}(\pi))=\mathcal{R}(\textnormal{id}_{B})
.
Thus, there exist cut-free proof-nets
\mathcal{R}(\pi)
and
\mathcal{R}(\tau)
whose composition over
B
(resp.
A
) yields after cut-elimination the identity proof-net of
A
(resp.
B
).

Remark 40
.

The converse of
39
holds.
Indeed, the goal of the next section is to prove
\overset{\theta,\psi}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}\implies A=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}B
.
Therefore, using
5
and
39
:

A=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}B\implies A\mathrel{\simeq}B\implies\overset{\theta,\psi}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}\implies A=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}B

8.
Completeness

Our method relates closely to the one used by Balat and Di Cosmo in
[
BDC99
]
, with some more work due to the distributivity isomorphisms.
We work on proof-nets, as they highly simplify the problem by representing proofs up to rule commutations
[
HvG16
]
.
We start by studying identity proof-nets (
Section 8.1
), then prove isomorphisms yield proof-nets of a particular shape, called
bipartite full
(
Section 8.2
).
Having previously reduced the problem to distributed formulas (
16
), we can consider even more constrained proof-nets (
Section 8.3
).
These are the key differences with the proof in MLL from
[
BDC99
]
, where some properties are given for free as there is no slice nor distributivity isomorphism.
From this point the problem is similar to unit-free MLL, with commutativity and associativity only.
We conclude as in
[
BDC99
]
: restricting the problem to so-called
non-ambiguous
formulas (
Section 8.4
), isomorphisms are easily characterized (
Section 8.5
).

The special shapes of proof-nets we consider are the followings.

{defi}

[Full,
Ax
-unique, Bipartite proof-net]
A cut-free proof-net is called
full
if any of its leaves has (at least) one link on it.
Furthermore, if for any leaf there exists a unique link on it (possibly shared among several linkings), then we call this proof-net
ax-unique
.

A cut-free proof-net is
bipartite
if it has two conclusions,
A
and
B
, and each of its links is between a leaf of
A
and a leaf of
B
(no link between leaves of
A
, or between leaves of
B
).

For instance:

•

the three proof-nets on
Figure 4
(
\autopageref
fig:id_bi_u) are bipartite and
ax
-unique

•

the top-left proof-net of
Figure 5
(
\autopageref
fig:no_anticut) is non-bipartite, full and non-
ax
-unique; meanwhile, its top-right proof-net is bipartite and non-full (so non-
ax
-unique)

•

both proof-nets on
Figure 6
(
\autopageref
fig:dist_not_uniq) are bipartite, full and non-
ax
-unique

Lemma 41
.

Let
\theta
and
\psi
be bipartite proof-nets of respective conclusions
A,B
and
B^{\perp},C
.
Their composition over
B
reduces to a bipartite proof-net.

Proof 8.1
.

The resulting proof-net has for conclusions
A,C
.
The only links in the new proof-net that were not in
\theta
nor in
\psi
are those resulting from the replacement of a pair of links
(l,m)
and
(m^{\perp},n)
with a link
(l,n)
, where
m
is a leaf of
B
.
By bipartiteness of
\theta
and
\psi
, it follows
l
is a leaf of
A
and
n
one of
C
, so the new axiom link is between a leaf of
A
and one of
C
.

8.1.
Properties of identity proof-nets

Using an induction on the formula
A
, we can prove the following results on the identity proof-net of
A
, and in particular that it is bipartite
ax
-unique (see
Figure 4
for a graphical intuition).

Lemma 42
.

The axiom links of an identity proof-net are exactly the
(l,l^{\perp})
, for any leaf
l
.

Proof 8.2
.

By induction on the formula (see
Figure 4
).

Corollary 43
.

An identity proof-net is bipartite ax-unique.

Proof 8.3
.

This follows from
42
.

Lemma 44
.

Let
\lambda
be a linking of an identity proof-net and
V
an additive vertex in its additive resolution.
Then
V^{\perp}
is also inside with, as premise kept, the dual premise of the one kept for
V
.

Proof 8.4
.

Assume
w.l.o.g.
that the left premise of
V
is kept in
\lambda
.
There is a left-ancestor
l
of
V
in the additive resolution of
\lambda
, hence with a link
a\in\lambda
on it.
By
42
,
a=(l,l^{\perp})
.
As
l^{\perp}
is a right-ancestor of
V^{\perp}
, the conclusion follows.

The next result allows to go from exactly one linking on any
\with
-resolution of
A^{\perp},A
(P1) to exactly one linking on any additive resolution of
A
.

Lemma 45
.

In the identity proof-net of
A
, exactly one linking is on any given additive resolution of the conclusion
A
.

Proof 8.5
.

Consider such an additive resolution
R
. There is an associated
\with
-resolution
R^{\prime}
of
A^{\perp},A
by taking the choices of premise of
R
on
A
and, for a
\with
-vertex
W
of
A^{\perp}
, taking the dual premise chosen in
R
for
W^{\perp}
.
By
44
, a linking
\lambda
is on
R
if and only if it is on
R^{\prime}
.
Meanwhile, by (P1) there is a unique linking
\lambda
on
R^{\prime}
; thus the same holds on
R
.

8.2.
Bipartite full proof-nets

We prove here that proof-nets of isomorphisms are bipartite full.
Neither fullness,
ax
-uniqueness nor bipartiteness is preserved by cut anti-reduction.
A counter-example is given on
Figure 5
, with a non bipartite proof-net and a non full one whose composition reduces to the identity proof-net (bipartite
ax
-unique by
43
).
However, if
both
compositions yield identity proof-nets,
we get bipartiteness and fullness.

Lemma 46
.

Let
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
be cut-free proof-nets of respective conclusions
A^{\perp},B
and
B^{\perp},A
, such that
\theta^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$A$}}}{\bowtie}}\theta
reduces to the identity proof-net of
B
.
For any linking
\lambda\in\theta
, there exists
\lambda^{\prime}\in\theta^{\prime}
such that
\lambda\cup\lambda^{\prime}
matches in the composition over
B
of
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
,
\theta\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$B$}}}{\bowtie}}\theta^{\prime}
.

Proof 8.6
.

Let us consider a linking
\lambda\in\theta
, and call
\mathcal{C}
the choices of premise on additive connectives of
B
that
\lambda
makes.
We search some
\lambda^{\prime}\in\theta^{\prime}
making the dual choices of premise on additive connectives of
B^{\perp}
compared to
\mathcal{C}
.
Consider the composition of
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
over
A
.
It reduces to the identity proof-net of
B
by hypothesis.
By
45
, there exists a unique linking
\nu
in the identity proof-net of
B
corresponding to
\mathcal{C}
.
Furthermore, this linking
\nu
of the identity proof-net is derived from some
\mu\cup\mu^{\prime}
for
\mu
a linking of
\theta
and
\mu^{\prime}
one of
\theta^{\prime}
, with
\mu\cup\mu^{\prime}
matching for a cut over
A
: a linking in the identity proof-net is a linking of the form
\mu\cup\mu^{\prime}
where axiom links
(l,m_{1})
,
(m_{1}^{\perp},m_{2})
, …,
(m_{n}^{\perp},l^{\perp})
in
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are replaced with
(l,l^{\perp})
, with
l
a leaf of
B
and the
m_{i}
and
m_{i}^{\perp}
of
A^{\perp}
and
A
(because an identity proof-net has only links of the form
(l,l^{\perp})
by
42
).
Therefore,
\mu
makes the choices
\mathcal{C}
on
B
and
\mu\cup\mu^{\prime}
matches for the composition of
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
over both
A
and
B
.
But
\lambda
makes the same choices
\mathcal{C}
on
B
as
\mu
:
\lambda\cup\mu^{\prime}
also matches for a cut over
B
.

Remark 47
.

46
is the analogue of
22
in proof-nets

Corollary 48
.

Assuming
\overset{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
,
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
are bipartite.

Proof 8.7
.

We proceed by contradiction:
w.l.o.g.
there is a link
a
in some linking
\lambda\in\theta
which is between leaves of
A^{\perp}
.
By
46
there exists
\lambda^{\prime}\in\theta^{\prime}
such that
\lambda\cup\lambda^{\prime}
matches for a cut over
B
.
Whence
a
, which does not involve leaves of
B
, belongs to a linking of the composition where cuts have been eliminated (it belongs to the linking resulting from
\lambda\cup\lambda^{\prime}
).
But this reduction yields the identity proof-net of
A
, which is bipartite by
43
, so there cannot be an axiom link between leaves of
A^{\perp}
inside: contradiction.

Lemma 49
.

Assume
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
are cut-free proof-nets of respective conclusions
A^{\perp},B
and
B^{\perp},A
, and that their composition over
B
yields the identity proof-net of
A
.
Then any leaf of
A^{\perp}
(resp.
A
) has (at least) one axiom link on it in
\theta
(resp.
\theta^{\prime}
).

Proof 8.8
.

Towards a contradiction, assume
w.l.o.g.
a leaf
l
of
A^{\perp}
has no link on it in
\theta
.
Then, the composition over
B
of
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
has no link on
l
either.
And reducing cuts cannot create links using
l
, for it only takes links
(l,m)
and
(m,n)
to merge them into
(l,n)
.
However, the identity proof-net of
A
is ax-unique by
43
, thence full: contradiction.

Remark 50
.

49
holds not only for isomorphisms but more generally for retractions, which are formulas
A
and
B
such that there exist proofs
\pi
of
\vdash A^{\perp},B
and
\pi^{\prime}
of
\vdash B^{\perp},A
whose composition by cut over
B
(but not necessarily over
A
) is equal to the axiom on
\vdash A^{\perp},A
up to axiom-expansion and cut-elimination.
An example of retraction is given by the proof-nets on
Figure 5
, yielding a retraction between
(A\invamp A^{\perp})\otimes B
and
((A\invamp A^{\perp})\otimes B)\oplus B
, which is not an isomorphism.
As another example, in MLL there is a well-known retraction between
A
and
(A\multimap A)\multimap A=(A\otimes A^{\perp})\invamp A
.

Theorem 51
.

Assuming
\overset{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
,
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
are bipartite full.

Proof 8.9
.

By
48
,
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
are bipartite, and thanks to
49
, they are full.

8.3.
Ax
-uniqueness

In general, isomorphisms do not yield
ax
-unique proof-nets.
A counter-example is distributivity:
A\otimes(B\oplus C)\mathrel{\simeq}(A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes C)
, see
Figure 6
.
Nonetheless, distributivity equations are the only ones in
{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}
not giving
ax
-unique proof-nets.
Recall we can consider only distributed formulas (
16
).
On distributed formulas, distributivity isomorphisms can be ignored, and isomorphisms between distributed formulas happen to be bipartite
ax
-unique.

\oplus

8.3.1.
Preliminary results

We mostly use the correctness criterion through the fact we can sequentialize,
i.e.
recover a proof tree from a proof-net by
33
.
However, in order to prove
ax
-uniqueness, we make a direct use of the correctness criterion to deduce geometric properties of proof-nets.
This part of the proof takes benefits from the specificities of this syntax.
We begin with two preliminary results.
For
\Lambda
a set of linkings and
W
a
\with
-vertex,
\Lambda^{W}
denote the set of all linkings in
\Lambda
whose additive resolution does not contain the right argument of
W
.

Lemma 52

(Adaptation of Lemma 4.32 in
[
HvG05
]
)
.

Let
\omega
be a jump-free switching cycle in a proof-net
\theta
.
There exists a subset of linkings
\Lambda\subseteq\theta
such that
\omega\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
,
\omega\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{W}}
and for any
\with
-vertex
W
toggled by
\Lambda
, there exists an axiom link
a\in\omega
depending on
W
in
\Lambda
.

Proof 8.10
.

The proof of this lemma uses some facts from
[
HvG05
]
reproduced verbatim here.
Write
\lambda\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$W$}}}{=}}\lambda^{\prime}
if linkings
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\theta
are either equal or
W
is the only
\with
toggled by
\{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\}
.
A subset
\Lambda
of a proof-net
\theta
is
saturated
if any strictly larger subset toggles more
\with
than
\Lambda
.
It is straightforward to check that:

(S1)

If
\Lambda
is saturated and toggles
W
, then
\Lambda^{W}
is saturated.

(S2)

If
\Lambda
is saturated and toggles
W
and
\lambda\in\Lambda
, then
\lambda\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$W$}}}{=}}\lambda_{W}
for some
\lambda_{W}\in\Lambda^{W}
.

Let us now prove our lemma.
Take
\Lambda
a minimal saturated subset of
\theta
with
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
containing
\omega
, and
W
a
\with
-vertex it toggles.
Since
\Lambda
is minimal,
\omega\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{W}}
using (S1), so some edge
e
of
\omega
is in
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
but not in
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{W}}
.
We claim that, without loss of generality,
e
is an axiom link.
If it is indeed the case then, by (S2),
e\in\lambda\in\Lambda
and
e\notin\lambda_{W}
for
e\notin\Lambda^{W}
, so
e
depends on
W
in
\Lambda
.
We now prove our claim by eliminating other possibilities step by step.

Without loss of generality,
e
is an edge from a leaf
l
to some
X
, because for any other edge
Y\rightarrow X
in
\omega
we have
l\rightarrow Z_{1}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow Z_{n}\rightarrow Y\rightarrow X
in
\omega
for some leaf
l
, and
Y\rightarrow X
is in
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{W}}
whenever
l\rightarrow Z_{1}
is in
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{W}}
.
This is because a switching cycle must have an edge using a leaf, for all other edges are in the syntactic forest of the sequent, which is acyclic.

Still without loss of generality,
e
is not an edge in a syntactic tree.
Indeed, in such a case
e\notin\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{W}}
implies
l\notin\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{W}}
.
As
e
belongs to the switching cycle
\omega
, let us look at the other edge in this cycle with endpoint
l
, say
e^{\prime}
. As
l\notin\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{W}}
, we also have
e^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{W}}
.
Remark that
e^{\prime}
cannot be an edge in a syntactic tree, for only one such edge has for endpoint the leaf
l
, namely
e
.
We can replace
e
with
e^{\prime}
to assume
e
is not an edge in a syntactic tree.

As
\omega
is jump-free,
e
cannot be a jump edge.
The sole possibility is
e
being a link.

For
U
and
V
vertices in a tree, their
first common descendant
is the vertex of the tree which is a descendant of both
U
and
V
and which has no ancestor respecting this property, with a tree represented with its root at the bottom, which is a
descendant
of the leaves.
Or equivalently, looking at a tree as a partial order of minimal element the root, the first common descendant is the infimum.

Lemma 53
.

Let
\theta
be a proof-net of conclusions
\Gamma,A
, with a jump edge
l\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$j$}}}{\longrightarrow}}W
between
l,W\in T(A)
.
If
W
is not a descendant of
l
, then their first common descendant
C
is a
\invamp
.

Proof 8.11
.

As there is a jump

, there exist linkings

such that

is the only

, and a link

using the leaf

. In particular, the jump

is in
\mathcal{G}_{\{\lambda;\lambda^{\prime}\}}
.
For
l
and
W
are both in the additive resolution of
\lambda
, both premises of
C
are also in this additive resolution, thus
C
cannot be an additive connective, so not a
\with
nor a

-vertex.

Assume by contradiction that
C
is a
\otimes
.
Call
\delta
the path in
T(A)
from
W
to
C
, and
\mu
the one from
C
to
l
(see
Figure 7
).
Then,
(l\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$j$}}}{\longrightarrow}}W)\delta\mu
is a switching cycle in
\mathcal{G}_{\{\lambda;\lambda^{\prime}\}}
.
According to (P3), there exists a
\with
toggled by
\{\lambda;\lambda^{\prime}\}
not in any switching cycle of
\mathcal{G}_{\{\lambda;\lambda^{\prime}\}}
.
A contradiction, for
W
is the only
\with
toggled by
\{\lambda;\lambda^{\prime}\}
.
Whence,
C
can only be a
\invamp
.

A
T(A)
l
W\with

\mu
\delta
j

8.3.2.
Isomorphisms of distributed formulas

Now, let us prove that isomorphisms of distributed formulas are bipartite
ax
-unique.
We will consider proof-nets corresponding to an isomorphism that we cut and where we eliminate all cuts not involving atoms.
To give some intuition, let us consider the non-
ax
-unique proof-nets of
Figure 6
(on
\autopageref
fig:dist_not_uniq).
Composing them together by cut on
(A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes C)
gives the proof-net illustrated on
Figure 8
.
Reducing all cuts not involving atoms yields the proof-net on
Figure 9
, that we call an
almost reduced composition
.
We stop there because of the switching cycle produced by the two links on
A
(dashed in blue on
Figure 9
), less visible in the non-reduced composition of
Figure 8
.
However, reducing all cuts gives the identity proof-net, which has no switching cycle: during these reductions, both links on
A
are merged.
By using almost reduced composition, we are going to prove that links preventing
ax
-uniqueness yield switching cycles, and moreover that these cycles are due to non-distributed formulas only.

{defi}

[Almost reduced composition]

Take
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
cut-free proof-nets of respective conclusions
\Gamma,B
and
B^{\perp},\Delta
.
The
almost reduced composition
over
B
of
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
is the proof-net resulting from the composition over
B
of
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
where we repeatedly reduce all cuts not involving atoms (
i.e.
not applying step (a) of
Section 6.2
).

Let us fix
A
and
B
two unit-free MALL (not necessarily distributed yet) formulas as well as
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
such that
\overset{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
.
By
51
,
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
are bipartite full.
We denote by
\psi
the almost reduced composition over
B
of
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
.
Here, we can extend our duality on vertices and premises (defined in
Section 6
) to links.

Lemma 54
.

An axiom link
a=(l,m)
belongs to some linking
\lambda\in\psi
if and only, up to swapping
l
and
m
,
l
is a leaf of
A^{\perp}
(resp.
A
),
m
is in the leaves of
B
(resp.
B^{\perp}
) and there is an axiom link
(l^{\perp},m^{\perp})
in the same linking
\lambda
, that we will denote
a^{\perp}=(l^{\perp},m^{\perp})
(see
Figure 10
).

Proof 8.12
.

Linkings of
\psi
are disjoint union of linkings in
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
.
By symmetry, assume
(l,m)\in\lambda\in\psi
comes from a linking in
\theta
.
As
\theta
is bipartite, one of the leaves, say
l
, is in
A^{\perp}
and the other,
m
, is a leaf of
B
.
For the cut
m\ast m^{\perp}
belongs to the additive resolution of
\lambda
(for
m
is inside),
m^{\perp}
is a leaf in this resolution. Thus, there is a link
(m^{\perp},l^{\prime})\in\lambda
for some leaf
l^{\prime}
, which necessarily belongs to
A
by bipartiteness of
\theta^{\prime}
.
It stays to prove
l^{\prime}=l^{\perp}
.
If we were to eliminate all cuts in
\psi
, we would get the identity proof-net on
A
by hypothesis.
But eliminating the cut
m\ast m^{\perp}
yields a link
(l,l^{\prime})
, which is not modified by the elimination of the other atomic cuts.
By
42
,
l^{\prime}=l^{\perp}
follows.

Lemma 55
.

Let
\lambda
be a linking of
\psi
, and
V
an additive vertex in its additive resolution.
Then

is also inside, with as premise kept the dual premise of the one kept for

.

Proof 8.13
.

Assume

that the left premise of

\lambda
.
There is a left-ancestor

.
As

Lemma 56
.

Let
W
and
P
be respectively a
\with
-vertex and a
\oplus
-vertex in
\psi
, with
W
an ancestor of
P
.
Then for any axiom link

in

,

in
\psi
.

Proof 8.14
.

There exist linkings
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\psi
such that
W
is the only
\with
toggled by
\{\lambda;\lambda^{\prime}\}
and
a\in\lambda\backslash\lambda^{\prime}
.
We consider a linking
\lambda_{P^{\perp}}
defined by taking an arbitrary
\with
-resolution of
\lambda
where we choose the other premise for
P^{\perp}
(and arbitrary premises for
\with
-vertices introduced this way, meaning for
\with
-ancestors of
P^{\perp}
): by (P1), there exists a unique linking on it.
By
55
, the additive resolutions of
\lambda
and
\lambda_{P^{\perp}}
(resp.
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
) differ, on

and

, exactly on ancestors of

and
P^{\perp}
(resp.
W
and

). Thus, the additive resolutions of
\lambda^{\prime}
and
\lambda_{P^{\perp}}
also differ, on

and
A^{\perp}
, exactly on ancestors of
P
and
P^{\perp}
, for
W
is an ancestor of
P
.
In particular,
\{\lambda;\lambda_{P^{\perp}}\}
, as well as
\{\lambda^{\prime};\lambda_{P^{\perp}}\}
, toggles only
P^{\perp}
.
If

, then

depends on
P^{\perp}
in
\{\lambda^{\prime};\lambda_{P^{\perp}}\}
.
Otherwise,

depends on
P^{\perp}
in
\{\lambda;\lambda_{P^{\perp}}\}
.

The key result to use distributivity is that a positive vertex “between” a leaf
l
and a
\with
-vertex
W
in the same tree prevents them from interacting,
i.e.
there is no jump
l\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$j$}}}{\longrightarrow}}W
.

Lemma 57
.

Let
l\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$j$}}}{\longrightarrow}}W
be a jump edge in
\psi
, with
l
not an ancestor of
W
and
l,W\in T(A^{\perp})
(resp.
T(A)
).
Denoting by

the first common descendant of

and

, there is no positive vertex in the path between

and

in
T(A^{\perp})
(resp.
T(A)
).

Proof 8.15
.

Let

be a vertex on the path between

and

in
T(A^{\perp})
.
By

,

is a
\invamp
-vertex.
We prove by contradiction that

can neither be a
\oplus
nor a
\otimes
-vertex.

Suppose

is a
\oplus
-vertex.
By

,

P^{\perp}
, and so does
a^{\perp}
through

: there is a jump edge
l^{\perp}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$j$}}}{\longrightarrow}}P^{\perp}
.
Applying

, the first common descendant of

and

, which is

, is a

Assume now

As there is a jump

, there exist linkings

Remark

Then the switching cycle

l\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$j$}}}{\longrightarrow}}W\rightarrow P\leftarrow p\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$b$}}}{\mathrel{\text{\textemdash}}}}q\rightarrow\ast\leftarrow q^{\perp}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$b^{\perp}$}}}{\mathrel{\text{\textemdash}}}}p^{\perp}\rightarrow P^{\perp}\rightarrow N^{\perp}\leftarrow l^{\perp}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$a^{\perp}$}}}{\mathrel{\text{\textemdash}}}}m^{\perp}\rightarrow\ast\leftarrow m\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$a$}}}{\mathrel{\text{\textemdash}}}}l

Theorem 58
.

Assuming
\overset{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
with

are bipartite ax-unique.

Proof 8.16
.

.

that

is not ax-unique: there exist a leaf

and two distinct leaves

and

and

.

\psi
the almost reduced composition of

over

, depicted on

.

, and we did not eliminate atomic cuts).
Using
54
, we have in
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}

\omega=l\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$a$}}}{\mathrel{\text{\textemdash}}}}l_{0}\rightarrow\ast\leftarrow l_{0}^{\perp}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$a^{\perp}$}}}{\mathrel{\text{\textemdash}}}}l^{\perp}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$b^{\perp}$}}}{\mathrel{\text{\textemdash}}}}l_{1}^{\perp}\rightarrow\ast\leftarrow l_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$b$}}}{\mathrel{\text{\textemdash}}}}l

8.4.
Non-ambiguous formulas

Once our study is restricted to bipartite
ax-unique
proof-nets, we can also restrict formulas further.

{defi}

[Non-ambiguous formula]

A formula
A
is said
non-ambiguous
if each atom in
A
occurs at most once positive and once negative.

Remark 59
.

This means all leaves in
A
are distinct.
If
A
is non-ambiguous, so is
A^{\perp}
.

For instance,
X\with X^{\perp}
is non-ambiguous, whereas
(A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes C)
is ambiguous.
The reduction to non-ambiguous formulas requires to restrict to distributed formulas first: in
(A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes C)\mathrel{\simeq}A\otimes(B\oplus C)
we need the two occurrences of
A
to factorize.
The goal of this section is to prove that we can consider only non-ambiguous formulas (
62
) and that isomorphisms for these formulas correspond simply to the existence of bipartite proof-nets (
68
).
These two results are a direct adaptation of the work on MLL by Balat & Di Cosmo (see Section 3 in
[
BDC99
]
).

8.4.1.
Reduction to non-ambiguous formulas

In the following, we will call
substitution
the usual operation
[A_{1}/X_{1},\dotsc,A_{n}/X_{n}]
of replacement of the atoms
X_{i}
of a formula by the formulas
A_{i}
.
In our case, the
A_{i}
will always be atoms or negated atoms,
i.e.
leaves.
We will consider substitutions extended to proof-nets,
i.e.
if
\sigma
is a substitution and
\theta
a proof-net,
\sigma(\theta)
will be the proof-net obtained from
\theta
by relabeling all leaves
X_{i}
appearing in it by
\sigma(X_{i})
.
We also use a more general notion,
renaming
, that may replace
different occurrences
of the
same
atom by different atoms or negated atoms in a proof-net,
i.e.
substitute on leaves instead of atoms.

{defi}

[Renaming]

An application
\alpha
from the set of
leaves
of a proof-net
\theta
to a set of atoms and negated atoms is a
renaming
if
\alpha(\theta)
, the graph obtained by substitution of each label of a leaf
l
of
\theta
by
\alpha(l)
, is a proof-net.

Remark that if
\theta
is
bipartite ax-unique
, then the definition of
\alpha
only on leaves in
one conclusion
of
\theta
is sufficient to uniquely define a renaming
\alpha
on
\theta
.
This is because every leaf of the other conclusion is linked to exactly one leaf in this conclusion, and no leaves in a given conclusion are linked together.
It then suffices to define
\alpha(Y)
as
\alpha(X)^{\perp}
for
Y
linked to
X
, with
X
in the conclusion where
\alpha
is defined.
Note also that if the conclusions of
\theta
are ambiguous formulas, then two different occurrences of the same atom can be renamed differently, unlike what happens in the case of substitutions.

Theorem 60

(Renaming preserves distributed isomorphisms)
.

For
A
and
B
distributed formulas, assume
\overset{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
.
If
\alpha
is a renaming of the leaves of
\theta
, then there exists
\alpha^{\prime}
, a renaming of the leaves of
\theta^{\prime}
, such that
\overset{\alpha(\theta),\alpha^{\prime}(\theta^{\prime})}{\alpha^{\prime}(A)\mathrel{\simeq}\alpha(B)}
; more precisely:

•

\alpha^{\prime}(\theta^{\prime})
is a proof-net

•

\alpha(A^{\perp})=\alpha^{\prime}(A)^{\perp}
and
\alpha^{\prime}(B^{\perp})=\alpha(B)^{\perp}

•

the composition of
\alpha(\theta)
and
\alpha^{\prime}(\theta^{\prime})
by cut over
\alpha(B)
(resp.
\alpha^{\prime}(A)
) gives the identity proof-net of
\alpha^{\prime}(A)
(resp.
\alpha(B)
).

Proof 8.17
.

We first define
\alpha^{\prime}
.
By
58
,
\theta^{\prime}
is bipartite ax-unique, so it is sufficient to define
\alpha^{\prime}
only on the occurrences of
B^{\perp}
,
i.e.
to define
\alpha^{\prime}(B^{\perp})
. We set
\alpha^{\prime}(B^{\perp})=\alpha(B)^{\perp}
.
Then the composition of
\alpha(\theta)
and
\alpha^{\prime}(\theta^{\prime})
by cut over
\alpha(B)
is a proof-net.
Since cut-elimination does not depend on labels, this composition reduces to a proof-net with the same structure as an identity proof-net, but with conclusions
\alpha(A^{\perp}),\alpha^{\prime}(A)
.
An induction, on the size of
\alpha(A^{\perp})
, shows that this is the identity proof-net of
\alpha(A^{\perp})
.
Thus
\alpha(A^{\perp})=\alpha^{\prime}(A)^{\perp}
.
But then, the composition of
\alpha^{\prime}(\theta^{\prime})
and
\alpha(\theta)
by cut over
\alpha^{\prime}(A)
is well-defined and reduces, since cut-elimination does not depend on labels, to the identity proof-net of
\alpha(B)
.
Hence,
\overset{\alpha(\theta),\alpha^{\prime}(\theta^{\prime})}{\alpha^{\prime}(A)\mathrel{\simeq}\alpha(B)}
.

Lemma 61

(Distributed ambiguous isomorphic formulas)
.

Let
A
and
B
be distributed formulas, such that
\overset{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
.
There exists a substitution
\sigma
and distributed formulas
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
,
non-ambiguous
, such that
A=\sigma(A^{\prime})
,
B=\sigma(B^{\prime})
and
\overset{\psi,\psi^{\prime}}{A^{\prime}\mathrel{\simeq}B^{\prime}}
for some proof-nets
\psi
and
\psi^{\prime}
.

Proof 8.18
.

The proof-nets
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
are bipartite ax-unique (
58
), with conclusions
A^{\perp},B
and
B^{\perp},A
respectively.
One can define a renaming
\alpha
of
\theta
such that
\alpha(B)
has distinct positive leaves (
i.e.
no leaf of
\alpha(B)
occurs twice in
\alpha(B)
, and all are positive atoms), for it is sufficient to define
\alpha
only on leaves of
B
.
In particular,
\alpha(B)
is non-ambiguous.
Furthermore,
\alpha(\theta)
being bipartite ax-unique,
\alpha(A^{\perp})
has for leaves exactly the duals of the leaves of
\alpha(B)
.
Whence,
\alpha(A^{\perp})
is non-ambiguous too (with distinct negative leaves).

Now,
60
gives an algorithm for defining a renaming
\alpha^{\prime}
such that
\overset{\alpha(\theta),\alpha^{\prime}(\theta^{\prime})}{\alpha^{\prime}(A)\mathrel{\simeq}\alpha(B)}
, with in particular
\alpha(A^{\perp})=\alpha^{\prime}(A)^{\perp}
and
\alpha^{\prime}(B^{\perp})=\alpha(B)^{\perp}
.
Pose
A^{\prime}\coloneqq\alpha^{\prime}(A)
and
B^{\prime}\coloneqq\alpha(B)
, hence
\overset{\alpha(\theta),\alpha^{\prime}(\theta^{\prime})}{A^{\prime}\mathrel{\simeq}B^{\prime}}
and
\alpha(\theta)
has for conclusions
{A^{\prime}}^{\perp},B^{\prime}
.
Formulas
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
are distributed, as renaming acts only on leaves, and non-ambiguous (for
\alpha(A^{\perp})
and
\alpha(B)
are non-ambiguous).

On
\alpha(\theta)
one can define a renaming
\alpha^{-1}
such that
\alpha^{-1}(B^{\prime})=B
, again as
\alpha(\theta)
is bipartite ax-unique, so it is equivalent to define
\alpha^{-1}
on
\alpha(\theta)
or only on leaves of
B^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
\theta=\alpha^{-1}(\alpha(\theta))
and then
\alpha^{-1}({A^{\prime}}^{\perp})=A^{\perp}
follow from
\alpha^{-1}(B^{\prime})=B
.

Recall that all leaves of
B^{\prime}
are distinct and positive.
Hence, the definition of
\alpha^{-1}
on
B^{\prime}
can be seen as a substitution
\sigma
on atoms of
B^{\prime}
, with
\sigma(X)=\alpha^{-1}(l(X))
where
l(X)
is the unique leaf of
B^{\prime}
with label
X
.
Thus,
\theta=\alpha^{-1}(\alpha(\theta))=\sigma(\alpha(\theta))
: in particular,
\sigma(B^{\prime})=B
and
\sigma({A^{\prime}}^{\perp})=A^{\perp}
, so
\sigma(A^{\prime})=A
.
Finally,
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
are distributed non-ambiguous formulas such that
\overset{\alpha(\theta),\alpha^{\prime}(\theta^{\prime})}{A^{\prime}\mathrel{\simeq}B^{\prime}}
,
A=\sigma(A^{\prime})
and
B=\sigma(B^{\prime})
.

Corollary 62

(Reduction to distributed non-ambiguous formulas)
.

The set of couples of distributed formulas
A
and
B
such that
\overset{\theta,\psi}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
is the set of instances (by a substitution on atoms) of couples of distributed
non-ambiguous
formulas
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
such that
\overset{\theta^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}}{A^{\prime}\mathrel{\simeq}B^{\prime}}
.

Proof 8.19
.

We show each inclusion separately.
Let
A
and
B
be two distributed formulas such that
\overset{\theta,\psi}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
.
Then
A
and
B
are instances of two non-ambiguous distributed formulas
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
such that
\overset{\theta^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}}{A^{\prime}\mathrel{\simeq}B^{\prime}}
by
61
.

Conversely, let
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
be distributed non-ambiguous formulas such that
\overset{\theta^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}}{A^{\prime}\mathrel{\simeq}B^{\prime}}
,
\theta^{\prime}
and
\psi^{\prime}
being bipartite ax-unique proof-nets (
58
).
Take
\sigma
a substitution on atoms of
A^{\prime}
(so also on atoms of
B^{\prime}
).
The substitution
\sigma
defines on
\theta^{\prime}
a renaming
\alpha
for any substitution can be seen as a renaming.
Let
\alpha^{\prime}
be the renaming defined on
\psi^{\prime}
, associated to
\alpha
in
60
.
Since
\sigma({A^{\prime}}^{\perp})=\alpha({A^{\prime}}^{\perp})=(\alpha^{\prime}(A^{\prime}))^{\perp}
,
\alpha^{\prime}
is also the renaming induced by
\sigma
on
\psi^{\prime}
.
As
\overset{\alpha(\theta^{\prime}),\alpha^{\prime}(\psi^{\prime})}{\alpha^{\prime}(A^{\prime})\mathrel{\simeq}\alpha(B^{\prime})}
by
60
, it follows
\overset{\sigma(\theta^{\prime}),\sigma(\psi^{\prime})}{\sigma(A^{\prime})\mathrel{\simeq}\sigma(B^{\prime})}
.

8.4.2.
Simplification with non-ambiguous formulas

The goal of this part is to show isomorphisms between non-ambiguous formulas correspond simply to the existence of bipartite proof-nets, speaking no more about cut-elimination nor identity proof-nets.
This is done by proving properties close to the ones of identity proof-nets from
Section 8.1
.

Lemma 63
.

Let
\theta
be a bipartite proof-net of conclusions
A^{\perp},A
, with
A
a non-ambiguous formula.
Axiom links of
\theta
are of the form
(l^{\perp},l)
for
l
a leaf of
A
.

Proof 8.20
.

Let
a
be an axiom link of
\theta
.
By bipartiteness, it uses a leaf
l
of
A
and a leaf
m
of
A^{\perp}
.
Denote by
X
the label of
l
, whence the label of
m
is
X^{\perp}
.
However, the only leaf of
A^{\perp}
with label
X^{\perp}
is
l^{\perp}
, because
A^{\perp}
is non-ambiguous.
Thus,
m=l^{\perp}
and
a=(l,l^{\perp})
.

Lemma 64
.

Let
\theta
be a bipartite proof-net of conclusions
A^{\perp},A
, with
A
a non-ambiguous formula.
Take a linking
\lambda\in\theta
and an additive vertex
V
in its additive resolution.
The vertex
V^{\perp}
is in the additive resolution of
\lambda
, and
\lambda
keeps for
V^{\perp}
the dual premise it keeps for
V
.

Proof 8.21
.

As
V
is in the additive resolution
(A^{\perp},A)\upharpoonright\lambda
of
\lambda
, one of its ancestor leaves, say
l
, is in
(A^{\perp},A)\upharpoonright\lambda
: there is a link
a\in\lambda
on it.
By
63
,
a=(l,l^{\perp})
.
But
l^{\perp}
is an ancestor of
V^{\perp}
, so
V^{\perp}
is in
(A^{\perp},A)\upharpoonright\lambda
, with as premise the dual premise chosen for
V
.

Lemma 65
.

Let
A
be a non-ambiguous formula,
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
bipartite proof-nets of conclusions
A^{\perp},A
.
Then
\theta=\theta^{\prime}
.

Proof 8.22
.

Take
\lambda\in\theta
a linking.
It is on some
\with
-resolution
R
of
A^{\perp},A
.
By (P1), there exists a unique linking
\lambda^{\prime}\in\theta^{\prime}
on
R
.
We have to prove
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}
.
They have the same additive resolution, for their choice on a
\oplus
-vertex
P
is determined by the premise taken for the
\with
-vertex
P^{\perp}
, which is in
R
(
64
).
They have the same axiom links on this additive resolution, because any leaf on it is linked to its dual (
63
).
Therefore,
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}
, so
\theta\subseteq\theta^{\prime}
.
By symmetry, the same reasoning yields
\theta^{\prime}\subseteq\theta
, thus
\theta=\theta^{\prime}
.

Corollary 66
.

Let
A
be a non-ambiguous formula.
There is exactly one bipartite proof-net of conclusions
A^{\perp},A
: the identity proof-net of
A
.

Proof 8.23
.

This follows from
43
and
65
.

Remark 67
.

This property does not hold outside of non-ambiguous formulas, even distributed. For instance, there are two bipartite ax-unique proof-nets of conclusions
X_{1}^{\perp}\otimes X_{2}^{\perp},X_{3}\invamp X_{4}
(where each

is an occurrence of the atom

(X_{1}^{\perp},X_{4})
and
(X_{2}^{\perp},X_{3})
, and the “swap” with axiom links
(X_{1}^{\perp},X_{3})
and

(see

Theorem 68

(Bipartite proof-nets for non-ambiguous formulas)
.

Let
A
and
B
be non-ambiguous formulas.
If there exist bipartite proof-nets
\theta
and
\psi
of respective conclusions
A^{\perp},B
and
B^{\perp},A
, then
\overset{\theta,\psi}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
.

Proof 8.24
.

We prove both compositions reduce to identity proof-nets.
By symmetry, consider the composition over
B
.
Using
41
, it reduces to a bipartite proof-net, of conclusions
A^{\perp},A
.
By
66
, this can only be the identity proof-net of
A
.

8.5.
Completeness for unit-free MALL

We now prove the completeness of
{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}
for unit-free MALL by reasoning as in Section 4 of
[
BDC99
]
, with some more technicalities for we have to reorder not only
\invamp
-vertices but also
\with
-vertices.

{defi}

[Sequentializing vertex]

A terminal (
i.e.
with no descendant) non-leaf vertex
V
in a proof-net
\theta
is called
sequentializing
if, depending on its kind:

•

\otimes\backslash\ast
-vertex: the removal of
V
in
\mathcal{G}_{\theta}
has two connected components.

•

\oplus
-vertex: the left or right syntactic sub-tree of
V
does not belong to
\mathcal{G}_{\theta}
(
i.e.
has no link on any of its leaves in
\mathcal{G}_{\theta}
).

•

\invamp\backslash\with
-vertex: a terminal
\invamp\backslash\with
-vertex is always sequentializing.

It is easy to check that removing a sequentializing vertex produces proof-net(s).
The sequentialization theorem (
33
) affirms there exists a sequentializing vertex in a proof-net.

Lemma 69
.

In a bipartite full proof-net with conclusions
A_{l}\circledcirc A_{r},B
, where
\circledcirc\in\{\otimes;\oplus\}
, the root of
A_{l}\circledcirc A_{r}
is not sequentializing.

Proof 8.25
.

Let
l
be a leaf of
A_{l}
and
r
one of
A_{r}
.

and

of

with axiom links
(l,m)
and
(r,s)
in the proof-net (see
Figure 14
).
As there is a path in
T(B)
between
m
and
s
, whether
\circledcirc=\oplus
or
\circledcirc=\otimes
, it is not sequentializing.

Lemma 70

(Reordering
\invamp
-vertices)
.

\theta
be a bipartite ax-unique proof-net of conclusions

and

and
A
a distributed formula.
Then

and there exist two bipartite ax-unique proof-nets of respective conclusions
A_{l}^{\prime},B_{l}
and
A_{r}^{\prime},B_{r}
where
A_{l}^{\prime}\invamp A_{r}^{\prime}
is equal to

up to associativity and commutativity of
\invamp
.

Proof 8.26
.

We remove all terminal (hence sequentializing)

, without modifying the linkings.
The resulting graph is a proof-net of conclusions

The roots of the new trees

These

B_{l}\odot B_{r}
(reasoning as in the proof of

).
Thus, the sequentializing vertex of this proof-net is necessarily
B_{l}\odot B_{r}
.
It follows
\odot=\otimes
, because all leaves of
B
are connected to leaves in
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
, so if
\odot=\oplus
then
B_{l}\odot B_{r}
cannot be sequentializing.
Removing the sequentializing
B_{l}\otimes B_{r}
gives two proof-nets, with a partition of the
A_{i}

B_{l}
and the others linked to leaves of
B_{r}
.
We recover from these proof-nets bipartite ax-unique ones by adding

in an arbitrary order, yielding formulas

(with those linked to

) and
A_{r}^{\prime}
(with those linked to
B_{r}
).
As we only removed and put back
\invamp
-vertices,
A_{l}^{\prime}\invamp A_{r}^{\prime}
is equal to
A_{l}\invamp A_{r}
up to associativity and commutativity of
\invamp
.
