Lemma 71

(Reordering
\with
-vertices)
.

Let
\theta
be a bipartite ax-unique proof-net of conclusions
A=A_{l}\with A_{r}
and
B=B_{l}\oplus B_{r}
with
A
a distributed formula.
Then there exist two bipartite ax-unique proof-nets of respective conclusions
A_{l}^{\prime},B_{l}
and
A_{r}^{\prime},B_{r}
where
A_{l}^{\prime}\with A_{r}^{\prime}
is equal to
A_{l}\with A_{r}
up to associativity and commutativity of
\with
.

Proof 8.27
.

We remove all terminal
\with
-vertices in the proof-net, then all terminal
\invamp
-vertices, all in
A
.
The resulting graphs are proof-nets
\theta_{i}
(for terminal negative vertices are sequentializing), of conclusions
A_{1}^{i},\dots,A_{n_{i}}^{i},B_{l}\oplus B_{r}
for the
i
-th proof-net.
An illustration is
Figure 15
, except we have several of these proof-nets, having in common exactly
T(B)
.
As in the proof of
70
, the roots of the new trees
A_{j}^{i}
cannot be negative vertices because the formula
A
is distributed: so they are
\otimes\backslash\oplus
-vertices or atoms.
These
\otimes\backslash\oplus
-vertices cannot be sequentializing, since by bipartiteness and fullness every leaf of
A_{j}^{i}
is connected to the formula
B_{l}\oplus B_{r}
(reasoning as in the proof of
69
).
Thus, the sequentializing vertex of these proof-nets is necessarily
B_{l}\oplus B_{r}
, we can remove it: for a given
i
, all
A_{j}^{i}
are linked only to either
B_{l}
or
B_{r}
.
We put back the
\invamp
-vertices we removed, in the very same order.
We then put back the
\with
-vertices we removed, but in another order: we put together all
\theta_{i}
linked to
B_{l}
, and all those to
B_{r}
, yielding two proof-nets of conclusions
B_{l},A_{l}^{\prime}
and
B_{r},A_{r}^{\prime}
.
These proof-nets are bipartite ax-unique ones (because adding and removing
\invamp
does not modify the linkings, and
\with
is disjoint union of linkings).
We indeed have
A_{l}^{\prime}\with A_{r}^{\prime}
equal to
A_{l}\with A_{r}
up to associativity and commutativity of
\with
, because we only reordered
\with
-vertices.

We conclude by induction on the size
s(A)
of
A
, which we recall is its number of connectives, and is thus unaffected by commutation and associativity of connectives.

Theorem 72

(Isomorphisms completeness for unit-free MALL)
.

Given
A
and
B
two unit-free MALL formulas, if
\overset{\theta,\psi}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
for some proof-nets
\theta
and
\psi
, then
A=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}B
.

Proof 8.28
.

We assume
A
and
B
to be distributed and non-ambiguous formulas by
16
and
62
.
We reason by induction on the size of
A
,
s(A)
.
4
4

4

Remark that
s(A)=s(B)
, because
\theta
is bipartite
ax
-unique (
58
), thence
A
and
B
have the same number of atoms, so of connectives as they are all binary ones.

If
A
and
B
are atoms (
i.e.
of null size), then the axiom link in
\theta
between
A^{\perp}
and
B
yields
A=B
and the property holds.
Otherwise,
A^{\perp}
and
B
are
both
non atomic.
By
58
,
\theta
and
\psi
are bipartite ax-unique; they have respective conclusions
A^{\perp},B
and
B^{\perp},A
.
By
69
, one of the formulas
A^{\perp},B
is negative, otherwise neither the root of
A^{\perp}
nor
B
is sequentializing in
\theta
, contradicting sequentialization (
33
).
A symmetric reasoning on
\psi
implies that the other formula is positive.
Assume
w.l.o.g.
that
B=B_{0}\odot B_{1}
is positive (
i.e.

\odot\in\{\otimes;\oplus\}
) and
A^{\perp}
negative.
We distinguish cases according to the kind of the roots of
A^{\perp}
and
B
, considering the proof-net
\theta
.
If
B
a
\otimes
-formula and
A
a
\with
-formula, we instead consider
\psi
of conclusions
B^{\perp},A
, where
A
is a
\oplus
-formula and
B^{\perp}
a
\invamp
-formula.
Whence, either
A^{\perp}
is a
\invamp
-formula, or
B
and
A^{\perp}
are respectively a
\oplus
-formula and a
\with
-formula.

In the first (resp. second) case, by
70
(resp.
71
)
\odot=\otimes
(in the first case only) and there exist two bipartite ax-unique proof-nets
\theta_{0}
and
\theta_{1}
of respective conclusions
{A_{0}^{\prime}}^{\perp},B_{0}
and
{A_{1}^{\prime}}^{\perp},B_{1}
, with
{A^{\prime}}^{\perp}={A_{1}^{\prime}}^{\perp}\invamp{A_{0}^{\prime}}^{\perp}
(resp.
{A^{\prime}}^{\perp}={A_{1}^{\prime}}^{\perp}\with{A_{0}^{\prime}}^{\perp}
) equal to
A^{\perp}
up to associativity and commutativity of
\invamp
(resp.
\with
).
In particular,
{A^{\prime}}^{\perp}=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}A^{\perp}
, and
s(A_{0}^{\prime})
and
s(A_{1}^{\prime})
are both less than
s(A)
.
To conclude, we only need bipartite proof-nets
\psi_{0}
and
\psi_{1}
of respective conclusions
B_{0}^{\perp},A_{0}^{\prime}
and
B_{1}^{\perp},A_{1}^{\prime}
.
We will then apply
68
to obtain
\overset{\theta_{0},\psi_{0}}{A_{0}^{\prime}\mathrel{\simeq}B_{0}}
and
\overset{\theta_{1},\psi_{1}}{A_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\simeq}B_{1}}
.
Thence, by induction hypothesis,
A_{0}^{\prime}=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}B_{0}
and
A_{1}^{\prime}=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}B_{1}
, thus
A^{\prime}=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}B
.
5
5

5

The formula
A^{\prime}
is distributed for it is equal up to associativity and commutativity to the distributed
A
. Whence,
A_{0}^{\prime}
and
A_{1}^{\prime}
are also distributed.

As
A=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}A^{\prime}
, we will finally conclude
A=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}B
.

Thus, we look for two bipartite proof-nets of respective conclusions
B_{0}^{\perp},A_{0}^{\prime}
and
B_{1}^{\perp},A_{1}^{\prime}
.
As
\overset{\theta,\psi}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
, and
A\mathrel{\simeq}A^{\prime}
by soundness of
{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}
(
5
), it follows using
39
that
\overset{\Theta,\Theta^{\prime}}{A^{\prime}\mathrel{\simeq}B}
for some proof-nets
\Theta
and
\Theta^{\prime}
.
6
6

6

One can easily check that isomorphisms in proof-nets form equivalence classes on formulas.

Furthermore,
\Theta
is a bipartite ax-unique proof-net (
58
) of conclusions
B^{\perp},A^{\prime}
,
i.e.
of conclusions
B_{1}^{\perp}\odot^{\perp}B_{0}^{\perp},A_{0}^{\prime}\circledcirc A_{1}^{\prime}
with
(\odot^{\perp},\circledcirc)\in\{(\invamp,\otimes);(\with,\oplus)\}
.
We had a bipartite ax-unique proof-net
\theta_{0}
of conclusions
{A_{0}^{\prime}}^{\perp},B_{0}
, therefore the atoms or negated atoms of
B_{0}
are exactly those of
A_{0}^{\prime}
.
Similarly, the atoms and negated atoms of
B_{1}
are exactly those of
A_{1}^{\prime}
.
Whence, no atom nor negated atom of
B_{0}
(resp.
B_{1}
) is one of
A_{1}^{\prime}
(resp.
A_{0}^{\prime}
), for otherwise an atom or negated atom of
A_{0}
(resp.
A_{1}
) also occurs in
A_{1}^{\prime}
(resp
A_{0}^{\prime}
), contradicting non-ambiguousness of
A^{\prime}
.
This implies that axiom links in
\Theta
must be between leaves of
B_{0}^{\perp}
and
A_{0}^{\prime}
, and between leaves of
B_{1}^{\perp}
and
A_{1}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, once we sequentialize the negative root
\odot^{\perp}
of
B^{\perp}
in
\Theta
, the positive root
\circledcirc
of
A^{\prime}
is sequentializing.
After sequentializing both, we obtain two bipartite ax-unique proof-nets, of respective conclusions
B_{0}^{\perp},A_{0}^{\prime}
and
B_{1}^{\perp},A_{1}^{\prime}
.

The above theorem, associated with preceding results, yields our main contribution.

Theorem 73

(Isomorphisms completeness)
.

{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}
is complete for unit-free MALL:

Given
A
and
B
two unit-free MALL formulas, if
A\mathrel{\simeq}B
, then
A=_{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}B
.

\mathcal{L}
is complete for MALL:

Given
A
and
B
two MALL formulas, if
A\mathrel{\simeq}B
, then
A=_{\mathcal{L}}B
.

Proof 8.29
.

For
A
and
B
unit-free formulas such that
A\mathrel{\simeq}B
, using
39
there exist proof-nets
\theta
and
\psi
such that
\overset{\theta,\psi}{A\mathrel{\simeq}B}
.
The first point then follows by
72
.

The second point results from the first one and
29
.

9.
Star-autonomous categories with finite products

Since MALL semantically corresponds to
\star
-autonomous categories with finite products (which also have finite coproducts), we can use our results on MALL to characterize isomorphisms valid in all such categories.
For the historical result of how linear logic can be seen as a category, see
[
See89
]
.

We consider objects of
\star
-autonomous categories described by formulas in the language:

F\coloneqq X\mid F\otimes F\mid 1\mid F\multimap F\mid\bot\mid F\with F\mid\top

There is some redundancy here since one could define
1
as
\bot\multimap\bot
in any
\star
-autonomous category, but we prefer to keep
1
in the language as it is at the core of monoidal categories.
Our goal is to prove the theory
\mathcal{D}
of
Table 7
to be sound and complete for the isomorphisms of
\star
-autonomous categories with finite products.

\mathcal{D}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\quad}rcl@{\quad}rcl}F\otimes(G\otimes H)&=&(F\otimes G)\otimes H&F\otimes G&=&G\otimes F&F\otimes 1&=&F\\
(F\otimes G)\multimap H&=&F\multimap(G\multimap H)&&&&1\multimap F&=&F\\
\\
F\with(G\with H)&=&(F\with G)\with H&F\with G&=&G\with F&F\with\top&=&F\\
F\multimap(G\with H)&=&(F\multimap G)\with(F\multimap H)&&&&F\multimap\top&=&\top\end{array}\right\}\mathcal{S}\\
\\
(F\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot=F\end{array}\right.

Table 7.
Type isomorphisms in
\star
-autonomous categories with finite products

We establish this result from the one on MALL, first proving that MALL (with proofs considered up to
\beta\eta
-equality) defines a
\star
-autonomous category with finite products (
Section 9.1
).
Then, we conclude using a semantic method based on this syntactic category (
Section 9.2
).
In a third step we look at the more general case of symmetric monoidal closed categories – without the requirement of a dualizing object (
Section 9.3
).

9.1.
MALL as a star-autonomous category with finite products

The logic MALL, with proofs taken up to
\beta\eta
-equality, defines a
\star
-autonomous category with finite products, that we will call
\mathbb{MALL}
.
Indeed, we can define it as follows.

Objects of
\mathbb{MALL}
are formulas of MALL, while its morphisms from
A
to
B
are proofs of
\vdash A^{\perp},B
, considered up to
\beta\eta
-equality.
7
7

7

We recall that
(\cdot)^{\perp}
is defined by induction, making it an involution.

One can check that a proof of MALL is an isomorphism if and only if, when seen as a morphism, it is an isomorphism in
\mathbb{MALL}
.

We define a bifunctor
\otimes
on
\mathbb{MALL}
, associating to formulas (
i.e.
objects)
A
and
B
the formula
A\otimes B
and to proofs (
i.e.
morphisms)
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
respectively of
\vdash A_{0}^{\perp},B_{0}
and
\vdash A_{1}^{\perp},B_{1}
the following proof of
\vdash(A_{0}\otimes A_{1})^{\perp},B_{0}\otimes B_{1}
:

\pi_{0}

\vdash

A_{0}^{\perp},B_{0}

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1}^{\perp},B_{1}

\otimes

\vdash

A_{1}^{\perp},A_{0}^{\perp},B_{0}\otimes B_{1}

\invamp

\vdash

A_{1}^{\perp}\invamp A_{0}^{\perp},B_{0}\otimes B_{1}

One can check that
(\mathbb{MALL},\otimes,1,\alpha,\lambda,\rho,\gamma)
forms a symmetric monoidal category, where
1
is the
1
-formula,
\alpha
are isomorphisms of MALL associated to
(A\otimes B)\otimes C\mathrel{\simeq}A\otimes(B\otimes C)
seen as natural isomorphisms of
\mathbb{MALL}
, and similarly for
\lambda
with
1\otimes A\mathrel{\simeq}A
,
\rho
with
A\otimes 1\mathrel{\simeq}A
, and
\gamma
with
A\otimes B\mathrel{\simeq}B\otimes A
.

Furthermore, define
A\multimap B\coloneqq A^{\perp}\invamp B
and
ev_{A,B}
as the following morphism from
A\otimes(A\multimap B)
to
B
(
i.e.
a proof of
\vdash(B^{\perp}\otimes A)\invamp A^{\perp},B
):

ax

\vdash

B
{}^{\perp},
B

ax

\vdash

A
{}^{\perp},
A

\otimes

\vdash

B^{\perp}\otimes A,A^{\perp},B

\invamp

\vdash

(B^{\perp}\otimes A)\invamp A^{\perp},B

It can be checked that
\mathbb{MALL}
is a symmetric monoidal closed category with as exponential object
(A\multimap B,ev_{A,B})
for objects
A
and
B
.

Moreover, one can also check that
\bot
is a dualizing object for this category, making
\mathbb{MALL}
a
\star
-autonomous category.
This relies on the following morphism from
(A\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot
to
A
(which is an inverse of the currying of
ev_{A,\bot}
):

1

\vdash 1

ax

\vdash

A
{}^{\perp},
A

\bot

\vdash

A^{\perp},\bot,A

\invamp

\vdash

A^{\perp}\invamp\bot,A

\otimes

\vdash

1\otimes(A^{\perp}\invamp\bot),A

Finally,
\top
is a terminal object of
\mathbb{MALL}
, and
A\with B
is the product of objects
A
and
B
, with, as projections
\pi_{A}
and
\pi_{B}
, the following morphisms respectively from
A\with B
to
A
and from
A\with B
to
B
:

ax

\vdash

A
{}^{\perp},
A

\oplus_{2}

\vdash

B^{\perp}\oplus A^{\perp},A

and

ax

\vdash

B
{}^{\perp},
B

\oplus_{1}

\vdash

B^{\perp}\oplus A^{\perp},B

Therefore,
\mathbb{MALL}
is a
\star
-autonomous category with finite products
[
See89
]
.

9.2.
Isomorphisms of star-autonomous categories with finite products

We translate formulas in the language of
\star
-autonomous categories with finite products into MALL formulas and conversely by means of the following translations:

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\qquad\quad}rcl}\ell(X)&=&X&\partial(X)&=&X\\
&&&\partial(X^{\perp})&=&X\multimap\bot\\
\ell(F\otimes G)&=&\ell(F)\otimes\ell(G)&\partial(A\otimes B)&=&\partial(A)\otimes\partial(B)\\
\ell(1)&=&1&\partial(1)&=&1\\
\ell(F\multimap G)&=&\ell(F)^{\perp}\invamp\ell(G)&\partial(A\invamp B)&=&((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(B)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot\\
\ell(\bot)&=&\bot&\partial(\bot)&=&\bot\\
\ell(F\with G)&=&\ell(F)\with\ell(G)&\partial(A\with B)&=&\partial(A)\with\partial(B)\\
\ell(\top)&=&\top&\partial(\top)&=&\top\\
&&&\partial(A\oplus B)&=&((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\with(\partial(B)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot\\
&&&\partial(0)&=&\top\multimap\bot\end{array}

The translation
\ell(\_)
corresponds exactly to the interpretation of the constructions on objects of a
\star
-autonomous categories with finite products in the concrete category
\mathbb{MALL}
.

Lemma 74
.

The
\partial(\_)
and
\ell(\_)
translations satisfy the following properties:

•

\partial(A^{\perp})=_{\mathcal{D}}\partial(A)\multimap\bot

•

\partial(\ell(F))=_{\mathcal{D}}F

•

A=_{\mathcal{L}}B
entails
\partial(A)=_{\mathcal{D}}\partial(B)

Proof 9.1
.

The second property relies on the first while the third is independent.

•

By induction on
A
:

\displaystyle\partial(X^{\perp})

\displaystyle=X\multimap\bot=_{\mathcal{D}}\partial(X)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle\partial(X^{\perp\perp})

\displaystyle=\partial(X)=X=_{\mathcal{D}}(X\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot=\partial(X^{\perp})\multimap\bot

\displaystyle\partial((A\otimes B)^{\perp})

\displaystyle=((\partial(B^{\perp})\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(A^{\perp})\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot)\otimes((\partial(B)\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\otimes\partial(B))\multimap\bot=\partial(A\otimes B)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle\partial(1^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\bot=_{\mathcal{D}}1\multimap\bot=\partial(1)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle\partial((A\invamp B)^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\partial(B^{\perp})\otimes\partial(A^{\perp})=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(B)\multimap\bot)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(B)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=\partial(A\invamp B)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle\partial(\bot^{\perp})

\displaystyle=1=_{\mathcal{D}}(1\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot=_{\mathcal{D}}\bot\multimap\bot=\partial(\bot)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle\partial((A\with B)^{\perp})

\displaystyle=((\partial(B^{\perp})\multimap\bot)\with(\partial(A^{\perp})\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot)\with((\partial(B)\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\with\partial(B))\multimap\bot=\partial(A\with B)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle\partial(\top^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\top\multimap\bot=\partial(\top)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle\partial((A\oplus B)^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\partial(B^{\perp})\with\partial(A^{\perp})=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\with(\partial(B)\multimap\bot)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\with(\partial(B)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=\partial(A\oplus B)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle\partial(0^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\top=_{\mathcal{D}}(\top\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot=\partial(0)\multimap\bot

•

By induction on
F
, the key case being:

\displaystyle\partial(\ell(F\multimap G))

\displaystyle=\partial(\ell(F)^{\perp}\invamp\ell(G))=((\partial(\ell(F)^{\perp})\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(\ell(G))\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(((F\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot)\otimes(G\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot=_{\mathcal{D}}(F\otimes(G\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}F\multimap((G\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot)=_{\mathcal{D}}F\multimap G

•

We prove that the image of each equation of
Table 1
through
\partial(\_)
is derivable with equations of
Table 7
.
Commutativity, associativity and unitality for
\otimes
and
\with
are immediate.

\displaystyle\partial(A\invamp B)

\displaystyle=((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(B)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}((\partial(B)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(A)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot=\partial(B\invamp A)

\displaystyle\partial(A\invamp(B\invamp C))

\displaystyle=((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes((((\partial(B)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(C)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes((\partial(B)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(C)\multimap\bot)))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(B)\multimap\bot))\otimes(\partial(C)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(((((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(B)\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot))\otimes(\partial(C)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=\partial((A\invamp B)\invamp C)

\displaystyle\partial(A\invamp\bot)

\displaystyle=((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\bot\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes((1\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot=_{\mathcal{D}}((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes 1)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot=_{\mathcal{D}}\partial(A)

Commutativity, associativity and unitality for
\oplus
follow the same pattern as for
\invamp
.

Then we have:

\displaystyle\partial(A\otimes(B\oplus C))

\displaystyle=\partial(A)\otimes(((\partial(B)\multimap\bot)\with(\partial(C)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}((\partial(A)\otimes(((\partial(B)\multimap\bot)\with(\partial(C)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\multimap(((\partial(B)\multimap\bot)\with(\partial(C)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\multimap((\partial(B)\multimap\bot)\with(\partial(C)\multimap\bot)))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}((\partial(A)\multimap(\partial(B)\multimap\bot))\with(\partial(A)\multimap(\partial(C)\multimap\bot)))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(((\partial(A)\otimes\partial(B))\multimap\bot)\with((\partial(A)\otimes\partial(C))\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=\partial((A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes C))

\displaystyle\partial(A\otimes 0)

\displaystyle=\partial(A)\otimes(\top\multimap\bot)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}((\partial(A)\otimes(\top\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\multimap((\top\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\multimap\top)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}\top\multimap\bot=\partial(0)

\displaystyle\partial(A\invamp(B\with C))

\displaystyle=((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes((\partial(B)\with\partial(C))\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\multimap(((\partial(B)\with\partial(C))\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\multimap(\partial(B)\with\partial(C))

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\multimap\partial(B))\with((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\multimap\partial(C))

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\multimap((\partial(B)\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot))\with((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\multimap((\partial(C)\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot))

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(B)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot)\with(((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\partial(C)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot)

\displaystyle=\partial((A\invamp B)\with(A\invamp C))

\displaystyle\partial(A\invamp\top)

\displaystyle=((\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\otimes(\top\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\multimap((\top\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\partial(A)\multimap\bot)\multimap\top=_{\mathcal{D}}\top=\partial(\top)

Theorem 75

(Isomorphisms in
\star
-autonomous categories with finite products)
.

The equational theory
\mathcal{D}
(
Table 7
) is sound and complete for isomorphisms in
\star
-autonomous categories with finite products.

Proof 9.2
.

Soundness follows by definition of
\star
-autonomous categories with finite products.
For completeness, take an isomorphism
F\mathrel{\simeq}G
.
It yields an isomorphism
\ell(F)\mathrel{\simeq}\ell(G)
in
\mathbb{MALL}
.
As
A\mathrel{\simeq}B
in
\mathbb{MALL}
is generated by
\mathcal{L}
(
73
), we get
\ell(F)=_{\mathcal{L}}\ell(G)
.
From
74
, we deduce
F=_{\mathcal{D}}\partial(\ell(F))=_{\mathcal{D}}\partial(\ell(G))=_{\mathcal{D}}G
.

9.3.
Isomorphisms of symmetric monoidal closed categories with finite products

Isomorphisms in symmetric monoidal closed categories (SMCC) have been characterized
[
DP97
]
and proved to correspond to equations in the first two lines of
Table 7
.

We want to extend this result to finite products by proving the soundness and completeness of the theory
\mathcal{S}
presented in
Table 7
.

Theorem 76

(Isomorphisms in SMCC with finite products)
.

The equational theory
\mathcal{S}
(
Table 7
) is sound and complete for isomorphisms in symmetric monoidal closed categories with finite products.

Proof 9.3
.

The language of SMCC with finite products is the language of
\star
-autonomous categories with finite products in which we remove
\bot
.
In particular the translation
\ell(\_)
can be used to translate the associated formulas into MALL formulas.
In order to analyse the image of this restricted translation, we consider the following grammar of MALL formulas (
output formulas

o
and
input formulas

\iota

[
Lam96
]
):

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccccccccc}o&\coloneqq&X&\mid&o\otimes o&\mid&o\invamp\iota&\mid&\iota\invamp o&\mid&1&\mid&o\with o&\mid&\top\\
\iota&\coloneqq&X^{\perp}&\mid&\iota\invamp\iota&\mid&\iota\otimes o&\mid&o\otimes\iota&\mid&\bot&\mid&\iota\oplus\iota&\mid&0\end{array}

The dual of an output formula is an input formula and conversely.
Moreover no MALL formula is both an input and an output formula – let us call this the
non-ambiguity property
.
One can check by induction on a formula
F
in the language of SMCC with finite products that
\ell(F)
is an output formula.
We define a translation back from output formulas to SMCC formulas (which is well defined thanks to the non-ambiguity property):

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\qquad}rcl}{X}^{\circ}&=&X\\
{(o\otimes o^{\prime})}^{\circ}&=&{o}^{\circ}\otimes{o^{\prime}}^{\circ}&{1}^{\circ}&=&1\\
{(o\with o^{\prime})}^{\circ}&=&{o}^{\circ}\with{o^{\prime}}^{\circ}&{\top}^{\circ}&=&\top\\
{(o\invamp\iota)}^{\circ}&=&{(\iota^{\perp})}^{\circ}\multimap{o}^{\circ}&{(\iota\invamp o)}^{\circ}&=&{(\iota^{\perp})}^{\circ}\multimap{o}^{\circ}\end{array}

We use the notation
{\iota}^{\bullet}={(\iota^{\perp})}^{\circ}
(so that
{(o^{\perp})}^{\bullet}={o}^{\circ}
).
We can check, by induction on
F
, that
{\ell(F)}^{\circ}=F
.
We now prove that when
o=_{\mathcal{L}}A
(resp.
\iota=_{\mathcal{L}}A
) is an equation from
Table 1
(or its symmetric version) then
A
is an output (resp. input) formula and
{o}^{\circ}=_{\mathcal{S}}{A}^{\circ}
(resp.
{\iota}^{\bullet}=_{\mathcal{S}}{A}^{\bullet}
):

•

If
o
or
\iota
is of the shape
A\otimes(B\otimes C)
, we have the following possibilities:

–

A
,
B
and
C
are output, then
(A\otimes B)\otimes C
is output and:

\displaystyle{(A\otimes(B\otimes C))}^{\circ}

\displaystyle={A}^{\circ}\otimes({B}^{\circ}\otimes{C}^{\circ})

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{S}}({A}^{\circ}\otimes{B}^{\circ})\otimes{C}^{\circ}={((A\otimes B)\otimes C)}^{\circ}

–

A
and
B
are output and
C
is input, then
(A\otimes B)\otimes C
is input and:

\displaystyle{(A\otimes(B\otimes C))}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle={A}^{\circ}\multimap({B}^{\circ}\multimap{C}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{S}}({A}^{\circ}\otimes{B}^{\circ})\multimap{C}^{\bullet}={((A\otimes B)\otimes C)}^{\bullet}

–

A
and
C
are output and
B
is input, then
(A\otimes B)\otimes C
is input and:

\displaystyle{(A\otimes(B\otimes C))}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle={A}^{\circ}\multimap({C}^{\circ}\multimap{B}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{S}}({A}^{\circ}\otimes{C}^{\circ})\multimap{B}^{\bullet}=_{\mathcal{S}}({C}^{\circ}\otimes{A}^{\circ})\multimap{B}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{S}}{C}^{\circ}\multimap({A}^{\circ}\multimap{B}^{\bullet})={((A\otimes B)\otimes C)}^{\bullet}

–

A
is input and
B
and
C
are output, then
(A\otimes B)\otimes C
is input and:

\displaystyle{(A\otimes(B\otimes C))}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle=({B}^{\circ}\otimes{C}^{\circ})\multimap{A}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{S}}({C}^{\circ}\otimes{B}^{\circ})\multimap{A}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{S}}{C}^{\circ}\multimap({B}^{\circ}\multimap{A}^{\bullet})={((A\otimes B)\otimes C)}^{\bullet}

The symmetric case follows the same pattern, as well as associativity of
\invamp
.

•

If
o
or
\iota
is of the shape
A\otimes B
, we have the following possibilities:

–

A
and
B
are output, then
B\otimes A
is output and
{(A\otimes B)}^{\circ}={A}^{\circ}\otimes{B}^{\circ}=_{\mathcal{S}}{B}^{\circ}\otimes{A}^{\circ}={(B\otimes A)}^{\circ}

–

A
is output and
B
is input, then
B\otimes A
is input and
{(A\otimes B)}^{\bullet}={A}^{\circ}\multimap{B}^{\bullet}={(B\otimes A)}^{\bullet}

–

A
is input and
B
is output, then
B\otimes A
is input and
{(A\otimes B)}^{\bullet}={B}^{\circ}\multimap{A}^{\bullet}={(B\otimes A)}^{\bullet}

The commutativity of
\invamp
follows the same pattern.

•

If
o
or
\iota
is of the shape
A\otimes 1
then either
A
is output and
{(A\otimes 1)}^{\circ}={A}^{\circ}\otimes 1=_{\mathcal{S}}{A}^{\circ}
,
or
A
is input and
{(A\otimes 1)}^{\bullet}=1\multimap{A}^{\bullet}=_{\mathcal{S}}{A}^{\bullet}
.
The symmetric case follows the same pattern, as well as unitality for
\invamp
.

•

If
o=A\with(B\with C)
then
A
,
B
and
C
are output and
(A\with B)\with C
as well.
We have
{(A\with(B\with C))}^{\circ}={A}^{\circ}\with({B}^{\circ}\with{C}^{\circ})=_{\mathcal{S}}({A}^{\circ}\with{B}^{\circ})\with{C}^{\circ}={((A\with B)\with C)}^{\circ}
.
The symmetric case follows the same pattern, as well as associativity of
\oplus
.

•

If
o=A\with B
then
A
and
B
are output and
B\with A
as well.
We have
{(A\with B)}^{\circ}={A}^{\circ}\with{B}^{\circ}=_{\mathcal{S}}{B}^{\circ}\with{A}^{\circ}={(B\with A)}^{\circ}
.
The commutativity of
\oplus
follows the same pattern.

•

If
o=A\with\top
then
A
is output and
{(A\with\top)}^{\circ}={A}^{\circ}\with\top=_{\mathcal{S}}{A}^{\circ}
.
The symmetric case follows the same pattern, as well as unitality for
\oplus
.

•

If
\iota=A\otimes(B\oplus C)
then
A
is output and
B
and
C
are input, and
(A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes C)
is input. We have:

\displaystyle{(A\otimes(B\oplus C))}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle={A}^{\circ}\multimap({C}^{\bullet}\with{B}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{S}}({A}^{\circ}\multimap{C}^{\bullet})\with({A}^{\circ}\multimap{B}^{\bullet})={((A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes C))}^{\bullet}

The symmetric case follows the same pattern, as well as distributivity of
\invamp
over
\with
.

•

If
\iota=A\otimes 0
then
A
is output and
{(A\otimes 0)}^{\bullet}={A}^{\circ}\multimap\top=_{\mathcal{S}}\top={0}^{\bullet}
.
The symmetric case follows the same pattern, as well as cancellation of
\invamp
by
\top
.

Assume now that
F\mathrel{\simeq}G
in the class of SMCC with finite products.
As
\mathbb{MALL}
is such a SMCC with finite products, we have
\ell(F)\mathrel{\simeq}\ell(G)
in
\mathbb{MALL}
, thus
\ell(F)=_{\mathcal{L}}\ell(G)
by
73
.
As
\ell(F)
is an output formula, by induction on the length of the equational derivation of
\ell(F)=_{\mathcal{L}}\ell(G)
, we get that all the intermediary steps involve output formulas and each equation is mapped to
=_{\mathcal{S}}
by
{(\_)}^{\circ}
so that
{\ell(F)}^{\circ}=_{\mathcal{S}}{\ell(G)}^{\circ}
, and finally
F=_{\mathcal{S}}{\ell(F)}^{\circ}=_{\mathcal{S}}{\ell(G)}^{\circ}=_{\mathcal{S}}G
.

Conversely soundness easily comes from the definition of SMCC and products.

In
\star
-autonomous categories with finite products, we automatically have finite coproducts given by
A\oplus B\coloneqq((A\multimap\bot)\with(B\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot
and
0\coloneqq\top\multimap\bot
.
From equations of
Table 7
, one can derive:

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}A\oplus B&=&B\oplus A\\
A\oplus(B\oplus C)&=&(A\oplus B)\oplus C\\
A\oplus 0&=&A\\
A\otimes(B\oplus C)&=&(A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes C)\\
A\otimes 0&=&0\\
(A\oplus B)\multimap C&=&(A\multimap C)\with(B\multimap C)\\
0\multimap C&=&\top\end{array}\qquad\right\}\mathcal{C}

In the weaker setting of SMCC, finite products do not induce finite coproducts.
It justifies the possibility of considering them separately.
The case of products only was
76
. We are now looking at both products and coproducts on one side, and coproducts only on the other side.

As a preliminary result, let us give a necessary condition for formulas to be isomorphic in this symmetric monoidal closed setting.

Lemma 77
.

If
A\mathrel{\simeq}B
in symmetric monoidal closed categories with finite products and coproducts with
A
and
B
distributed (
i.e.

\ell(A)
and
\ell(B)
are distributed) then there exist cut-free proofs of
A\vdash B
and
B\vdash A
in IMALL (intuitionistic multiplicative additive linear logic)
[
Bie95
]
whose left
0
rules introduce
0\vdash 0
sequents only and right
\top
rules introduce
\top\vdash\top
sequents only.

Proof 9.4
.

If
A
and
B
are isomorphic in SMCC with finite products and coproducts, the associated isomorphisms can be represented as IMALL proofs which we can assume to be cut-free. These proofs can be interpreted as MALL proofs (corresponding to the fact that
\mathbb{MALL}
is an SMCC with finite products and coproducts).
By
25
, these MALL proofs have their
\top
rules introducing
\vdash\top,0
sequents only which gives the required property on the IMALL proofs we started with.

We conjecture that isomorphisms in SMCC with both finite products
and
finite coproducts correspond to adding the equations of theory
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{S}
(
Table 7
).
However our approach through
\star
-autonomous categories does not work since for example
\top\multimap(\top\oplus\top)
and
(0\with 0)\multimap 0
are isomorphic in
\star
-autonomous categories but not in SMCC with finite products and coproducts:

\displaystyle\top\multimap(\top\oplus\top)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}\top\multimap(((\top\multimap\bot)\with(\top\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\top\otimes(0\with 0))\multimap\bot=_{\mathcal{D}}((0\with 0)\otimes\top)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(0\with 0)\multimap(\top\multimap\bot)=_{\mathcal{D}}(0\with 0)\multimap 0

or directly by interpreting these formulas into MALL, one gets:

\ell(\top\multimap(\top\oplus\top))=_{\mathcal{L}}0\invamp(\top\oplus\top)=_{\mathcal{L}}(\top\oplus\top)\invamp 0=_{\mathcal{L}}\ell((0\with 0)\multimap 0)

(the analogue of the non-ambiguity property does not hold).
This isomorphism however is not valid in the SMCC setting as shown by
77
since all cut-free proofs of
(0\with 0)\multimap 0\vdash\top\multimap(\top\oplus\top)
in IMALL have the following shape:

\top
R

(0\with 0)\multimap 0,\top\vdash\top

\oplus_{i}
R

(0\with 0)\multimap 0,\top\vdash\top\oplus\top

\multimap
R

(0\with 0)\multimap 0\vdash\top\multimap(\top\oplus\top)

One could also investigate SMCC with finite coproducts only (without products).
It is important to notice that an initial object
0
in a SMCC induces that
0\multimap A
is a terminal object for any
A
.
This first means that we cannot uncorelate completely products and coproducts.
It also means that the theory of isomorphisms includes the equation
0\multimap A\mathrel{\simeq}0\multimap B
even if it does not occur in
\mathcal{C}
(it might be the only missing equation).
Regarding a characterization through
\star
-autonomous categories, it is again not possible since, if we denote by
\top
a terminal object,
(\top\multimap 0)\multimap 0

and
\top\multimap(\top\otimes\top)

are isomorphic in
\star
-autonomous categories (using
0=_{\mathcal{D}}\top\multimap\bot
):

\displaystyle(\top\multimap 0)\multimap 0

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\top\multimap(\top\multimap\bot))\multimap(\top\multimap\bot)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}((\top\otimes\top)\multimap\bot)\multimap(\top\multimap\bot)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(((\top\otimes\top)\multimap\bot)\otimes\top)\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}(\top\otimes((\top\otimes\top)\multimap\bot))\multimap\bot

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{D}}\top\multimap(((\top\otimes\top)\multimap\bot)\multimap\bot)=_{\mathcal{D}}\top\multimap(\top\otimes\top)

(or
\ell((\top\multimap 0)\multimap 0)=_{\mathcal{L}}(\top\otimes\top)\invamp 0=_{\mathcal{L}}0\invamp(\top\otimes\top)=_{\mathcal{L}}\ell(\top\multimap(\top\otimes\top))
in MALL).
But they are not isomorphic in SMCC with initial and terminal objects by
77
since all cut-free proofs of
(\top\multimap 0)\multimap 0\vdash\top\multimap(\top\otimes\top)
in IMALL have the following shape (up to permuting the premises of the (
\otimes
R)-rule):

\top
R

(\top\multimap 0)\multimap 0\vdash\top

\top
R

\top\vdash\top

\otimes
R

(\top\multimap 0)\multimap 0,\top\vdash\top\otimes\top

\multimap
R

(\top\multimap 0)\multimap 0\vdash\top\multimap(\top\otimes\top)

or

\top
R

(\top\multimap 0)\multimap 0,\top\vdash\top

\top
R

\vdash\top

\otimes
R

(\top\multimap 0)\multimap 0,\top\vdash\top\otimes\top

\multimap
R

(\top\multimap 0)\multimap 0\vdash\top\multimap(\top\otimes\top)

10.
Conclusion

Extending the result of Balat and Di Cosmo in
[
BDC99
]
, we give an equational theory characterising type isomorphisms in multiplicative-additive linear logic with units as well as in
\star
-autonomous categories with finite products: the one described on
Table 1
on
\autopageref
tab:eqisos (and on
Table 7
for
\star
-autonomous categories).
Looking at the proof, we get as a sub-result that isomorphisms for additive linear logic (resp. unit-free additive linear logic) are given by the equational theory
\mathcal{L}
(resp.
{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}}
) restricted to additive formulas – and more generally this applies to any fragment of MALL, thanks to the sub-formula property.
Proof-nets were a major tool to prove completeness, as notions like fullness and
ax
-uniqueness are much harder to define and manipulate in sequent calculus.
However, we could not use them for taking care of the (additive) units, because there is no known appropriate notion of proof-nets.
We have thus been forced to develop (some parts of) the theory of cut-elimination, axiom-expansion and rule commutations for the sequent calculus of MALL with units.

The immediate question to address is the extension of our results to the characterization of type isomorphisms for full propositional linear logic, thus including the exponential connectives.
This is clearly not immediate since the interaction between additive and exponential connectives is not well described in proof-nets.

A more general problem is the study of type retractions (where only one of the two compositions yields an identity) which is also much more difficult (see for example
[
RU02
]
).
The question is mostly open in the case of linear logic.
Even in multiplicative linear logic (where there is for example a retraction between
A
and
(A\multimap A)\multimap A=(A\otimes A^{\perp})\invamp A
which is not an isomorphism, and where the associated proof-nets are not bipartite), no characterization is known.
In the multiplicative-additive fragment, the problem looks even harder, with more retractions; for instance the one depicted on
Figure 5
, but there also is a retraction between
A
and
A\oplus A
.

Appendix A
Rule commutation is the core of cut-elimination

In this appendix is given the proof of
13
from
Section 4
.
We work in sequent calculus, hence by proof we mean a sequent calculus proof, never considering proof-nets.
We assume proofs to be axiom-expanded.

Our proof uses a general result from the theory of abstract rewriting systems.
We use standard notations from this theory: given a relation
\lhd
,
\lhd^{*}
(resp.
\lhd^{+}
, resp.
\lhd^{=}
) is the transitive reflexive (resp. transitive, resp. reflexive) closure of
\lhd
, while
\rhd
is the converse relation – symmetric relations will correspond to symmetric symbols.
We denote by
\cdot
the composition of relations.

{defi}

Let
\sim,\to
be relations on a set
A
such that
\sim
is an equivalence relation.
The relation
\to
is
Church-Rosser modulo
\sim
if
(\to\cup\leftarrow\cup\sim)^{*}\subseteq~\to^{*}\cdot\sim\cdot~\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.59723pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\leftarrow}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{*}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.59723pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\leftarrow}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{*}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.7543pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\leftarrow}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{*}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 9.2192pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\leftarrow}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{*}}}}\leftarrow
(see
Figure 16
).

{defi}

Let
\sim,\to
be relations on a set
A
such that
\sim
is an equivalence relation.
The relation
\to
is
locally confluent modulo
\sim
if
\leftarrow\cdot\to~\subseteq~\to^{*}\cdot\sim\cdot\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.59723pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\leftarrow}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{*}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.59723pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\leftarrow}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{*}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.7543pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\leftarrow}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{*}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 9.2192pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\leftarrow}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{*}}}}\leftarrow
(see
Figure 16
).

{defi}

Let
\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}},\to
be relations on a set
A
such that
\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}
is symmetric.
The relation
\to
is
locally coherent with
\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}
if
\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}\cdot\to~\subseteq~\to^{*}\cdot\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cdot\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.59723pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\leftarrow}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{*}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.59723pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\leftarrow}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{*}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.7543pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\leftarrow}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{*}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 9.2192pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\leftarrow}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{*}}}}\leftarrow
(see
Figure 16
).

We will deduce
13
from a Church-Rosser property, which will be found by applying the following result from Huet:

{lemC}
[
[
Hue80
]
]

Let
\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}},\to
be relations on a set
A
such that
\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}
is symmetric.
If
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cdot\to\cdot\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
is strongly normalizing,
\to
is locally confluent modulo
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
and locally coherent with
\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}
, then
\to
is Church-Rosser modulo
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
.

We denote by
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
a
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step other than a
cut-cut
commutation.
Also note
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
the
cut-cut
commutation (which is a symmetric relation).

We recall
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
(defined in
Section 2.2
) is one rule commutation
of cut-free MALL
,
i.e.
which is not a commutation involving a
cut
-rule nor having above the commuted rules a sub-proof with a
cut
-rule (but it may have a
cut
-rule in its external context); for instance in the
\top-\otimes
commutation creating or deleting a sub-proof
\pi
,
\pi
is cut-free.
8
8

8

Actually, we only need it for the
\top-\otimes
and
\with-\otimes
commutations, but ask it for all commutations to homogenize and simplify some proofs.

As
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
and
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
are symmetric,
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
and
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
are equivalence relations.

We will instantiate
Appendix A
with
\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}~=~(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})
and
\to~=~\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
.
To this aim, we first prove that
(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})^{*}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})^{*}
is strongly normalizing (
Section A.1
), before demonstrating the other hypotheses of the lemma, namely local confluence and local coherence (
Section A.2
).
Finally, we prove that
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
is Church-Rosser modulo
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
using
Appendix A
, leading to a proof of
13
(
Section A.3
).

A.1.
Strong normalization

The goal of this section is proving the strong normalization of
{(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})^{*}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})^{*}}
, namely
87
.
We do so by giving a measure which is preserved by
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
and
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
but decreases during a
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step.
This measure is complex, and will need some intermediate definitions.

{defi}

We define the
weight

w(A)
of a formula
A
by induction:

•

w(X)=w(X^{\perp})=w(1)=w(\bot)=w(\top)=w(0)=1

•

w(A\otimes B)=w(A\invamp B)=w(A\oplus B)=w(A)+w(B)+1

•

w(A\with B)=\max\left(w(A),w(B)\right)+1

We extend this notion to sequents, by defining for a sequent
\vdash\Gamma=~\vdash A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
that
w(\vdash\Gamma)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w(A_{i})
.

Fact 78
.

For any formula
A
,
w(A)
is a positive integer.

{defi}

We define the
weight

w(\pi)
of a proof
\pi
by induction:

•

Lemma 79
.

For a proof
\pi
of a sequent
\vdash\Gamma
,
w(\pi)\geq w(\vdash\Gamma)
.
Furthermore, if
\pi
is cut-free, then
w(\pi)=w(\vdash\Gamma)
.

Proof A.1
.

This can be easily proven by induction on
\pi
.
We give here the most interesting cases, others are easier or similar.

If
\pi=\text{
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces
\ignorespaces

.
Remark that
\pi
is cut-free if and only if
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are.
Then

\displaystyle w(\pi)

\displaystyle=\max(w(\pi_{1}),w(\pi_{2}))+1

\displaystyle\geq\max(w(\vdash A,\Gamma),w(\vdash B,\Gamma))+1\text{ by induction, with equality if $\pi$ is cut-free}

\displaystyle=\max(w(A)+w(\vdash\Gamma),w(B)+w(\vdash\Gamma))+1

\displaystyle=\max(w(A),w(B))+w(\vdash\Gamma)+1

\displaystyle=w(A\with B)+w(\vdash\Gamma)

\displaystyle=w(\vdash A\with B,\Gamma)

If
\pi=\text{

\ignorespaces\lower 17.98798pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 10.99306pt\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 11.0915pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\pi_{1}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash$ $A_{1},\Gamma$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\vskip-1.1648pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to62.35555pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6648pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$\oplus_{1}$}}\vskip-1.1648pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash$ $A_{1}\oplus A_{2},\Gamma$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}

.
Remark that
\pi
is cut-free if and only if
\pi_{1}
is.
Then

\displaystyle w(\pi)

\displaystyle=w(\pi_{1})+w(A_{2})+1

\displaystyle\geq w(\vdash A_{1},\Gamma)+w(A_{2})+1\text{ by induction hypothesis, with equality if $\pi$ is cut-free}

\displaystyle=w(\vdash\Gamma)+w(A_{1})+w(A_{2})+1

\displaystyle=w(\vdash\Gamma)+w(A_{1}\oplus A_{2})

\displaystyle=w(\vdash A_{1}\oplus A_{2},\Gamma)

Remark 80
.

There exists a proof
\pi
using a
cut
-rule but whose weight is the one of its sequent.
In the following proof, the left sub-tree of the
\with
-rule has weight
5
while its right sub-tree has weight
3
, whence the weight of the full proof is
6=w(\vdash(1\oplus(1\oplus 1))\with 1)
.

1

\vdash 1

\oplus_{1}

\vdash

1\oplus(1\oplus 1)

1

\vdash 1

\bot

\vdash

1,\bot

1

\vdash 1

cut

\vdash

1

\with

\vdash

(1\oplus(1\oplus 1))\with 1

This is due to the
\max
when weighting a
\with
-rule, which may “hide” some weight.

Remark 81
.

As a corollary of
79
,
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\eta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
preserves the weight of a proof, for it transforms a cut-free proof into another one, and weight is defined inductively.

{defi}

A
block

\mathfrak{B}
of
cut
-rules in a proof
\pi
is a maximal set of consecutive
cut
-rules in
\pi
.

We call
measure

|\mathfrak{B}|
of a block
\mathfrak{B}
of
cut
-rules in a proof
\pi
the weight of its root
cut
-rule,
i.e.

|\mathfrak{B}|=\sum_{i}w(\pi_{i})
where the
\pi_{i}
are the sub-proofs whose conclusions are the premises of the
cut
-rules of
|\mathfrak{B}|
, premises which are by definition not the conclusion of a
cut
-rule.

The
measure

|c|
of a
cut
-rule
c
in a proof
\pi
is the measure of the (unique) block it belongs to.

The
measure

|\pi|
of a proof
\pi
is the multiset of the measures of its
cut
-rules.

For example, consider the following proof
\pi
:

ax

\vdash

X
{}^{\perp},
X

ax

\vdash

X
{}^{\perp},
X

ax

\vdash

X
{}^{\perp},
X

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}cut

\vdash

X^{\perp},X

\oplus_{1}

\vdash

X^{\perp},X\oplus 0

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}cut

\vdash

X^{\perp},X\oplus 0

\top

\vdash

\top,X

ax

\vdash

X
{}^{\perp},
X

\with

\vdash

\top\with X^{\perp},X

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}cut

\vdash

X^{\perp},X

ax

\vdash

X
{}^{\perp},
X

ax

\vdash

X
{}^{\perp},
X

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}cut

\vdash

X^{\perp},X

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}cut

\vdash

X^{\perp},X

It contains two blocks: the blue one
\mathcal{B}_{b}
with one
cut
-rule, and the red one
\mathcal{B}_{r}
with the other four.
The measure of the blue block is the weight of the sub-proof starting from the blue
cut
-rule, namely
|\mathcal{B}_{b}|=4
.
Meanwhile, the measure of the red block is the weight of the whole proof,
|\mathcal{B}_{r}|=15
.
Therefore,
|\pi|=\{4;15;15;15;15\}
.

Remark 82
.

A block
\mathfrak{B}
of
n

cut
-rules in a proof
\pi
has its measure
|\mathfrak{B}|
appearing
n
times in
|\pi|
, once for each of its
cut
-rules.

Lemma 83
.

If
\tau\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\phi
then
|\tau|>|\phi|
.

Proof A.2
.

It suffices to compute the measure before and after each cut-elimination step.
To begin with, let us consider some general arguments.
Blocks “enough below” the rules involved in the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step can be easily handled.
These blocks are those in the external context of the reduction step, and not containing the
cut
-rule involved in the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step (as the bottom rule of a cut-elimination step is always a
cut
-rule).
It suffices to prove the weight of all sub-proofs does not increase when applying a
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step, to obtain that the measures of these blocks do not increase.
Remark that it is particularly important to have a
\max
in the inductive definition of the weight in case of a
\with
-rule; a
\with-cut
commutative reduction may duplicate a sub-proof, but leaves the weight of the proof unchanged.

Symmetrically, for blocks “enough above” the rules of the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step, there is nothing to do, for their sub-proofs remain the sames.
These blocks are those above the rules involved in the reduction step, not having a
cut
-rule directly above one of the rules of the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step.
They may be duplicated or erased, but as their measure is less than the one of the block containing the reduced
cut
-rule, it suffices to prove the measure of this last block decreases to obtain the result.

Hence, we only have to consider the block containing the
cut
-rule, as well as blocks just above the other rules involved in the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step.
That the weight of a proof does not increase during a
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step can be easily checked, as it has an inductive definition.

In all cases, we will call
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}
the block in
\tau
containing the
cut
-rule
c
on which the
\overline{\beta}
step acts, which is composed of
n+1

cut
-rules.

ax
key case.
We have

\tau=

ax

\vdash

A
{}^{\perp},
A

\pi

\vdash

A,\Gamma

cut

\vdash

A,\Gamma

\rho

and

\phi=

\pi

\vdash

A,\Gamma

\rho

.

Call
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\phi}
the block in
\phi
corresponding to the
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}
, which has corresponding
cut
-rules save for
c
the eliminated one (it might be empty, in which case the result holds).
The weight of sub-proofs decreases during the reduction step, for we removed an
ax
-rule.
By the preliminary remarks of our proof, we only have to compare the measures of
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\phi}
.

We have
|\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}|=|\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\phi}|+w(A^{\perp})+w(A)
, as we remove an
ax
-rule.
Therefore, in
\tau
we had
n+1

cut
-rules of weight
|\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\phi}|+w(A^{\perp})+w(A)
corresponding in
\phi
to
n

cut
-rules of weight
|\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\phi}|
.
Whence,
|\tau|>|\phi|
.

\invamp-\otimes
key case.
Here
\tau=

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A,\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

B,\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

A\otimes B,\Gamma,\Delta

\pi_{3}

\vdash

B^{\perp},A^{\perp},\Sigma

\invamp

\vdash

B^{\perp}\invamp A^{\perp},\Sigma

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma

\rho

while

\phi=

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A,\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

B,\Delta

\pi_{3}

\vdash

B^{\perp},A^{\perp},\Sigma

cut

\vdash

A^{\perp},\Delta,\Sigma

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma

\rho

.

Call
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
the (possibly empty) block at the root of
\pi_{i}
, containing
n_{i}

cut
-rules, for
i\in\{1;2;3\}
, and
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\phi}
the block in
\phi
containing the two produced
cut
-rules.
Blocks
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
are merged into the same block
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\phi}
.

We compute
|\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}|=|\mathfrak{B}_{1}|+|\mathfrak{B}_{2}|+|\mathfrak{B}_{3}|+2+\alpha
, with
\alpha
the sum of the weight of the other sub-proofs leading to the block
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}
than the two of roots the eliminated
\invamp
and
\otimes
-rules.
Also
|\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\phi}|=|\mathfrak{B}_{1}|+|\mathfrak{B}_{2}|+|\mathfrak{B}_{3}|+\alpha
(for we remove the
\invamp
and
\otimes
-rules).
Therefore, in
\tau
we had
n+1

cut
-rules of weight
|\mathfrak{B}_{1}|+|\mathfrak{B}_{2}|+|\mathfrak{B}_{3}|+2+\alpha
, and
n_{i}

cut
-rules of weight
|\mathfrak{B}_{i}|
, while in
\phi
we have
n+n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}+2

cut
-rules of weight
|\mathfrak{B}_{1}|+|\mathfrak{B}_{2}|+|\mathfrak{B}_{3}|+\alpha
.
Whence,
|\tau|>|\phi|
.

\with-\oplus_{1}
key case.
Here
\tau=

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{2},\Gamma

\with

\vdash

A_{1}\with A_{2},\Gamma

\pi_{3}

\vdash

A_{2}^{\perp},\Delta

\oplus_{1}

\vdash

A_{2}^{\perp}\oplus A_{1}^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta

\rho

while

\phi=

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{2},\Gamma

\pi_{3}

\vdash

A_{2}^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta

\rho

.

Call
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
the (possibly empty) block at the root of
\pi_{i}
, containing
n_{i}

cut
-rules, for
i\in\{1;2;3\}
, and
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\phi}
the block in
\phi
containing the produced
cut
-rule.
Blocks
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
are merged into the same block
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\phi}
, while
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
is deleted.

We compute
|\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}|=\max(|\mathfrak{B}_{1}|,|\mathfrak{B}_{2}|)+|\mathfrak{B}_{3}|+2+w(A_{1}^{\perp})+\alpha
, with

the sum of the weight of the other sub-proofs leading to the block

than the two of roots the eliminated
\with
and

-rules.
Also

.
Therefore, in
\tau
we had
n+1

cut

\max(|\mathfrak{B}_{1}|,|\mathfrak{B}_{2}|)+|\mathfrak{B}_{3}|+2+w(A_{1}^{\perp})+\alpha
, and

|\mathfrak{B}_{i}|
, while in

we have
n+n_{2}+n_{3}+1

cut
-rules of weight
|\mathfrak{B}_{2}|+|\mathfrak{B}_{3}|+\alpha
.
Whence,
|\tau|>|\phi|
.

\with-\oplus_{2}
key case.
This case is very similar to the
\with-\oplus_{1}
key case.

\bot-1
key case.
Here
\tau=

1

\vdash 1

\vdash

\Gamma

\vdash

and

\vdash

.

Call

, which has corresponding

, as we remove the

.
Therefore, in

and

, corresponding in

.
Whence,

.

\invamp-cut
commutative case.
Here
\tau=

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A,B,C,\Gamma

.

Call
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
the (possibly empty) block at the root of

, containing
n_{1}

cut
-rules,
\mathfrak{B}_{a}
the sub-block in

(and

) containing

and the

-rules directly above its right premise, and

.

and

are mapped to the blocks
\mathfrak{B}_{b}^{\phi}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{a}\cup\mathfrak{B}_{1}
.

The difference between
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
in

and
\mathfrak{B}_{b}^{\phi}
and

is that

(including

) moved from

to
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
.
We have

(provided

is not empty) and

, as the weight of the commuted
\invamp
-rule is included in
|\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}|
but not
|\mathfrak{B}_{a}\cup\mathfrak{B}_{1}|
.
Thence,
|\tau|>|\phi|
.

commutative case.

\vdash

\vdash

\rho

.

Call
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
the (possibly empty) block at the root of
\pi_{i}
, containing
n_{i}

cut
-rules,

,
\mathfrak{B}_{a}
the sub-block in
\phi
(and
\tau
) containing
c
and the
cut
-rules directly above its right premise, and
\mathfrak{B}_{b}^{\phi}
the (possibly empty) block in
\phi
containing the rules corresponding to the ones of
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}\backslash\mathfrak{B}_{a}
.
The blocks
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}
,

,

,

.

As in the previous cases, some

-rules (including at least

) strictly lose measure by going from

to

and

(both copies separately weight less than the original), and the duplicated blocks (in
