) are of weight less than the weight of
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{\tau}
.
So, again, we replace one or several measures by strictly smaller measures, hence
|\tau|>|\phi|
.

\otimes-cut-1
,
\otimes-cut-2
,
\oplus_{i}-cut
and
\bot-cut
commutative cases.
These cases are quite similar to the
\invamp-cut
commutative case.

\top-cut
commutative case.
We have
\tau=

\top

\vdash

A,\top,\Gamma

\pi

\vdash

A^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\vdash

\top,\Gamma,\Delta

\rho

whereas

\phi=

\top

\vdash

\top,\Gamma,\Delta

\rho

.

The weights of sub-proofs do not increase during the reduction step, using
79
.
As we remove at least the
cut
-rule
c
, and the measures of all blocks do not increase, the result follows as in the
ax
key case.

Lemma 84
.

If
\pi\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi^{\prime}
then
|\pi|=|\pi^{\prime}|
.

Proof A.3
.

Because a
cut-cut
commutation does not modify the measure of the block of
cut
-rules it is done in, nor the weight of the sub-proof starting from this block.

Lemma 85
.

If
\pi\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi^{\prime}
, then
|\pi|=|\pi^{\prime}|
.

Proof A.4
.

As rule commutations act below a cut-free proof, it is enough to prove they preserve the weight of the minimal sub-proof containing them.
This is the case by
79
, for two cut-free proofs on a given sequent share the same weight.

Remark 86
.

For
85
to hold, we need that rule commutations are applied only when there is no
cut
-rule above.
Otherwise, a sub-proof created (resp. destructed) by a
\top-\otimes
commutation may increase (resp. decrease) the weight and the measure of the proof.
Similarly, a sub-proof duplicated (resp. superimposed) by a
\with-\otimes
commutation may increase (resp. decrease) the measure of the proof, even if its weight remains unchanged.
Other rule commutations pose no such problem.

Proposition 87
.

The relation
(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})^{*}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})^{*}
is strongly normalizing.
In particular,
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
is strongly normalizing.

Proof A.5
.

By Lemmas
83
,
84
and
85
, a step of
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
decreases the measure of a proof while one of
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
or
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
preserves it.
Hence, a step of
(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})^{*}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})^{*}
strictly decreases the measure of the proof, ensuring termination.

Fact 88
.

As long as there exists a
cut
-rule, a
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
cut-elimination step can be applied.
Thus, cut-free proofs correspond to proofs in normal form for
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
.

Corollary 89
.

Cut-elimination
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}~=~(\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})
is weakly normalizing.

Proof A.6
.

Using
87
, one can reach a normal form for
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
.
This is also a normal form for
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
thanks to
88
.

A.2.
Local confluence and coherence

We prove here the two other conditions needed to apply
Appendix A
, which amount to three lemmas as
\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}~=~(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})
.

In this section we denote graphically some proofs with the following convention.
When writing proofs as in

\rho

r_{2}

x

r_{1}

⋮

\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}

\rho

r_{1}

x

r_{2}

⋮

we abuse notations in the cases where
r_{1}
or
r_{2}
is a
\with
or
\top
-rule.
The meaning is that, if say
r_{1}
is a
\with
-rule, then
r_{2}
is duplicated, and even possibly a whole sub-proof if
r_{2}
is a
\otimes
for instance.
Similarly, if
r_{1}
is a
\top
-rule, then this schema means that on the left hand-side
r_{2}
and
\rho
are not here, and are created by the
\top
-commutation.

We use the following preliminary results, identifying sufficient conditions to apply a rule commutation.

{defi}

A
linear block

\mathfrak{B}
of a proof
\pi
is a set of successive rules of
\pi
, which are not
ax
nor
cut
nor
1
nor
\top
-rules, along with a choice of premises for each
\otimes
-rules, such that if a
\otimes
-rule
r

\vdash A,\Gamma

\vdash B,\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

A\otimes B,\Gamma,\Delta

belongs to
\mathfrak{B}
, and the premise
\vdash A,\Gamma
(resp.
\vdash B,\Delta
) is chosen, then the rule above
\vdash B,\Delta
(resp.
\vdash A,\Gamma
) does not belong to
\mathfrak{B}
.

For example, consider the following proof
\pi_{ex}
.

ax

\vdash

A
{}^{\perp},
A

ax

\vdash

B
{}^{\perp},
B

\otimes

\vdash

A^{\perp}\otimes B,A,B^{\perp}

ax

\vdash

A
{}^{\perp},
A

ax

\vdash

B
{}^{\perp},
B

\otimes

\vdash

A^{\perp}\otimes B,A,B^{\perp}

\oplus_{1}

\vdash

(A^{\perp}\otimes B)\oplus A,A,B^{\perp}

\with

\vdash

(A^{\perp}\otimes B)\with((A^{\perp}\otimes B)\oplus A),A,B^{\perp}

ax

\vdash

B
{}^{\perp},
B

\oplus_{2}

\vdash

A^{\perp}\oplus B,B^{\perp}

\otimes

\vdash

(A^{\perp}\otimes B)\with((A^{\perp}\otimes B)\oplus A),A,B^{\perp}\otimes B^{\perp},A^{\perp}\oplus B

The following
\mathfrak{B}_{ex}
is a linear block (with the kept premise of the
\otimes
-rule having its sequent):

\vdash A^{\perp},A

\vdash B^{\perp},B

\otimes

\vdash

A^{\perp}\otimes B,A,B^{\perp}

\vdash A^{\perp}\otimes B,A,B^{\perp}

\oplus_{1}

\vdash

(A^{\perp}\otimes B)\oplus A,A,B^{\perp}

\with

\vdash

(A^{\perp}\otimes B)\with((A^{\perp}\otimes B)\oplus A),A,B^{\perp}

On the contrary, the following is not, for it keeps both premises of a
\otimes
-rule:

\vdash A^{\perp}\otimes B,A,B^{\perp}

\vdash(A^{\perp}\otimes B)\oplus A,A,B^{\perp}

\with

\vdash

(A^{\perp}\otimes B)\with((A^{\perp}\otimes B)\oplus A),A,B^{\perp}

\vdash B^{\perp},B

\oplus_{2}

\vdash

A^{\perp}\oplus B,B^{\perp}

\otimes

\vdash

(A^{\perp}\otimes B)\with((A^{\perp}\otimes B)\oplus A),A,B^{\perp}\otimes B^{\perp},A^{\perp}\oplus B

{defi}

Given a proof
\pi
of
\vdash\Theta
containing a rule
r
, a
domain
of
r
is a sub-list of
\Theta
defined (or not) depending on the kind of
r
, as the formulas of
\Theta
having for sub-formulas (following occurrences):

ax
-rule:

undefined;

cut
-rule:

undefined;

\otimes
-rule:

if
r
is

\vdash A,\Gamma

\vdash B,\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

A\otimes B,\Gamma,\Delta

then the sub-formulas to consider are either
A\otimes B,\Gamma
or
A\otimes B,\Delta
;

\invamp
-rule:

if
r
is

\vdash A,B,\Gamma

\invamp

\vdash

A\invamp B,\Gamma

then the sub-formula to consider is
A\invamp B
;

1
-rule:

undefined;

\bot
-rule:

if
r
is

\vdash\Gamma

\bot

\vdash

\bot,\Gamma

then the sub-formula to consider is
\bot
;

\with
-rule:

if
r
is

\vdash A,\Gamma

\vdash B,\Gamma

\otimes

\vdash

A\with B,\Gamma

then the sub-formula to consider is
A\with B
;

\oplus_{1}
-rule:

if
r
is

\vdash A,\Gamma

\oplus_{1}

\vdash

A\oplus B,\Gamma

then the sub-formula to consider is
A\oplus B
;

\oplus_{2}
-rule:

if
r
is

\vdash B,\Gamma

\oplus_{2}

\vdash

A\oplus B,\Gamma

then the sub-formula to consider is
A\oplus B
;

\top
-rule:

undefined;

The domain of a linear block
\mathfrak{B}
is the union of the domain of each of its rules, with the constraint that if
r
is a
\otimes
-rule

\vdash A,\Gamma

\vdash B,\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

A\otimes B,\Gamma,\Delta

of
\mathfrak{B}
whose premise
\vdash A,\Gamma
(resp.
\vdash B,\Delta
) is chosen, then the domain of
r
in this definition is
A\otimes B,\Delta
(resp.
A\otimes B,\Gamma
).

Two rules
r
and
s
in sub-proof
\pi
of conclusion
\vdash\Gamma
are
independent
if a domain of
r
is disjoint from a domain of
s
(as sub-lists of
\Gamma
, these domains being defined), with the convention that if
\pi
has several slices, then we ask for the disjointness of the union of domains of
r
(identifying rules in different slices with the same main formula occurrence) and of the union of domains of
s
, and if
r
or
s
is a
\otimes
-rule by choosing the same premise for this
\otimes
-rule in all slices.

A rule
r
and a linear block
\mathfrak{B}
in
\pi
are
independent
if all following conditions hold:

•

\mathfrak{B}
is at the root of
\pi
;

•

in every slice the rule
r
is the rule above a rule of
\mathfrak{B}
(identifying rules in different slices with the same main formula occurrence, with
\otimes
-rules unary following the choice of premises of
\mathfrak{B}
);

•

the (or one of the two if
r
is a
\otimes
-rule, choosing the same premise in every slice) union of domains of
r
across all slices is disjoint from the domain of
\mathfrak{B}
(as sub-lists of
\Gamma
); remark this corresponds to the independence of
r
with every rule of
\mathfrak{B}
.

For instance, on
\pi_{ex}
, the domain of the
\oplus_{1}
-rule is
(A^{\perp}\otimes B)\with((A^{\perp}\otimes B)\oplus A)
, the domains of the leftmost
\otimes
-rule are
(A^{\perp}\otimes B)\with((A^{\perp}\otimes B)\oplus A),A
or
(A^{\perp}\otimes B)\with((A^{\perp}\otimes B)\oplus A),B^{\perp}\otimes B^{\perp}
, the former also being the domain of
\mathfrak{B}_{ex}
.
The middle (upper)
\otimes
-rule is not independent with
\mathfrak{B}_{ex}
whereas the
\oplus_{2}
-rule is independent with the root
\otimes
-rule.

Fact 90
.

Consider two rules
r
and
s
in the two different sub-proofs given by a
cut
-rule.
Then
r
and
s
are independent in the sub-proof of root this
cut
-rule.

Lemma 91
.

Take
r
and
s
are successive non
0
-ary nor
cut
-rules in a proof
\pi
, with
r
above
s
and no
cut
-rules above
r
and
s
(in case
s
is a
\with
-rule, we ask
r
to be above
s
in both premises).
If they are independent, then they commute.
On the other end, if they commute, then they are independent in the sub-proof of root
s
.

Proof A.7
.

One has to check it for every type of rules
r
and
s
can be, among
\otimes
,
\invamp
,
\bot
,
\with
,
\oplus_{1}
and
\oplus_{2}
-rules.
A representative case is
r
is a
\otimes
-rule and
s
a
\with
-rule, other cases being left at the charge of the reader.
By symmetry, say that
r
keeps its right premise (in both slices, by independence with
s
).
Our proof must then be:

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{2},B_{1},\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1},\Gamma,\Delta

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},\Gamma

\pi_{3}

\vdash

A_{2},B_{2},\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta

\with

\vdash

A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1}\with B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta

We recognize a
\otimes-\with
rule commutation.

For the (partial) reciprocal, it suffices to check for every instance of a rule commutation that the rules are independent, as it is the case here for a
\otimes-\with
commutation.

Remark 92
.

The full reciprocal of
91
is false.
The following is a counter-example:

\vdash A,B,C,D,\Gamma

\invamp~r

\vdash A\invamp B,C,D,\Gamma

\invamp~s

\vdash A\invamp B,C\invamp D,\Gamma

\invamp

\vdash

(A\invamp B)\invamp(C\invamp D),\Gamma

Lemma 93
.

(1)

A rule commutation does not modify a domain of any (non-erased) rule.

(2)

A commutative cut-elimination case does not modify a domain of any (non-erased) rule.

(3)

A key cut-elimination case does not modify a domain of any (non-erased) rule.

Proof A.8
.

Each item to check consists in a tedious cases analysis.
We give here a few representative ones.
Remark that, for any transformation of proofs, a domain of a non-erased rule which is not involved in the transformation is unchanged (as our transformation are local, except when erasing rules).

Item 1
.
Consider the following
C_{\otimes}^{\with}
commutation:

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{2},B_{1},\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1},\Gamma,\Delta

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},\Gamma

\pi_{3}

\vdash

A_{2},B_{2},\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta

\with

\vdash

A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1}\with B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta

\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$C_{\otimes}^{\with}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{2},B_{1},\Delta

\pi_{3}

\vdash

A_{2},B_{2},\Delta

\with

\vdash

A_{2},B_{1}\with B_{2},\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1}\with B_{2},\Gamma,\Delta

For the
\with
-rule, its domain is (the formulas of the conclusion sequent whose sub-formulas are)
B_{1}\with B_{2}
in both cases.
For the
\otimes
-rule, its domain when keeping the right premise is
A_{1}\otimes A_{2},\Gamma
in both occurrences on the left, as well as on the right.
Similarly, its domain when keeping the left premise stays
A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1}\with B_{2},\Delta
.

Item 2
.
We take interest in a
\otimes-cut-1
commutative case.

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A,B,\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

C,\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

A,B\otimes C,\Gamma,\Delta

\pi_{3}

\vdash

A^{\perp},\Sigma

cut

\vdash

B\otimes C,\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma

\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A,B,\Gamma

\pi_{3}

\vdash

A^{\perp},\Sigma

cut

\vdash

B,\Gamma,\Sigma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

C,\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

B\otimes C,\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma

The domain of the
\otimes
-rule, when keeping its right premise, stays (the formulas of the conclusion sequent whose sub-formulas are)
B\otimes C,\Gamma
, for
A
is not a sub-formula of the conclusion sequent.
Similarly, when keeping its left premise its domain is still
B\otimes C,\Delta
.

Item 3
.
We study here a
\with-\oplus_{1}
key case:

\pi_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},\Gamma

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{2},\Gamma

\with

\vdash

A_{1}\with A_{2},\Gamma

\pi_{3}

\vdash

A_{2}^{\perp},\Delta

\oplus_{1}

\vdash

A_{2}^{\perp}\oplus A_{1}^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta

\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\beta$}}}{\longrightarrow}}

\pi_{2}

\vdash

A_{2},\Gamma

\pi_{3}

\vdash

A_{2}^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta

The initial remark is enough here: the rules involved in the transformation are erased, so there is nothing to check.

Lemma 94
.

Assume
r
is a rule and
\mathfrak{B}
a linear block, that they are independent and that there is no
cut
-rule above them.
Then
r
commutes successively with all rules of
\mathfrak{B}
:

\tau

r

\mathfrak{B}

\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}

\tau

\mathfrak{B}

r

x

.

Proof A.9
.

By induction on the number of rules of
\mathfrak{B}
.
If it is of null size,
i.e.
with no rules, then there is nothing to prove.
Otherwise, there is a rule
t_{s}\in\mathfrak{B}
below
r
in each slice
s
.
If one of the
t_{s}
is not a
\with
-rule, then we commute it with
r
by
91
.
In the resulting proof,
r
and the block
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
, made of the rules of
\mathfrak{B}
save for
t_{s}
, are independent by
Item 1
of
93
(we keep the same choice for premises of
\otimes
-rules).
We conclude using the induction hypothesis.

Otherwise, all
t_{s}
are
\with
-rules.
Take one of them, it must have two occurrences of
s
above its premises.
Hence, we can again make them commute by
91
, then conclude by induction hypothesis using
Item 1
of
93
.

Lemma 95
.

Let
\pi
be an axiom-expanded cut-free proof of a sequent
\vdash\top_{0},\Gamma
(up to permutation), with
\top_{0}
an occurrence of a
\top
-formula.
Then
\pi\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}

\top

\vdash

\top_{0},\Gamma

, this rule being applied on
\top_{0}
.

Proof A.10
.

We reason by induction on the size of
\pi
(
i.e.
its number of rules).

If
\pi
has a unique rule, then it must be a
\top
one: this is the only
0
-ary rule applicable on a sequent containing
\top
, except for a non-atomic
ax
-rule.
Up to a
\top-\top
commutation, this rule is applied on
\top_{0}
and
\pi
is of the desired shape.

If
\pi
has more than one rule, then consider its last rule
r
, which is not a
0
-ary one nor a
cut
.
By induction hypothesis on the premises of
r
containing
\top_{0}
(there is at least one), up to
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
these premises are
\top
-rules on
\top_{0}
.
A commutation between
r
and these
\top
-rules yields

\top

\vdash

\top_{0},\Gamma

.

Remark 96
.

95
corresponds to the terminality of
\top
in the category corresponding to MALL (see
Section 9.1
).

Lemma 97
.

Let
\pi
,
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
be MALL proofs such that
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{2}
.
Then there exist
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{=}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{2}
.
Diagrammatically:
\pi
\pi_{1}
\pi_{2}
\overline{\beta}

\overline{\beta}

\pi_{1}^{\prime}
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
\overline{\beta}^{*}

\mathrel{\vtop{{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{=}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}
\overline{\beta}^{*}

More precisely, we need a step of
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
exactly when both
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
steps are different commutative cases on the same
cut
-rule.

Proof A.11
.

If the
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}
and
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{2}
steps involve only distinct rules then, taking into account that rules of one may be duplicated or erased by the other step, they commute and we have a proof
\pi^{\prime}
such that
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{2}
, by applying one reduction after the other.

From now on, we assume both steps involve (at least) one common rule.
If both reductions share all of their rules, then the two reductions are the same, so
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}
and we are done (recall
1
for our convention on the
\invamp-\otimes
key case).
Hence, we will assume them not to share all of their rules.
We distinguish cases according to the kinds of the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
steps.

If one step is a key case other than an
ax
one.
Remark that on the three rules of a non-
ax
key case, no other
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step can be applied (only a
cut-cut
commutation could have been applied, but this case does not belong to
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
).
Whenceforth, this case cannot happen as it would lead to the two reductions sharing all of their rules.

If both steps are
ax
key cases.
As the two reductions share one rule, but not all rules, the shared rule must be the
cut
-rule, with as premises two
ax
-rules.
We can check that this critical pair leads to the same resulting proof from both choices of cut-elimination.
Thus
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}
.

If one step is an
ax
key case and the other a commutative case.
By symmetry, assume
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{2}
is the
ax
key case.
For the two reductions share a rule, and the
ax
-rule cannot participate in a commutative step, the shared rule must be the
cut
-rule.
We can still do this
ax
key step after the commutation (maybe twice in case of duplication, or zero time in case of erasure), recovering
\pi_{2}
.
Thus:

•

\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{2}
(
ax
-key case and not a
\with-cut
nor
\top-cut
commutative case)

•

\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{2}
(
ax
-key case and
\with-cut
commutative case)

•

\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}
(
ax
-key case and
\top-cut
commutative case)

If both steps are commutative cases.
Here again, as the two reductions share a rule, it must be the
cut
-rule, because there is at most one
cut
-rule directly below a given rule.
As the reductions do not share both of their rules, in
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}
we sent a rule
r_{1}
from a branch of the
cut
below it, and in
\pi\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{2}
we do similarly on a rule
r_{2}
in the other branch.
This case, more complex than the previous ones, is depicted schematically on
Figure 17
.
We can in
\pi_{1}
commute the
cut
-rule and
r_{2}
– maybe twice in case of a duplication, or zero in case of an erasure – obtaining
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
, and similarly in
\pi_{2}
the
cut
-rule and
r_{1}
, yielding
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
.
The two resulting proofs differ exactly by a commutation of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
(even if both are
\top
-rules, they differ by a
\top-\top
commutation).

Indeed,
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are independent in the sub-proof of
\pi
whose root is the
cut
-rule we consider (
90
).
They are still independent in the sub-proof of
\pi_{1}
(resp.
\pi_{2}
) and then of
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
(resp.
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
) whose root is
r_{1}
(resp.
r_{2}
) by
Item 2
of
93
.
For we apply rule commutation only on cut-free sub-proofs, we first eliminate all
cut
-rules above these two rules, in the same way in both sub-proofs (and in case of duplication, in the same way in all duplicates of the sub-proofs).
This can be done thanks to weak normalization of
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
(
87
).
In the resulting sub-proofs,
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are still independent as their domains do not depend on what happen above them, and cut-elimination is a local procedure (
Item 2
and
Item 3
of
93
).
So, they commute (
91
).

Notice the reasoning above does not apply to the case where
r_{1}
or
r_{2}
is a
\top
-rule.
Still, this case is easily handled using
95
: both sub-proofs are equal to a same
\top
-rule, up to rule commutations.

Thus,
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{2}
if both steps are commutative cases.

\pi_{1}=

\rho_{1}

\rho_{2}

r_{2}

x

cut

x

r_{1}

⋮

\pi=

\rho_{1}

r_{1}

x

\rho_{2}

r_{2}

x

cut

⋮

\pi_{2}=

\rho_{1}

r_{1}

x

\rho_{2}

cut

x

r_{2}

⋮

\pi_{1}^{\prime}=

\rho_{1}

\rho_{2}

cut

x

r_{2}

x

r_{1}

⋮

\pi_{2}^{\prime}=

\rho_{1}

\rho_{2}

cut

x

r_{1}

x

r_{2}

⋮

\rho^{\text{cut-free}}

r_{2}

x

r_{1}

⋮

\rho^{\text{cut-free}}

r_{1}

x

r_{2}

⋮

\overline{\beta}
\overline{\beta}
\overline{\beta}^{*}
\overline{\beta}^{*}
\overline{\beta}^{*}
\overline{\beta}^{*}
\mathrel{\vtop{{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}

Figure 17.
Schematic representation of the last case of the proof of
97

For local coherence with
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
, we will need one intermediate result on symmetry of cut, due to the following remark.

Remark 98
.

The
symmetry of a
cut
-rule
is the identification of the following (sub-)proofs:

\pi_{1}=

\rho_{1}

\vdash

A,\Gamma

\rho_{2}

\vdash

A^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta

and
\pi_{2}=

\rho_{2}

\vdash

A^{\perp},\Delta

\rho_{1}

\vdash

A,\Gamma

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta

This relation is included in
\mathrel{=_{\beta}}
, using a
cut-cut
commutation.
Indeed, define respectively
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
the two following proofs, where
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are both the formula
A
, with indices to follow their occurrences:

ax

\vdash

A_{1}^{\perp},A_{2}

\rho_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},\Gamma

cut

\vdash

A_{2},\Gamma

\rho_{2}

\vdash

A_{2}^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta

and

ax

\vdash

A_{1}^{\perp},A_{2}

\rho_{2}

\vdash

A_{2}^{\perp},\Delta

cut

\vdash

A_{1}^{\perp},\Delta

\rho_{1}

\vdash

A_{1},\Gamma

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta

Then
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{2}
, these
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
steps being
ax
key cases.

The next lemma allows us not to add symmetry of cut in
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
, which would complexify our proofs where there already are a lot of cases.

Lemma 99
.

Let
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
be two proofs equal up to symmetry of
cut
-rules.
Then
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{2}
.

Proof A.12
.

The idea is to eliminate any
cut
-rule in
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
in a symmetric way, and show the resulting proofs are equal up to symmetry of
cut
-rules.
The wished result follows through weak normalization, as two cut-free proofs equal up to symmetry of
cut
-rules are simply equal.

We reason by induction on a sequence
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\rho
, with
\rho
some cut-free proof found by weak normalization (
87
).
If this sequence is empty, then
\pi_{1}
is
cut
-free and so
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}
.
Thus, take
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{\prime}
its first step.
We apply the corresponding step in
\pi_{2}
, on the corresponding
cut
-rule which may be the symmetric version of
c
(for if a
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step can be applied, then the one with switched premises can be applied on the symmetric version).
We obtain a proof
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
with the
cut
-rule still permuted compared to
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
in case of a commutative case (or the
cut
-rule erased when commuting with a
\top
, or permuted and duplicated with a
\with
) and
0
,
1
or
2
symmetric
cut
-rules resulting from a key case.
In all cases,
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
are equal up to symmetry of
cut
-rules, allowing us to conclude by induction hypothesis.

Lemma 100
.

Let
\pi_{1}
,
\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{3}
be proofs such that
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
.
Then, there exist
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{+}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}
.
Diagrammatically:
\pi_{1}
\pi_{2}
\pi_{3}
\mathrel{\vtop{{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}
\overline{\beta}

\pi_{1}^{\prime}
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
\overline{\beta}^{+}

\mathrel{\vtop{{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}
\overline{\beta}^{*}

Proof A.13
.

An easily handled case is when the
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
steps involve only distinct rules; assume for now this is the case.
A first general sub-case is when rules of one step are neither erased nor duplicated by the other.
Then these steps commute and
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{3}
, using the same steps in the other order (because these are local transformations).

Now, consider the case where the two steps still involve distinct rules, but the
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
step duplicates a sub-proof containing the rules of
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}
(which may happen if
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
is a
\with-cut
commutative case).
We apply the corresponding
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step first in
\pi_{1}
, yielding
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}
, and then the
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
step twice, once for each occurrence, to recover
\pi_{3}
: we get
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cdot\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{3}
.

Another general case is when the rules involved in the two steps are distinct, but the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step eliminates a sub-proof containing the rules of the
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
step (this can arise when using a
\with-\oplus_{i}
key case or a
\top-cut
commutative case).
In this case, doing in
\pi_{1}
the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step directly yields
\pi_{3}
:
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
.

Remark that, if these steps use distinct rules, the
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}
step cannot duplicate nor erase the rules involved in
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
.
Indeed, this may happen if the
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
step is a
\with-\otimes
or
\top-\otimes
commutative case, but we assumed that a sub-proof corresponding to a rule commutation is cut-free, and a
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step involves a
cut
-rule by definition.

From now on, we suppose both steps involve at least one common rule, which cannot be a
cut
one, for there is no commutation involving a
cut
-rule in
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
.
In fact, there is exactly one shared rule.
Indeed, the rules that can commute using
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
are one rule on top of another rule, so both cannot be above a
cut
-rule.
We distinguish cases according to the kind of
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
.

If
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
is an
ax
key case.
As an
ax
-rule never commutes, the two steps share no rule, contradiction.

If
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
is a
\invamp-\otimes
key case.
In this case,
\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{3}
are the following proofs:

\rho_{1}

\vdash

A,\Gamma

\rho_{2}

\vdash

B,\Delta

\otimes

\vdash

A\otimes B,\Gamma,\Delta

\rho_{3}

\vdash

B^{\perp},A^{\perp},\Sigma

\invamp

\vdash

B^{\perp}\invamp A^{\perp},\Sigma

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma

\rho_{4}

\rho_{1}

\vdash

A,\Gamma

\rho_{2}

\vdash

B,\Delta

\rho_{3}

\vdash

B^{\perp},A^{\perp},\Sigma

cut

\vdash

A^{\perp},\Delta,\Sigma

cut

\vdash

\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma

\rho_{4}

(up to symmetry of the
cut
-rule, the case where
\pi_{2}
has a
\invamp
-rule on the left and a
\otimes
one on the right being symmetric and solved similarly; and up to the order of the formulas in each sequent).

By our assumption,
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}
was a step pushing down the
\otimes
or
\invamp
-rule, and up some non
cut
-rule
r
.
We can in
\pi_{1}
commute the
cut
-rule up and
r
down (as
r
cannot be the rule of the main connective of the formula on which we cut, nor an
ax
-rule).
This yields a proof
\pi_{1}^{1}
such that
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}
with this commutative step, with
\pi_{1}^{1}
being
\pi_{2}
except
r
is below the
cut
-rule and not above the
\otimes
or
\invamp
-rule (by abuse, for if
r
is a
\top
-rule then the
cut
-rule is not here anymore).
Thus,
\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{2}
using the same step as in
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
; unless if
r
is a
\top
-rule, in which case there is nothing to do and we set
\pi_{1}^{1}=\pi_{1}^{2}
; or if
r
is a
\with
-rule, where we have to apply this step in both occurrences, obtaining
\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{2}
.
In any case,
\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{2}
.
Observe that
\pi_{1}^{2}
is like
\pi_{3}
, except that
r
is above some
cut
-rule(s) in
\pi_{3}
and below in
\pi_{1}^{2}
.
But, using
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
in
\pi_{3}
,
r
can commute down one or two of the
cut
-rules created by the key case, yielding
\pi_{1}^{2}
(including if
r
is a
\with
or
\top
-rule).
Therefore,
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{+}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}
, concluding this case.

If
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
is a
\with-\oplus_{i}
or
\bot-1
key case.
These cases are similar to the previous one, in simpler as we create less
cut
-rules.

If
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
is a commutative case.
As
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
have exactly one rule in common, the
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
step involves the rule
r
that will be commuted down in the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step, and another rule that we call
s
(
r
and
s
are not
cut
-rules).
The proof
\pi_{1}
has from top to bottom
r
,
s
and
cut
,
\pi_{2}
has
s
,
r
and
cut
, and
\pi_{3}
has
s
,
cut
and
r
.
Schematically:

\pi_{1}=\text{ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces

\ignorespaces}\qquad\pi_{3}=\text{ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces
\ignorespaces \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces
\ignorespaces\lower 33.12466pt\hbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\thinspace\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to11.81117pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$s$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-0.57353pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to41.95244pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.57352pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$cut$}}\vskip-0.57352pt\hbox{\hskip 15.07063pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 15.07063pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to11.81117pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$r$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hskip 15.07063pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}
\ignorespaces}

Suppose first that the
cut
-rule commutes with
s
.
Our reasoning for this case is depicted on
Figure 18
.
In
\pi_{1}
, we commute
s-cut
then
r-cut
, yielding
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{2}
(the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
being of length one, except if
s
is a
\with
-rule, in which case we apply the
r-cut
commutation for both occurrences, or if
s
is a
\top
-rule, in which case there is no commutation to apply).
The proof
\pi_{1}^{2}
has from top to bottom
cut
,
r
and
s
.
Meanwhile, in
\pi_{3}
we commute
s-cut
(twice if
r
is a
\with
-rule, or zero time if it is a
\top
-rule), yielding
\pi_{3}^{1}
having from top to bottom
cut
,
s
and
r
.
Now, both
\pi_{1}^{2}
and
\pi_{3}^{1}
have above
r
and
s
a same proof (maybe duplicated or erased).
We use weak normalization of
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
(
87
) to eliminate all
cut
-rules in this sub-proof, in the same way for all its occurrences in
\pi_{1}^{2}
and
\pi_{3}^{1}
, obtaining proofs
\pi_{1}^{3}
and
\pi_{3}^{2}
equal up to the commutation of
r
and
s
(the very same one that was used in
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}
).
We thus obtain
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{3}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{3}^{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}
.

To check that indeed we can apply a
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
step, by
91

r
and
s
are independent in the sub-proof of root
s
of
\pi_{1}
, so also of root the
cut
-rule (as their domains on the premise sequent of the
cut
-rule are their domains in the conclusion sequent, for none use the cut formula).
Using commutative cut-elimination cases does not change this, nor does then eliminating all
cut
-rules above them (
Item 2
and
Item 3
of
93
).
Thus,
r
and
s
commute in
\pi_{1}^{3}
(
91
).
Notice this reasoning does not apply to the case where
r
or
s
is a
\top
-rule.
Still, this case is easily handled using
95
: both sub-proofs are equal to a same
\top
-rule, up to rule commutations.

Assume now
s
is a rule associated to the main connective of the formula on which we cut.
Remark
s
cannot be an
ax
-rule, for it commutes with
r
.
This case is represented on
Figure 19
.
We first reduce in the same way all
cut
-rules in the branch of the
cut
-rule not containing
s
, yielding
\pi_{1}^{1}
from
\pi_{1}
through
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}
and
\pi_{3}^{1}
from
\pi_{3}
through
\pi_{3}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}^{1}
; they share this sub-proof, and we use weak normalization of
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
(
87
).
Denote by
s^{\perp}
the rule introducing the dual formula of
s
(
i.e.
the other formula on which we cut), and by
\rho
the rules between
s^{\perp}
and the
cut
-rule in
\pi_{1}^{1}
(which are also those in
\pi_{3}^{1}
).
Commuting the
cut
-rule above all rules in
\rho
yields
\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{2}
with
\pi_{1}^{2}
having the
cut
-rule between
s
and
s^{\perp}
.
(Remark we do not need
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
steps, as we eliminated the
cut
-rules above.)
Doing the same commutations in
\pi_{3}^{1}
gives
\pi_{3}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}^{2}
, with
\pi_{3}^{2}
differing from
\pi_{1}^{2}
by having
r
below
\rho
and not above
s
.
Using the appropriate key case or
\top-cut
commutative case to eliminate the
cut
-rule in
\pi_{1}^{2}
, using
s^{\perp}
and maybe
s
, we obtain a new proof
\pi_{1}^{3}
(as usual, if there are
\with
-rules in
\rho
, we need to do so for all duplicates).
In this new proof, if
cut
-rules have been introduced by the key case we used, we commute them with the rule
r
(which cannot introduce the formula of the
cut
-rule, for this is a sub-formula of the
s
rule, which commutes with the
r
rule).
The produced proof is called
\pi_{1}^{4}
, and we have
\pi_{1}^{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{+}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{3}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{4}
.
On the other hand, we also eliminate the
cut
-rule in the same way in
\pi_{3}^{2}
, yielding a proof
\pi_{3}^{3}
such that
\pi_{3}^{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}^{3}
(again, in all duplicates if there were
\with
-rules in
\rho
, or with nothing to do if
r
is a
\top
-rule).
Remark
\pi_{3}^{3}
is
\pi_{1}^{4}
, except the rule
r
is below the rules of
\rho
in
\pi_{3}^{3}
and above in
\pi_{1}^{4}
.
Now we use again weak normalization of
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
(
87
) to eliminates all
cut
-rules above
r
or the rules in
\rho
, in the same way in
\pi_{1}^{4}
and
\pi_{3}^{3}
.
We obtain two proofs equal to the commutation of
r
with all rules in
\rho
, so equal up to
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
now that there is no

-rule above (including a
\top-\top
commutation if both

and

are
\top
-rules).
If

or

is a
\top
-rule, then

and we commute

with

in this proof, “producing” the rule

that we can then commute down with rules of

to recover

.

Indeed, in
\pi_{1}^{1}
the rule

is independent of every rule of
\rho
(
90
),
\rho
being a linear block by definition (it consists of rules commuting with a given
cut
-rule, we keep for premises of
\otimes
-rules the one leading to the

rules).
They are still independent in
\pi_{1}^{2}
,
\pi_{1}^{3}
,
\pi_{1}^{4}
and the resulting proof with cut above eliminated (
Item 2
and
Item 3
of
93
).
But then we remark
r
independent with the linear block
\rho
, so it can indeed commute down (
94
).
Notice this reasoning does not apply to the case where
r
is a
\top
-rule.
Still, this case is easily handled using
95
: both sub-proofs are equal to a same
\top
-rule, up to rule commutations.

Finally,
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{+}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{3}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{4}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}^{3}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}^{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}
.

Lemma 101
.

Let
\pi_{1}
,
\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{3}
be proofs such that
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
.
Then there exist
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{+}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}
.
Diagrammatically:
\pi_{1}
\pi_{2}
\pi_{3}
\mathrel{\vtop{{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}
\overline{\beta}

\pi_{1}^{\prime}
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
\overline{\beta}^{+}

\mathrel{\vtop{{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}
\overline{\beta}^{*}

Proof A.14
.

Call
c
the (unique)
cut
-rule of
\pi_{2}
involved in
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
, and
c_{d}
and
c_{u}
the ones of
\pi_{2}
from top to bottom involved in
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}
(so that in
\pi_{1}
we have
c_{u}
above
c_{d}
).

If
c\notin\{c_{u};c_{d}\}
, then there exists
\rho
such that
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\rho\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{3}
.
Indeed, the commutation
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{2}
involves no rule of the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step, meaning we can do the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step first.
If this
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step does not erase nor duplicate a sub-proof containing
c_{u}
and
c_{d}
, then we have the result by first doing the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step, then the
cut
-
cut
commutation:
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{3}
.
If the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step erases
c_{u}
and
c_{d}
, then
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
.
Finally, if
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
duplicates the rules
c_{u}
and
c_{d}
, then we apply the
cut-cut
commutation on each copy to recover
\pi_{3}
, yielding
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cdot\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{3}
.
Thus, if
c\notin\{c_{u};c_{d}\}
then
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\rho\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{3}
.

Whence, we now assume
c\in\{c_{u};c_{d}\}
.
Call
\mathcal{R}
the set of the non-
cut
-rules involved in
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
(
i.e.
those other than
c
).
Also call
c^{\prime}
the
cut
-rule in
\{c_{u};c_{d}\}
other than
c
.
Looking at the possible cases for
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
, we have
\mathcal{R}
of cardinal
1
or
2
.
If all rules of
\mathcal{R}
are just above
c
in
\pi_{1}
then, as it is also the case in
\pi_{2}
,
\mathcal{R}
must be a singleton
\{r\}
(because
c^{\prime}
must be just above
c
in
\pi_{1}
or in
\pi_{2}
), and so
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
is a commutative step or an
ax
key case.
Assume
r
is an
ax
-rule; we have two sub-cases, according to
(c,c^{\prime})=(c_{u},c_{d})
or
(c^{\prime},c)=(c_{u},c_{d})
.
If
(c,c^{\prime})=(c_{u},c_{d})
, then we have (up to symmetry of
cut
-rules):

•

\pi_{1}=

r

\vdash A^{\perp},A

\vdash

\vdash A,B^{\perp},\Gamma

\vdash\Gamma,\Delta

•

\pi_{2}=

r

\vdash A^{\perp},A

\rho_{1}

\vdash

A,B^{\perp},\Gamma

\rho_{2}

\vdash

B,\Delta

c^{\prime}

\vdash A,\Gamma,\Delta

c

\vdash\Gamma,\Delta

•

\pi_{3}=

\rho_{1}

\vdash

A,B^{\perp},\Gamma

\rho_{2}

\vdash

B,\Delta

c^{\prime}

\vdash A,\Gamma,\Delta

We remark
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
.
Similarly, if
(c^{\prime},c)=(c_{u},c_{d})
then
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
.

Suppose now that
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
is a commutative step.
Remark that
r
above
c^{\prime}
is a commutative cut elimination case, for we have
c^{\prime}
not below
r
in either
\pi_{1}
or
\pi_{2}
.
We also have two sub-cases here, according to
(c,c^{\prime})=(c_{u},c_{d})
or
(c^{\prime},c)=(c_{u},c_{d})
.

In the first sub-case,
(c,c^{\prime})=(c_{u},c_{d})
, we have schematically:

\pi_{1}=\text{ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces

(with in
\pi_{3}
rules above

erased if

is a
\top

is a

We apply in
\pi_{1}
the commutative cut elimination case between

and

, yielding

\pi_{1}^{1}=

c

x

r

x

c^{\prime}

x

, then the same one between

and

to get

\pi_{1}^{2}=

x

c^{\prime}

x

r

and finally one
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
step (or zero if

, or two if

), obtaining
\pi_{3}
.
Thence,
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{2}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{3}
.

In the second sub-case,
(c^{\prime},c)=(c_{u},c_{d})
, we have:

\pi_{1}=\text{ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces
\ignorespaces \ignorespaces\ignorespaces

\ignorespaces \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 32.44115pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\thinspace\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to11.81117pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$r$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to42.0543pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$c$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hskip 15.12157pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-1.25705pt\hbox{\hskip 15.12157pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to56.71407pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 3.25703pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$c^{\prime}$}}\vskip-1.25703pt\hbox{\hskip 37.57301pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}

\ignorespaces \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 32.44115pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\thinspace\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to11.81117pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$c$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to11.81117pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$r$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-1.25705pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to42.0543pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 3.25703pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$c^{\prime}$}}\vskip-1.25703pt\hbox{\hskip 15.12157pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}
\ignorespaces}

Similarly to the previous sub-case,

\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}

\pi_{1}^{1}=

c^{\prime}

x

c

x

r

and

\pi_{3}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}

x

x

r

.
Then,
\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{3}^{1}
by commuting
c
and
c^{\prime}
(using one
cut-cut
commutation, or zero if
r=\top
, or two if
r=\with
).
Thus,
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\pi_{3}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}
.
This solves the case where all rules of
\mathcal{R}
are above
c
in
\pi_{1}
.

We suppose now that a rule
r\in\mathcal{R}
is above
c^{\prime}
in
\pi_{1}
, and then above
c
in
\pi_{2}
, thus
c^{\prime}=c_{u}
and
c=c_{d}
.
In this case, the possible other rule in
\mathcal{R}
cannot be above
c^{\prime}
in
\pi_{1}
, for only one sub-proof may be shared between the two
cut
-rules.
Schematically:

\pi_{1}=\text{ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces
\ignorespaces \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 32.44115pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\thinspace\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to11.81117pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$r$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-1.25705pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to42.0543pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 3.25703pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$c^{\prime}$}}\vskip-1.25703pt\hbox{\hskip 15.12157pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 15.12157pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to59.51962pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$c$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hskip 38.9758pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}
\ignorespaces}\qquad\pi_{2}=\text{ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces
\ignorespaces \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 32.44115pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\thinspace\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to11.81117pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$r$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to42.0543pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$c$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hskip 15.12157pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-1.25705pt\hbox{\hskip 15.12157pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to56.71407pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 3.25703pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$c^{\prime}$}}\vskip-1.25703pt\hbox{\hskip 37.57301pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}
\ignorespaces}\qquad\pi_{3}=\text{ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces
\ignorespaces \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 19.11983pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\thinspace\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-0.62312pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to11.81117pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 0.67868pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$\gamma$}}\vskip-0.62312pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-1.25705pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to42.44225pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 3.25703pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$c^{\prime}$}}\vskip-1.25703pt\hbox{\hskip 15.31554pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize\phantom{x}}}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}
\ignorespaces}

with
\gamma
the rules resulting of applying the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step, which may be a key case.

If
r
and
c^{\prime}
commute (with a commutative
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step), then we apply this commutation in
\pi_{1}
, yielding

\pi_{1}^{1}=

c^{\prime}

x

r

x

c

x

.
Then we apply the
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step with the rules corresponding to the ones of
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
, yielding

\pi_{1}^{2}=

c^{\prime}

x

\gamma

x

.
At this point, either this step was a commutative step with
r
, meaning
\gamma
is composed of
c
above
r
, and we can in
\pi_{3}
commute
c^{\prime}
and
r
with a
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
step, then
c^{\prime}
and
c
with one
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
, in order to recover
\pi_{1}^{2}
(or no
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
needed if
r=\top
, or two if
r=\with
).
Otherwise, this reduction step was a key case, producing
\gamma
which consists in
0
,
1
or
2

cut
-rules.
We commute them with
c^{\prime}
so as to obtain
\pi_{3}
.
Therefore, if
r
and
c^{\prime}
commute then
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{2}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{=}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}
.

Suppose now that
r
and
c^{\prime}
do not commute.
This means
r
is a rule corresponding to the main connective of the formula
A
on which
c^{\prime}
cuts, a
\top
-rule or an
ax
-rule on
A
.
If it is an
ax
-rule, then
r
and
c
, as well as
r
and
c^{\prime}
, make an
ax
key case.
One can check that applying this key case with
c^{\prime}
in
\pi_{1}
yields
\pi_{3}
(in this case
\gamma
is empty), maybe up to symmetry of the
cut
-rule.
Thus
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
or, using
99
,
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}
.

Assume now
r
is not an
ax
-rule, so it is a rule corresponding to the main connective of the formula
A
on which
c^{\prime}
cuts, or a
\top
-rule.
Thus
\pi_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}
is not a key step, because
r
cannot also correspond to the main connective of the formula on which
c
cuts (only the
ax
-rule has two principal formulas).
Hence in the schema of
\pi_{3}
,
\gamma
consists of
c
followed below by
r
.
A schematic representation of the reasoning we will do is depicted on
Figure 20
.

Call
\rho
the sub-proof of
\pi_{1}
above
c^{\prime}
in the branch not leading to
r
.
This proof
\rho
is also the sub-proof of
\pi_{3}
above
c^{\prime}
in the same branch (
c
does not belong to this branch as it commuted with
r
).
We reduce all
cut
-rules in
\rho
using only
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
steps (thanks to
87
), in the same way in both
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{3}
, obtaining a cut-free proof
\rho^{\prime}
, which is a sub-proof of the resulting
\pi_{1}^{1}
and
\pi_{3}^{1}
respectively.
In particular, we have
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}
and
\pi_{3}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}^{1}
.

Call
r^{\perp}
the rule in
\rho^{\prime}
introducing the formula
A^{\perp}
(which is a rule of the main connective of
A^{\perp}
or an
ax
or
\top
-rule), and
\tau
the sequence of rules in
\rho^{\prime}
between
c^{\prime}
and
r^{\perp}
.
Remark that rules in
\tau
cannot be
\top
or
cut
-rules.
We commute in
\pi_{1}^{1}
(resp.
\pi_{3}^{1}
) the rules of
\tau
with
c^{\prime}
, yielding
\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{2}
(resp.
\pi_{3}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}^{2}
) as
\tau
is cut-free.
In the two proofs obtained,
c^{\prime}
has on its premises the rules
r
and
r^{\perp}
(or all duplicated
c^{\prime}
, as
\tau
may contain
\with
-rules).

We now apply a key or
\top-cut
commutative case on
c^{\prime}
, using
r
and
r^{\perp}
(or just
r^{\perp}
if it is an
ax
or
\top
-rule), in all slices as there can be
\with
-rules in
\tau
, yielding
\pi_{1}^{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{+}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{3}
and
\pi_{3}^{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{+}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{3}^{3}
, producing rules
\gamma^{\prime}
(in each slice), which are
0
,
1
or
2

cut
-rules, or the
\top
-rule
r^{\perp}
.
Observe that
\pi_{1}^{3}
and
\pi_{3}^{3}
differ only by the fact that in
\pi_{1}^{3}
the
cut
-rule
c
is below
\gamma^{\prime}
and
\tau
, while it is above (or erased in case of a
\top-cut
commutative case) in
\pi_{3}^{3}
(in all slices).
We can commute
c
up in
\pi_{1}^{3}
until going above
\tau
, using
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
steps, obtaining
\pi_{1}^{4}
; this is because rules in
\tau
cannot introduce the formula on which
c
cuts, looking at
\pi_{3}^{3}
.
Then we commute
c
with
\gamma^{\prime}
to recover
\pi_{3}^{3}
, using
0
,
1
or
2

\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
steps or a
\top-cut
commutation
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
, in all slices as they can be
\with
-rules in
\tau
.
Whence
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{+}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{3}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}^{4}(\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{+}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})\pi_{3}^{3}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{+}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}^{2}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}^{1}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}^{*}$}}}{\longleftarrow}}\pi_{3}

Church-Rosser modulo

We can now prove the wished result.

Theorem 102
.

In the sequent calculus of MALL,
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
is Church-Rosser modulo
(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})^{*}
.

Proof A.15
.

We apply
Appendix A
:
(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})^{*}\cdot\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}\cdot(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})^{*}
is strongly-normalizing by

,

Proof A.16
.

By
102
the normal forms, related by
\mathrel{=_{\beta}}
, are related by
(\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}\cup\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}})^{*}
, for no reduction
\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$\overline{\beta}$}}}{\longrightarrow}}
can be applied to them.

\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr{$\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}$}\crcr{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{\ast}}}{{\hphantom{\mathrel{\lower 0.94722pt\hbox{$\vdash\!\dashv$}}}\vphantom{\to}}}$}\crcr}}}
